**What is in Whisper's silence: constant filler or working memory** (speech; wildness 4)  
Claim: Whisper pads every clip to 30 seconds and the encoder processes the padding. Either the encoder states over the padded region are almost the same for every clip, in which case they can be stored once and reused and the encoder only needs to run on the speech; or they differ by clip, in which case the model is using the padding as storage and the decoder reads it. One of these is true and each one is useful.  
Prediction: For 5-second clips with whisper-small and large-v3: pad states more than 1 second past the end of speech have cosine similarity above 0.98 across different clips. Running the encoder on speech plus a 1-second margin (about 300 of 1500 frames) and appending stored pad states gives word error rate …  
Control that could embarrass it: Pad with low-level noise instead of digital zero and repeat. If the stored-state arm only works for exact zeros, it is a property of the padding value and breaks on real audio with trailing room noise. Also run clips where speech ends at 25 s, where the …  
Kill criterion: If pad-state similarity is between 0.9 and 0.98 and both the splice arm and the swap arm change error by 0.3 to 1 absolute, the result is mushy and neither story holds; drop it. Also dead if someone has already shipped this: whisper.cpp exposes a shorter …  
Cost: One to two days on the Mac Studio. Under 8 GB memory. Downloads: LibriSpeech test-clean (about 350 MB) and model weights up to 3 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [Ellena Reid, Whisper interpretability blog](https://er537.github.io/blog/2023/09/05/whisper_interpretability.html), 2023-09-05 (read); [Beyond Transcription: Mechanistic Interpretability in ASR, arXiv 2508.15882](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15882), 2025-08 (not read directly); whisper.cpp audio_ctx option (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch on Whisper padding and encoder states; gh searches in ggml-org/whisper.cpp and openai/whisper for audio_ctx and padding (nothing useful returned); HF paper search and arXiv listing search both returned 429. Fine-tuned …

**Say it twice: clause order and a free repair for image models that read prompts left to right** (image generation; wildness 3)  
Claim: Image models that use a left-to-right language model as the text encoder bind a describing word to its noun less well when the describing word comes after the noun, because the noun's state was computed before the model saw it. Writing the prompt twice and conditioning only on the states from the second copy should repair most of this with no training.  
Prediction: On 200 two-object prompts written both ways ('a red cube and a blue ball' versus 'a cube that is red and a ball that is blue'), attribute binding accuracy scored by a vision-language model is at least 10 points lower for the noun-first form on left-to-right encoder models, and under 3 points …  
Control that could embarrass it: Repeat a different, unrelated sentence before the prompt. If that helps as much as repeating the prompt, the gain comes from longer context or shifted positions and not from seeing the future words. Also check that the scoring model itself is not biased by …  
Kill criterion: If the order gap on left-to-right encoder models is under 4 points, or within 1.5 times the gap on the T5 model, the bias is too small to matter in current models and the repair is moot.  
Cost: About 6,400 images at 512 px. On the M1 Max that is one to three days depending on the model; fewer seeds would halve it. Scoring is a few hours. No new downloads if the lab models are present.  
Nearest work listed: [Ma et al., LLM text encoders for diffusion prompt encoding, arXiv 2406.11831](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.11831), 2024-06 (not read directly); [Wang et al., arXiv 2506.08210](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08210), 2025 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch on causal text encoders and word order in text-to-image models; HF paper search for 'echo' and 'repeat prompt' with diffusion returned nothing on point before the 429s. The bias itself is known. I did not find the …

**Text gets outvoted: prompt strength falls as the image gets bigger in shared-attention models** (image and video generation; wildness 3)  
Claim: In image models where text tokens and image tokens share one attention, the number of image tokens grows with resolution and the number of text tokens does not. Unless the model learned to compensate, the share of attention going to text falls, and prompt adherence should fall with it. Adding a constant to the attention scores of text keys, equal to the log of the token-count ratio, should restore it.  
Prediction: On a shared-attention model, mean attention mass on text tokens from image queries falls by at least a factor of 3 between 512 px and 1536 px, and compositional prompt accuracy falls by at least 5 points. Adding log(N/N_train) to text-key scores recovers at least half of the lost accuracy. A …  
Control that could embarrass it: Models trained with mixed resolutions may already have learned the compensation; if measured text mass is flat across resolution, the premise is wrong for that model. Second control: large images also have more places for objects to go wrong, so score the …  
Kill criterion: If text attention mass changes by less than 1.5 times across a ninefold change in image tokens, the models already compensate and the idea is dead. If adherence drops equally on the cross-attention control, the drop is not about dilution.  
Cost: Two to three days on the Mac Studio, mostly the high-resolution runs. Peak memory near the 32 GB limit at 1536 px. No downloads beyond models the lab already holds.  
Nearest work listed: Resolution-dependent timestep shift in rectified-flow models (SD3 paper) (not read directly); Round-one idea 'Cache along the condition axis' (read)  
Author's note on the search: Not completed. HF paper search and the arXiv listing search for joint attention, text dilution and resolution all returned HTTP 429, and the WebSearch budget was spent. Treat novelty as unchecked. High-resolution prompt drift is widely …

**Repeating data helps when the answer is certain and hurts when it is not** (training / data; wildness 3)  
Claim: Whether repeating training examples beats fresh data at a fixed number of steps depends on how predictable the target is given the input. For near-deterministic targets (arithmetic, code that must compile) repetition helps. For high-entropy targets (prose) it hurts. There is a crossover that can be located.  
Prediction: On a synthetic language where each next token is drawn from a rule with adjustable randomness, at a fixed step budget: with conditional entropy under 0.2 bits per token, a two-set mix (a small subset repeated, the rest fresh) beats all-fresh by at least 5% lower held-out loss above the entropy …  
Control that could embarrass it: Match the tasks on difficulty, not only on entropy. If a hard deterministic task and an easy deterministic task behave differently, the variable is something like 'how much optimisation is left to do', not entropy. Run one hard low-entropy task and one easy …  
Kill criterion: If the sign of the repetition effect does not change across the entropy dial (same sign at all 6 settings beyond seed noise), the hypothesis is wrong.  
Cost: 54 small runs of 10 to 20 minutes each on the M1 Max: about one day. Under 4 GB memory. No downloads beyond a small prose corpus.  
Nearest work listed: [Charton and Kempe, Emergent properties with repeated examples, arXiv 2410.07041](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.07041), 2024-10-09 (read); [Hernandez et al., Scaling Laws and Interpretability of Learning from Repeated Data, arXiv 2205.10487](https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.10487), 2022-05 (read)  
Author's note on the search: HF paper search on repeated examples (found the two papers above); arXiv listing search for follow-ups returned 429. I did not check papers that cite Charton and Kempe, so a follow-up testing this exact variable may exist.

**A head start in loss is mostly an illusion** (language / training; wildness 4)  
Claim: If you start a language model from closed-form statistics so that its loss at step zero equals what a normal run reaches after some number of steps, it will not finish that many steps earlier. Most of the head start is lost, because reaching a loss level by a shortcut does not build the internal features the normal path builds on the way there.  
Prediction: Define the head start as the step at which a baseline run first reaches the initialised model's starting loss (say step 80 of 1,200). The initialised run reaches the final target loss fewer than 40 steps before the baseline, so it keeps under 50% of its head start. A run restarted from the …  
Control that could embarrass it: The initialised run may simply need a different learning-rate warmup. Sweep three warmup lengths for the initialised arm before concluding. If a retuned schedule keeps over 80% of the head start, the loss was a fair measure of progress and the idea is wrong.  
Kill criterion: If the count-initialised run, with the best of three warmups, keeps 80% or more of its head start across 5 seeds, drop the idea.  
Cost: About 25 short GPU runs, 2 to 4 GPU-hours on a single rented card. CPU count tables: under an hour. No new downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [modded-nanogpt PR #375](https://github.com/KellerJordan/modded-nanogpt/pull/375), 2026-09-29 (read); Scaling Smart (arXiv 2409.12903) and A Little Help Goes a Long Way (arXiv 2410.18779) (not read directly); Bigram Subnetworks, arXiv 2504.15471 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: HF paper search on n-gram and small-model initialisation (three hits listed above, none opened). arXiv API was rate limited. I did not check whether anyone has measured head-start retention specifically. The 'early gains wash out' pattern …

### Lens: build-something-new

**Tell the model how wrong it just was: prediction error as an input to the next position** (text language model; wildness 3)  
Claim: A language model never sees its own mistakes on the document it is reading; it has to infer them. If an early layer makes a prediction and the error of that prediction at position t-1 is fed in as an input at position t, the upper layers can adapt to the document in one forward pass. This should recover part of what test-time gradient steps give, at a few percent extra cost.  
Prediction: On the modded-nanogpt optimisation track setup (fixed data, steps to 3.28 validation loss) at matched FLOPs: 4 to 8 percent fewer steps. On a Mac-scale 6-layer, d=384 GPT on FineWeb or TinyStories with equal wall-clock: 0.02 to 0.04 nats lower validation loss, with the gain concentrated on tokens …  
Control that could embarrass it: The auxiliary-loss-only arm (deep supervision without feeding the error back) and an arm that feeds a shuffled error from another position. If either matches the full method, the gain is from the extra loss or extra parameters and not from the error signal. …  
Kill criterion: Stage 2 gain under 0.01 nats over the auxiliary-loss-only arm across 5 seeds, or no extra gain at late document positions compared with early ones.  
Cost: Stage 1: 2 hours CPU or MPS. Stage 2: 4 arms x 5 seeds x about 1.5 hours on the M1 Max, about 30 hours, under 16 GB. Stage 3: roughly 10 to 20 free-tier GPU hours. Download: FineWeb tokens, a few GB.  
Nearest work listed: [modded-nanogpt PR #205, test-time training (samacqua)](https://github.com/KellerJordan/modded-nanogpt/pull/205), 2026-01-23 (read); [modded-nanogpt PR #378, document-local copy feature](https://github.com/KellerJordan/modded-nanogpt/pull/378), 2026-10-02 (read); [Transformer Copilot, arXiv 2505.16270](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16270) (not read directly); Feedback Transformer (Fan et al. 2020) and self-conditioning in diffusion (Chen et al. 2022) (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch on prediction error as input to an autoregressive transformer; arXiv API queries on self-conditioning plus autoregressive language model plus previous prediction (zero hits) and on error feedback. Read the modded-nanogpt …

**Per-band Wiener preconditioning for diffusion denoisers** (image diffusion (pixel space first); wildness 2)  
Claim: Standard diffusion preconditioning assumes the data has one variance. Images have a steep power spectrum, so the best linear denoiser is different in each frequency band and is known in closed form from the data spectrum. Supplying that filter for free and training the network only on what is left should reach a given quality in fewer steps.  
Prediction: Small pixel-space model (about 30M parameters) on ImageNet 64x64 or FFHQ 64x64: reaches the baseline's final FID in 25 to 40 percent fewer training steps, and 5 to 10 percent better FID at equal steps. In an SD-VAE latent space the gain is under 5 percent because latents are closer to white.  
Control that could embarrass it: Scalar EDM with the loss weighting retuned per noise level, and scalar EDM with a zero-initialised learned linear layer on the skip path. If a learned linear skip reaches the same result in the first few thousand steps, the network learns the linear part …  
Kill criterion: Under 10 percent step saving to matched FID on pixel-space CIFAR-10 and ImageNet 64 across 3 seeds, or the learned-linear-skip control matches it within noise.  
Cost: CIFAR-10: 4 arms x 3 seeds x about 3 hours on M1 Max, about 36 hours. ImageNet 64 subset: about 4 days of Mac time or 20 to 30 free GPU hours. Under 20 GB memory. Download: CIFAR-10 170 MB, …  
Nearest work listed: [Spectral Forcing for Pixel-Space Diffusion, arXiv 2606.15236](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15236), 2026-06-16 (read); [Locality in Image Diffusion Models, arXiv 2509.09672](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09672) (not read directly); EDM, Karras et al. 2022, and EDM2 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch for Wiener filter plus EDM preconditioning per frequency; arXiv API queries on spectral diffusion preconditioning. Found Spectral Forcing and the locality paper, nothing combining per-band Wiener skip with per-band input and …

**Soft targets from codec jitter for speech token language models** (speech (text to speech and audio language models over codec …; wildness 2)  
Claim: Codec tokens are trained on as if each frame had one right answer. Shifting the waveform by a few samples or adding noise nobody can hear changes many of the tokens. Training on the distribution of tokens seen across such harmless changes should make the model spend less capacity on noise in the labels.  
Prediction: First measurement, no training: 20 to 40 percent of first-codebook tokens and over 60 percent of deeper codebook tokens flip under a half-hop shift for EnCodec or DAC class codecs (this is a guess). Then on a small LibriTTS-trained token model at equal steps: 5 to 10 percent relative lower word …  
Control that could embarrass it: Uniform label smoothing with the same average target entropy. If it does as well, the structure of the jitter distribution adds nothing and this is plain regularisation. A second control: train on one randomly jittered encoding per epoch (data augmentation), …  
Kill criterion: Step 0 flip rate under 5 percent on the first codebook, or step 1 improvement over matched label smoothing and over random-jitter augmentation both under 3 percent relative word error rate.  
Cost: Step 0: 1 hour CPU, codec weights about 100 MB. Step 1: 4 arms x 2 seeds x about 6 hours on a free T4, about 48 GPU hours, or slower on the Mac. LibriTTS-clean-100 about 8 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [Analyzing and Mitigating Inconsistency in Discrete Audio Tokens for Neural Codec Language Models, arXiv …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.19283), 2024-09-28 (read)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API queries: discrete audio tokens inconsistency (one hit, above); codec soft targets for language model (zero hits, but the API was rate limiting). No web search was spent on this. Not checked: label smoothing variants in VALL-E …

**Hash-table hints for a denoiser** (image diffusion; wildness 4)  
Claim: The fastest small language models now get a large share of their early progress from hashed n-gram lookup tables that feed a memorised guess into the network. A denoiser could get the same help: look up a learned vector keyed on a coarse code of the local noisy patch and the noise level. The table would learn the local patch statistics and leave the network the global structure.  
Prediction: Small DiT (6 layers, d=384) on CIFAR-10 or ImageNet 64: 15 to 30 percent fewer steps to reach FID 10 on CIFAR-10 in a short-budget run, with the gain falling to under 5 percent at convergence. I put the chance of any gain over the wider-patch-embedding control at about 30 percent.  
Control that could embarrass it: A convolutional stem with the same receptive field and the same compute. Continuous patches are not discrete tokens, and a small convolution may already do everything the table does with smooth interpolation, where the hash table gives hard cell boundaries …  
Kill criterion: No step saving over the matched convolutional stem at any of the five checkpoints, or the key is so unstable under the noise that the same clean patch at the same sigma hits the same row less than 20 percent of the time (measurable before training, numpy …  
Cost: Key stability check: 30 minutes CPU. Training: 4 arms x 3 seeds x 3 hours on the M1 Max, about 36 hours. Table memory 2^20 x 64 floats = 256 MB per table.  
Nearest work listed: [modded-nanogpt records 62 and 92, and PR #375](https://github.com/KellerJordan/modded-nanogpt), 2026-08-30 (read); Instant-NGP (Müller et al. 2022) (not read directly); Kamb and Ganguli, analytic theory of creativity in convolutional diffusion models (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API queries: hash encoding plus diffusion denoiser (zero hits), lookup table plus diffusion plus denoiser plus patch (zero hits). Both ran while the API was rate limiting, so zero hits is weak evidence. No web search spent. Not …

**Train every block from the output error through the unembedding transpose, with no backprop through depth** (text language model; wildness 5)  
Claim: In a residual transformer the middle layers already hold something close to the output representation, which is why the logit lens works. So the output error mapped back through the transpose of the output matrix may be a good enough teaching signal for every block, with no gradient passed through the blocks above. Each block would then need only a local backward pass.  
Prediction: Measurement first, no training: on GPT-2 small, cosine between W_U^T(p - y) and the true gradient at the residual stream is above 0.5 in the last third of layers and 0.2 to 0.3 in the first third (guess). Training: a 6-layer model trained this way reaches a validation loss within 0.15 nats of …  
Control that could embarrass it: The per-layer greedy loss with a shared head (the variant above) is a known family and may match or beat the transpose feedback, in which case the idea adds nothing to greedy local learning. Also the first-layers control: if early layers get a signal with …  
Kill criterion: Step 0 cosine under 0.1 in the lower half of GPT-2 small, or step 1 more than 0.3 nats behind backprop at equal steps, or no better than the frozen-lower-blocks baseline.  
Cost: Step 0: 1 hour, GPT-2 small weights 500 MB. Step 1: 5 arms x 3 seeds x 1.5 hours on the M1 Max, about 23 hours.  
Nearest work listed: [Launay et al. 2020, Direct Feedback Alignment Scales to Modern Deep Learning Tasks and Architectures, arXiv …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.12878) (not read directly); [Scaling Laws Beyond Backpropagation, arXiv 2210.14593](https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.14593) (not read directly); [GrAPE (ICLR 2026) and Jacobian alignment work, arXiv 2409.12965](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.12965) (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch on direct feedback alignment for transformers with the unembedding as feedback; one arXiv API query on feedback alignment plus residual plus transformer plus local loss (zero hits). Found no use of the unembedding transpose …

**A speech recogniser fed only the sign of the waveform** (speech recognition; wildness 4)  
Claim: Speech clipped to plus or minus one stays understandable to people. A recogniser could take the raw sign bits as input, with no FFT, no mel filters and no gain normalisation. If accuracy holds, the front end becomes a one-bit comparator and the model is unaffected by loudness.  
Prediction: LibriSpeech train-clean-100, small CTC encoder (about 20M parameters), no language model: test-clean word error rate within 1.3x of the log-mel baseline (for example 9 percent against 7 percent), and within 1.1x under plus or minus 20 dB random gain at test time where the mel baseline without gain …  
Control that could embarrass it: Test-other and the 10 dB noise condition. Sign of a noisy waveform is dominated by the noise in quiet segments, so the model may fall apart exactly where real use happens. Also the full-precision learned filterbank arm: if raw-waveform input alone costs most …  
Kill criterion: Test-clean word error rate more than 2x the log-mel baseline, or more than 3x at 10 dB added noise.  
Cost: 4 arms x 2 seeds x about 8 hours on a free T4, about 64 GPU hours, spread over Kaggle weekly quota. LibriSpeech-100 about 6 GB plus test sets.  
Nearest work listed: Licklider and Pollack 1948, effects of differentiation, integration and infinite peak clipping on the … (not read directly); [Raw-waveform acoustic models such as arXiv 1806.07098](https://arxiv.org/abs/1806.07098) (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch for speech recognition from one-bit or sign-only waveforms; it returned work on one-bit weights and on raw-waveform models but nothing on one-bit input. Not checked: zero-crossing feature literature from the 1980s and 1990s …

### Lens: capabilities-agents-and-long-horizon-behaviour

**Hazard rises inside a run while it falls across the population** (text, agent loops with tools; wildness 2)  
Claim: Inside a single agent run, each earlier error still visible in the context raises the chance of the next error. The falling hazard seen in task-horizon fits is produced by mixing easy and hard tasks, not by anything that happens within a run. Three numbers measured on runs of 20 steps or fewer (base error rate, the lift per visible error, and the rate at which errors leave view) predict outcomes at 200 steps, where …  
Prediction: On a synthetic step-checkable task (state updates with ground truth at every step), log-odds of an error at step t rises by at least 0.3 per visible earlier error, fitted on 20-step runs. The fitted model predicts the 200-step fraction of runs with more than 20% errors within 10 percentage points; …  
Control that could embarrass it: Role swap: present the same error-containing history as another agent's transcript that the model is asked to continue or take over. If the lift is the same, this is ordinary in-context learning of an error rate and the 'own errors' framing is wrong. Second …  
Kill criterion: The fitted lift per visible error is below 0.1 log-odds with a confidence interval including zero in all model sizes tested, or the 200-step prediction is no better than constant hazard. Also killed as a horizon explanation if the single-difficulty task set …  
Cost: About 30 to 40 GPU-free hours on the M1 Max for three model sizes (roughly 80K short generations). No new downloads beyond checkpoints already used. Analysis in an afternoon.  
Nearest work listed: [Sinha et al., The Illusion of Diminishing Returns (arXiv 2509.09677)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09677), 2025-09-11 (read); [Toby Ord, Hazard Rates for AI Agents Decline as a Task Goes On](https://www.tobyord.com/writing/hazard-rates-for-ai-agents-decline), 2026-02-04 (read); [Gus Hamilton, Peto's paradox for AI agents](https://gushamilton.github.io/lab/2026/01/23/petos-paradox-ai-agents), 2026-01-23 (read); [When to Intervene / ReInBench (arXiv 2610.36471)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36471), 2026-10-27 (read)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face paper search for self-conditioning, error accumulation, hazard rate agents, error recovery agents; WebSearch for Weibull hazard agent task horizon; fetched the Ord and Hamilton posts. arXiv API and Semantic Scholar were …

**The done-clock: stopping follows turn number more than task state** (text, agent loops with tools; wildness 4)  
Claim: A model's decision to declare a task finished is driven largely by how many turns have passed and how a typical transcript of that kind ends, and only weakly by whether the task is actually finished. Early false completion and endless extra steps are the same fault seen on tasks shorter or longer than the learned typical length.  
Prediction: For a fixed model, the curve of P(declare done at turn t) on solvable tasks and on tasks rigged to be unsolvable overlaps by more than 70% (histogram intersection). Padding the history with 5 neutral no-op turns moves median completion turn earlier by at least 2 real turns. Replacing the turn …  
Control that could embarrass it: Hold turn count fixed and vary only true remaining work (D minus completed steps). If the done logit tracks remaining work tightly and barely moves with turn count, the clock idea is wrong. Also pad with tokens inside a single turn rather than extra turns; if …  
Kill criterion: In a regression of the done logit on remaining work and turn index, turn index explains less than 10% of the explained variance for all models tested, or padding turns shifts median completion by less than 0.5 turns.  
Cost: About 10 to 15 hours on the M1 Max; around 6,000 short runs. No downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [Is Your Long-Horizon Benchmark Really Deep (arXiv 2611.01927)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01927), 2026-11-01 (read); [Agentic self-awareness (arXiv 2610.19226)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19226), 2026-10-13 (read); [Serving-stack confounds in local tool use (arXiv 2609.26693)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26693), 2026-09-22 (read)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face paper search for premature termination agents, turn count, long-horizon depth. No WebSearch spent on this specific claim. Not checked against the 'stop-decision noise' idea already on the programme list beyond its title; this …

**Copy errors are captures by a rival string, not noise** (text, tool-call arguments; wildness 2)  
Claim: When a model miscopies an opaque string into a tool argument (path, ID, hash), the wrong characters almost always come from another string in the context that shares a prefix. Error rate depends on how many such rivals are in view and how long the shared prefix is, and barely on the string's own length.  
Prediction: At least 80% of wrong characters in miscopied strings match the character at the same offset in a rival string present in context. Error rate with 8 rivals sharing a 12-character prefix is at least 5x the rate with 8 unrelated strings of equal length. Doubling string length from 16 to 32 with no …  
Control that could embarrass it: Rivals of the same length and alphabet with no shared prefix. If they hurt as much as prefix-sharing rivals, the cause is plain load and the capture story is wrong. Also a no-rival condition at 64 and 128 characters: if errors grow steeply with length alone, …  
Kill criterion: Fewer than 50% of wrong characters trace to a rival at the same offset, or prefix-sharing rivals raise errors less than 2x over unrelated rivals.  
Cost: About 8 hours on the M1 Max; short prompts, roughly 100K generations at small sizes. No downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [Length Is Cheap, Competition Is Not (arXiv 2611.04169)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04169), 2026-11-03 (read); [Verbatim transcription failures (arXiv 2601.03640)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03640) (not read directly); tianpan.co blog on UUIDs versus integer aliases in agent tools, 2026-05-31 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch on identifier copying errors in tool calls; Hugging Face paper search for verbatim copy, transcription. Did not open 2601.03640, so it may already report source-of-error analysis. Induction-head copying literature not …

**Agent context rot is set by the number of superseded observations of the same thing** (text, agent loops with tools; wildness 2)  
Claim: An agent's accuracy on state questions falls with the number of out-of-date observations of the same resource in its history, not with total tokens. A harness that keeps only the latest observation per resource should let a small model hold its accuracy over long runs.  
Prediction: With total context held at 16K tokens, accuracy on 'what is the current value of X' drops by at least 30 percentage points going from 0 to 16 stale observations of X. With stale count fixed at 0, going from 2K to 16K tokens of other tool output costs less than 5 points. Long shot number: with …  
Control that could embarrass it: Fill with observations of different resources in the same format and same count. If those hurt as much as stale observations of the queried resource, it is format load, not supersession. Also the 'marked superseded' policy: if a text label fixes the problem, …  
Kill criterion: Stale count explains less variance in accuracy than log token count in a two-factor fit, or latest-only context improves 50-update accuracy by less than 10 points over full history.  
Cost: About 20 hours on the M1 Max, dominated by the 16K-token conditions on the 8B model. No downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [Proactive interference in LLM retrieval (arXiv 2506.08184)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08184), 2025-06 (read); [Stateless Language Agents (arXiv 2610.07625)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07625), 2026-10-06 (read); [WMIP cumulative state tracking (arXiv 2603.27343)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27343), 2026-10-30 (read); [Delivered, Not Used (arXiv 2611.01718)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01718), 2026-11-01 (read)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face paper search for context rot, proactive interference, agent memory, stateless agents. No WebSearch. Context-compaction products do something similar in practice; I did not survey them.

**Noticed at the tool result, lost before the action** (text, activations in an agent loop; wildness 4)  
Claim: When a tool result contradicts what the model expected, the model's activations at the result tokens carry a readable signal of the mismatch. The signal fades before the token where the next action is chosen, and that fading, not a failure to notice, is why the model repeats the failed call. Adding the signal back at the action token should raise the repair rate.  
Prediction: A linear probe on mid-layer activations at the last tool-result token separates expected from contradicting results with AUC above 0.9. The same probe direction read at the first token of the next action has AUC below 0.7 on runs where the model repeats the failed call, and above 0.85 on runs where …  
Control that could embarrass it: A random direction of equal norm, and a direction for an unrelated surface feature (result contains the word 'error'). If either raises the repair rate as much, steering is just disruption. Also test whether a probe trained only on error-word cases fails on …  
Kill criterion: Probe AUC at the result token is below 0.75 on held-out contradiction types with no error wording, or the direction's readability at the action token does not differ between repeat and changed-course runs (AUC gap below 0.05).  
Cost: About 15 hours on the M1 Max including capture. Storage of activations at a few positions per case, under 5 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [Hidden-state probes for localizing reasoning failures (arXiv 2610.22046)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22046), 2026-10-15 (read); [Self-verification in terminal agents (arXiv 2609.38812)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38812), 2026-09-30 (read); [Self-Correction Bench (arXiv 2507.02778)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02778), 2025-07 (read)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face paper search for probes agent errors, recognition action gap, self-correction. No WebSearch. Did not search the wider interpretability literature on surprise or prediction-error directions; there is likely related work on …

**A model cannot catch an error it would make again** (text, self-verification; wildness 3)  
Claim: The chance that a model's verification step catches its own wrong answer is close to one minus the chance that a fresh attempt at the same item gives the same wrong answer. Verification is mostly re-derivation, so it shares the bias. The benefit of using a second model as checker equals how much less often it repeats the first model's specific wrong answer.  
Prediction: Across items, detection rate against (1 minus same-wrong-answer repeat rate) has slope between 0.8 and 1.2 and R-squared above 0.6. For model pairs, the gain from cross-model checking over self-checking is predicted within 10 percentage points by the difference in repeat rates.  
Control that could embarrass it: Item difficulty alone. Fit detection rate on item accuracy only; if repeat rate adds less than 0.1 to R-squared beyond accuracy, the law is just 'hard items are hard to check'. Also verify a wrong answer the model never produces itself (from another model): …  
Kill criterion: R-squared below 0.3 after controlling for item accuracy, or slope outside 0.5 to 1.5 in all models.  
Cost: About 12 hours on the M1 Max; around 150K short generations across models. No downloads if a second-family small model is already present, otherwise one small checkpoint, which is outside this pass.  
Nearest work listed: [Limits of test-time compute for self-evaluation (arXiv 2611.00350)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00350), 2026-10-30 (read); [Self-refinement consolidates reachable solutions (arXiv 2609.34327)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34327), 2026-09-28 (read); [Winner's curse in self-improvement loops (arXiv 2610.09239)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09239), 2026-10 (read)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face paper search for self-verification, self-evaluation limits, self-consistency, correlated errors verifier. Saw the title of 2609.01345 on a related topic but did not open it. Older work on correlated errors across models …

**Layer budget for chaining two skills without writing the middle step** (text, activations; wildness 3)  
Claim: A model can chain skill A then skill B silently only if the layer where A's result becomes readable, plus the number of layers B needs when given its input directly, fits within the model's depth. Measuring those two numbers separately for a set of skills predicts which pairs each model size can chain without written steps.  
Prediction: Across at least 30 skill pairs and three model depths, the sign of (depth minus ready-layer of A minus needed-layers of B) predicts silent-chain success above or below 50% with accuracy at least 80%, and the margin correlates with success rate at R-squared above 0.6. Pairs that fail on a shallower …  
Control that could embarrass it: Predict from single-skill accuracy alone (product of the two accuracies). If that predicts pair success as well as the layer budget, depth is not the limiting factor. Also swap the order of A and B: the budget predicts asymmetry (A then B fits, B then A does …  
Kill criterion: Layer-budget sign predicts pair success at below 65% accuracy, or it adds less than 0.1 R-squared over the product of single-skill accuracies.  
Cost: About 25 hours on the M1 Max including probe training and patching runs for three depths. Activations at one position per item, under 10 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [The Composition Gap (arXiv 2609.32235)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32235), 2026-09-26 (read); Latent multi-hop and reversal papers (arXiv 2610.39016, 2607.00341, 2512.14427, 2406.05183 and others) (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face paper search for latent multi-hop, reversal curse, composition gap. Read abstracts only for the composition gap paper; the two-hop mechanism papers were not opened. Prior art is likely very close for the entity two-hop case. …

**Repeating a failed tool call gets more likely each time it is repeated** (text, agent loops with tools; wildness 1)  
Claim: After a tool call fails, the chance the model issues the identical call again goes up with the number of identical failed calls already in view, not down. The loop is fed by the copies of the call in context, so removing all but one copy breaks it even when the error message stays.  
Prediction: P(identical call | k identical failed calls in view) rises monotonically for k from 1 to 6, by at least 10 percentage points per added copy until it passes 80%. Collapsing the k copies to one copy plus the text 'failed k times' drops the repeat rate at k=4 by at least 40 points. Temperature 0.7 …  
Control that could embarrass it: Plant k failed calls that are all different from each other. If the model then repeats the most recent one at the same rising rate, the cause is recency or a general 'stuck' state, not copy strength from identical items. Also keep the k copies but make the …  
Kill criterion: Repeat probability is flat or falling in k for all three model sizes, or collapsing the copies changes the repeat rate at k=4 by less than 10 points.  
Cost: About 6 hours on the M1 Max; roughly 30K short generations. No downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [When to Intervene / ReInBench (arXiv 2610.36471)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36471), 2026-10-27 (read); [Restart monitors and their decision value (arXiv 2610.36544)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36544), 2026-10 (read); [Lost in Recovery / ReTrace (arXiv 2610.27954)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27954), 2026-10-20 (read)

### Lens: contradictions

**Does batch-size-1 SGD learn the rare tokens, or only match Adam on average?** (text; wildness 2)  
Claim: Marek et al. report that plain SGD at batch size 1 reaches about the same loss as Adam on language models. Kunstner et al. report that SGD makes little or no progress on rare tokens. Both can hold if batch-1 SGD closes the gap on frequent tokens and the average hides a remaining rare-token gap, or if many small steps really do fix rare tokens. A loss table split by token frequency decides which.  
Prediction: At batch size 1, the SGD minus Adam loss gap on the rarest frequency decile is under 0.15 nats, against over 1.0 nat at batch size 256 with the same token budget. If instead the rare-decile gap at batch 1 stays above 0.5 nats while the average gap is under 0.05, then Marek's result is an average …  
Control that could embarrass it: SGD at batch 256 with the learning rate raised until just stable, and SGD at batch 1 with gradient accumulated over 256 sequences. If accumulated batch-1 SGD also closes the rare gap, the effect is not about the number of updates. Also a frequency-reweighted …  
Kill criterion: If the rare-decile gap differs by less than 0.1 nats between batch 1 and batch 256, batch size is not the hidden variable and the idea is dropped. If the pilot cannot reproduce Kunstner's rare-token stall at batch 256, the setup is wrong and nothing is …  
Cost: About 16 hours on the Mac for 8 runs plus pilot; under 8 GB memory; downloads: WikiText-103 (about 180 MB) and a tokenizer. No model weights.

**Does a noise-free training target bring memorization forward, and only in low dimension?** (image (small diffusion / flow matching); wildness 3)  
Claim: Theory says the randomness of the diffusion training target is what lets models generalize. An experiment with the exact noise-free target found no loss in sample quality, so its authors say target randomness is not the cause. I think both hold in different dimensions, and the place to see it is the training time at which memorization starts, which neither side measured under the noise-free target.  
Prediction: On 32x32 grayscale faces (d=1024) with n=1024, the memorization onset time under the exact target is at least 0.8 of the onset time under the usual noisy target. After projecting the same images to their top 32 principal components, the ratio falls below 0.5. The quality onset time changes by less …  
Control that could embarrass it: An M=8 partial-averaging arm (Bertrand's intermediate estimator). If onset time does not move monotonically from M=1 to M=8 to exact, the effect is noise in my measurement. Also a matched-step-size check, because removing target noise lowers gradient noise …  
Kill criterion: If the onset ratio is within 0.8 to 1.2 at every dimension tested, target noise does not set the clock at any dimension I can reach and Vastola's mechanism is not visible in practice. If the larger-batch control reproduces the whole shift, the effect is …  
Cost: About 30 hours on the Mac (Bonnaire's onset is near 300 steps per training image, so about 300k steps at n=1024); under 6 GB memory; download CelebA aligned images (about 1.4 GB) or a pre-shrunk …  
Nearest work listed: [Bertrand et al., On the Closed-Form of Flow Matching (2506.03719)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03719), 2025-06 (read); [Bonnaire et al., Why Diffusion Models Don't Memorize (2505.17638)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17638), 2025-05 (read); [Vastola, Generalization through variance (2504.12532)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12532), 2025-04 (read)

**The min-p dispute is a temperature-order artefact: an exact identity and a matched re-test** (text (sampling); wildness 2)  
Claim: The min-p paper reports gains over top-p at high temperature; a reanalysis finds none. The original authors switched libraries because one applied temperature after truncation. Min-p is unchanged by that order up to a known change of threshold, but top-p is changed a great deal. So the choice of order changes how strong the top-p baseline is, and that alone can create or remove the reported win.  
Prediction: On GSM8K with a small local model at T=2 and T=3, top-p with temperature applied last matches min-p accuracy within 2 points at equal output entropy. Top-p with temperature applied first is at least 10 points below min-p at the same nominal settings. When all three are compared at equal measured …  
Control that could embarrass it: If min-p still wins at matched entropy by more than 3 points, the gain is real and not an order artefact. A second control is top-k, which is also order-invariant; if top-k tracks min-p at matched entropy then the story is only about order-invariance.  
Kill criterion: If the two top-p orders differ by less than 3 points at the paper's own settings, order is not the hidden variable. If 2408.13586 already reports a matched-entropy comparison that includes temperature order, the idea reduces to a note.  
Cost: About 10 hours on the Mac; 4 to 8 GB memory; one small model download (about 1 to 2 GB) when the lab is ready. The identity itself costs nothing.

**Shuffle the pixels: is locality in diffusion models the data's or the network's?** (image (small diffusion); wildness 3)  
Claim: Kamb and Ganguli explain what convolutional diffusion models generate by assuming the network is local and translation-equivariant. Lukoianov et al. say locality comes from pixel correlations in the data, and that U-Nets and transformers show similar sensitivity patterns. I think the answer depends on noise level: at high noise the data's correlations decide, at low noise the network's receptive field decides. A …  
Prediction: For a conv-only U-Net trained on pixel-shuffled CIFAR-10, the correlation between its measured sensitivity field (mapped back through the shuffle) and the Wiener field is above 0.7 at high noise (signal-to-noise below 1) and below 0.4 at low noise (signal-to-noise above 10). For an MLP denoiser the …  
Control that could embarrass it: A block shuffle that permutes only within 4x4 blocks. The data account predicts a smooth change with shuffle range; if the conv net's behaviour jumps instead, something other than correlation length is at work. Also check that the MLP's results really are …  
Kill criterion: If the conv net's field on shuffled data follows the Wiener field with correlation above 0.7 at every noise level, the data account wins outright and noise level is not the hidden variable. If it stays grid-local at every noise level, the architecture account …  
Cost: About 12 hours on the Mac for four short trainings plus Jacobians; under 8 GB; download CIFAR-10 (170 MB).

**Cut the chain: is a long reasoning chain a cause of errors or a sign of a hard draw?** (text (reasoning models); wildness 2)  
Claim: One 2025 paper finds that, for the same question, shorter thinking chains are far more accurate than the longest ones. Other work finds that more thinking budget helps. Both hold if length is a symptom: the model writes more when that particular sample has gone wrong. Truncating a given chain at fixed points, and forcing the answer, separates the symptom from any causal harm.  
Prediction: On a small local reasoning model, naturally short chains (bottom third by length within a question) are at least 10 points more accurate than naturally long ones (top third). But cutting the long chains at the question's median length and forcing an answer changes their accuracy by less than 3 …  
Control that could embarrass it: Cut chains at random token positions unrelated to length rank, to measure how much any forced stop hurts by itself. If forced stopping alone costs 5 points, the 3-point window is meaningless and the thresholds must be reset from that baseline.  
Kill criterion: If natural short and long chains differ by less than 5 points on this model, the starting observation does not reproduce at small scale and the test cannot arbitrate. If 2604.10739 already reports a per-chain truncation analysis with this split, the idea is a …  
Cost: About 10 hours on the Mac (2,400 base chains plus about 5,000 truncated continuations); 4 to 8 GB; one model download of 1 to 3 GB when the lab is ready.  
Nearest work listed: [Don't Overthink it: Preferring Shorter Thinking Chains for Improved LLM Reasoning (2505.17813)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17813), 2025-05 (not read directly); [When More Thinking Hurts: Overthinking in LLM Test-Time Compute Scaling (2604.10739)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10739), 2026-04 (not read directly); [Lanham et al., Measuring Faithfulness in Chain-of-Thought Reasoning (2307.13702, id from memory)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.13702), 2023-07 (not read directly)

**Is 'same initialization' in subliminal learning really 'same basis'? A relabelled twin and an aligned stranger** (small networks (MNIST), with a text follow-up; wildness 4)  
Claim: The subliminal learning paper says trait transfer through unrelated data needs teacher and student to share an initialization. A later paper says the transfer is one steering vector being distilled. A vector is defined in a basis, so the requirement may be a shared basis, not shared weights. A student whose hidden units are a permuted copy of the teacher's initialization computes the same function but has a …  
Prediction: Relabelled twin (teacher initialization with hidden units permuted): transfer is fully preserved, test accuracy within 2 points of the same-initialization student, because gradient descent is equivariant to that relabelling. Aligned stranger (independent seed, then weight-matched to the teacher's …  
Control that could embarrass it: A student aligned to a different, unrelated network (not the teacher). If it shows the same transfer as the stranger aligned to the teacher, alignment is not what carries the trait and any gain is an artefact of the matching step.  
Kill criterion: If the aligned stranger recovers under 10% of the gain across 10 seeds with both matching variants, 'same initialization' means what it says and the basis reading is wrong. If the relabelled twin fails, my reproduction is broken.  
Cost: About 2 hours of CPU; under 2 GB memory; download MNIST (11 MB).  
Nearest work listed: [Cloud et al., Subliminal Learning (2507.14805)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14805), 2025-07 (read); [Subliminal Learning Is Steering Vector Distillation (2606.00995)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00995), 2026-06 (not read directly); [Channel Location Constrains the Auditability of Subliminal Learning (2606.22019)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22019), 2026-06 (not read directly)

**Where does speech sit? Measuring how long the training target stays random for conditional speech data** (audio (measurement only, no model); wildness 4)  
Claim: The argument about whether target randomness matters in diffusion training was fought on images. Text-conditioned speech is different in two ways: spectrograms have low effective dimension, and each text condition has about one training example. The first should make target randomness last longer across the noise schedule than in images; the second should make it vanish. Nobody has measured which wins, and it can be …  
Prediction: For unconditional 80-bin mel patches of 32 frames (d=2560) from 50k clips, the fraction of the schedule where the largest posterior weight is below 0.5 is at least 0.4, against about 0.2 for CIFAR-10 computed the same way. Conditioned on the exact text, it is 0 (one example). Conditioned on a …  
Control that could embarrass it: CIFAR-10 must reproduce a transition near 0.2; if it does not, the estimator is wrong. A Gaussian with the same covariance as the mel patches gives the answer that covariance alone predicts; if speech matches the Gaussian exactly, there is nothing …  
Kill criterion: If unconditional mel patches collapse as early as CIFAR (fraction within 0.05 of the CIFAR value), speech is not a different regime and the follow-up experiment (repeat of the memorization-clock test on a tiny speech model) is not worth queuing.

### Lens: control-signal

**RoPE leaves the top 1.65 octaves of the position spectrum empty: bands between 1 and pi radians per token** (language models (also any RoPE model: speech-token LMs, …; wildness 2)  
Claim: Token positions are integers, so by the sampling theorem the fastest useful rotary frequency is pi radians per token. Standard RoPE stops at 1 radian, and that single choice caps how sharply any head can pick one exact offset for a given query-key norm. Adding two or three bands between 1.7 and 2.9 radians should roughly double that sharpness and should help most in models with normalised queries and keys.  
Prediction: In a small GPT with QK-norm and a fixed attention scale, swapping the three slowest RoPE bands for bands at 1.7, 2.3 and 2.9 rad lowers validation loss by at least 0.005 nats at equal steps (3 seeds), and raises the peak attention mass of previous-token heads by at least 1.5x at equal logit scale. …  
Control that could embarrass it: Replace the three slowest bands with extra bands inside the existing range (0.5, 0.7, 0.9 rad). If that gives the same gain, the effect is about having more fast bands or about dropping slow ones (p-RoPE already shows dropping slow bands helps), not about …  
Kill criterion: Loss gain below 0.002 nats and no change in positional-head sharpness in the QK-normed model, or the within-range control matches the above-1 arm.  
Cost: About 1 to 2 hours per run at 30M parameters on the M1 Max, 12 to 15 runs: two days. No downloads beyond a public text set. Under 16 GB memory.  
Nearest work listed: [LeRoPE: Learnable RoPE Frequencies Improve Language Modeling (arXiv 2607.10134)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10134), 2026-07-11 (read); [Barbero et al., Round and Round We Go! What makes Rotary Positional Encodings useful? (arXiv 2410.06205)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.06205), 2024-10 (read); [WavePrune: One period is often enough for RoPE (arXiv 2610.06963)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06963), 2026-10-03 (read); [Rotary Positional Embeddings as Phase Modulation: bounds on the RoPE base (arXiv 2602.10959)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10959), 2026-02-11 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch on RoPE maximum frequency / Nyquist / pi; Hugging Face papers search 'RoPE frequencies above one radian'; read LeRoPE, WavePrune and Barbero et al. through WebFetch. arXiv and Semantic Scholar APIs were rate-limited (HTTP …

**The unpatchify layer is a time-interleaved converter: random patch-grid phase per sampling step, at no extra cost** (image and video generation (DiT / flow models); wildness 2)  
Claim: In a DiT each of the p*p pixel positions inside a patch is written by a different set of output weights, which is the structure of a time-interleaved converter. Gain and offset mismatch between the positions must leave energy at the patch-grid frequency and its harmonics, locked to the same place at every sampling step, so it adds coherently. Choosing a different grid offset at each step makes that error add …  
Prediction: (a) For a pretrained 2x2-patch flow model, the part of the velocity that changes when the patch grid is moved by one latent pixel has more than half its energy within one frequency bin of the latent Nyquist lines. (b) With a random grid offset in {0,1}^2 per step, the spectral peak of the final …  
Control that could embarrass it: Four-pass full averaging over all phases is the upper bound; if even that does not move the grid peak, the grid energy comes from the VAE and not the patch layer. Second control: a per-step perturbation of equal norm that is not a grid shift (tiny noise); if …  
Kill criterion: Phase-dependent part of the velocity is under 0.1% of velocity energy at all noise levels, or the grid peak moves less than 2 dB with random phase.  
Cost: Step 1 is about 400 forward passes: one to two hours. Step 2 is 400 images: three to six hours on the M1 Max depending on model. No new downloads if an already-cached model is used.  
Nearest work listed: [Phase Marginalization for Patch-Grid Instability in Vision Transformers (arXiv 2606.08132)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08132), 2026-06-06 (not read directly); [Alias-Free Latent Diffusion Models (arXiv 2503.09419)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09419), 2025-03-12 (not read directly); [Rojas-Gomez et al., Making Vision Transformers Truly Shift-Equivariant (arXiv 2305.16316)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.16316), 2023-05 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch on DiT patch-grid artifacts and per-step random shift; Hugging Face papers searches on shift-equivariant diffusion transformers and random shift per denoising step. Found nothing that randomises patch phase across sampling …

**Does the 10 ms hop alias voice pitch into the syllable band? A comb-shaped WER prediction for Whisper** (speech recognition; wildness 3)  
Claim: The log-mel front end used by Whisper and nearly every recogniser samples a 25 ms window every 10 ms. For voices near 100 Hz or 200 Hz the low mel channels beat at the pitch, and the 100 Hz frame rate records that beat at the difference frequency, which lands at 2 to 10 Hz where speech content lives. The squared Hann window has an exact zero at 120 Hz, so a voice at 120 Hz should be clean and a voice at 106 to 110 …  
Prediction: On pitch-flattened LibriSpeech speech resynthesised at fixed F0 in 2 Hz steps from 96 to 126 Hz, whisper-tiny and whisper-base WER at F0 between 104 and 110 Hz is at least 10% relative higher than at 120 Hz, and the encoder output moves at least 2x more under a 5 ms shift of the audio at F0=100 Hz …  
Control that could embarrass it: Vocoder artefacts vary with F0 on their own. Control: the same resynthesised audio fed through a front end with a 5 ms hop followed by proper low-pass decimation. If the comb in WER survives that, it is the vocoder or the model, not aliasing. Second control: …  
Kill criterion: WER at 104 to 110 Hz within the bootstrap interval of WER at 120 Hz for both tiny and base, and shift sensitivity ratio below 1.2.  
Cost: WORLD resynthesis of 500 utterances at 16 pitches: about 2 CPU hours. Whisper tiny/base on 8000 utterances: 2 to 4 hours. Whisper weights (tiny/base/small) total under 700 MB. Under 8 GB memory.  
Nearest work listed: [No Pitch Left Behind: Addressing Gender Unbalance in ASR through Pitch Manipulation (arXiv 2310.06590)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.06590), 2023-10 (not read directly); Kawahara et al., STRAIGHT (Speech Communication 1999), 1999 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch on mel hop, pitch harmonics, aliasing and Whisper WER for low voices: found pitch-and-gender work, nothing on hop aliasing of pitch. One Hugging Face papers search on Whisper sensitivity to small time shifts: returned …

**Is a codec's token rate wasted? The decoder's response to latent noise across frequency, and error-feedback encoding as the test** (speech and audio codecs; image tokenizers as a second case; wildness 3)  
Claim: First-order error feedback at the quantiser pushes quantisation error to high frequencies along the token sequence. It helps only if the decoder responds less to high-frequency latent error than to low-frequency error, and it must hurt by up to 3 dB if the response is flat. So measuring the decoder's response across temporal frequency tells in advance whether a frozen codec can be improved at encode time and whether …  
Prediction: For FSQ speech codecs at 50 Hz, the decoder's sensitivity to latent perturbation at the temporal Nyquist frequency is at least 6 dB below its sensitivity near DC, which is an effective oversampling ratio of 2 or more. On such a codec, error-feedback encoding with the unchanged decoder lowers …  
Control that could embarrass it: Add white noise of the same extra power as the feedback introduces, without shaping. If quality changes the same way, the effect is about moving latents off the training distribution, not about spectrum. Also report token entropy under a small n-gram model, …  
Kill criterion: The response curve fails to predict the sign of the error-feedback effect in two or more of four codecs.  
Cost: A day. Codec weights are 100 MB to 1 GB each. Decoding 200 utterances is minutes per codec.

**How long should the weight average be? A tracking-filter law, window proportional to (noise / drift^2)^(1/3)** (training (diffusion and language models); wildness 2)  
Claim: A weight EMA is a first-order tracking filter. Its error is a lag term from the weights' steady drift plus a noise term from step-to-step jitter, and the best window follows from the two in closed form. This predicts the best EMA length from saved snapshots without evaluating any EMA, and predicts that at constant learning rate the best window grows more slowly than training time.  
Prediction: Across four checkpoints spanning a 16x range of training time at constant learning rate, the best post-hoc EMA window grows as t^a with a between 0.55 and 0.8, not 1. The window computed from snapshot statistics alone, 1/K with K = (4*s^2/r)^(1/3), lands within a factor of 1.5 of the window that …  
Control that could embarrass it: Iterate noise is not white: it is correlated over roughly 1/(learning rate * curvature) steps, which the formula ignores. Test by estimating r at two snapshot spacings; if the prediction swings by more than 2x between spacings, the white-noise assumption has …  
Kill criterion: Predicted window off by more than 3x from the swept optimum at most checkpoints, or measured exponent a indistinguishable from 1.  
Cost: One training run of 4 to 8 hours plus about 40 evaluations: one to two days. No downloads beyond CIFAR-10 or a text slice.

**A whiteness test for samplers: clean-image estimates along a stochastic sampler must have uncorrelated increments** (image generation (diffusion and flow models); long shot; wildness 4)  
Claim: For an exact denoiser run along the exact reverse stochastic process, the sequence of clean-image estimates is a martingale. So the changes between consecutive estimates are uncorrelated, and their squared norms add up to the squared norm of the total change. Any departure is model or sampler error, measurable per prompt with no reference image, and guidance should break it in a specific direction.  
Prediction: On a class-conditional model with a 64-step stochastic sampler, R is within 1 +/- 0.05 at guidance scale 1, rises monotonically with guidance, and exceeds 1.3 at the scale that minimises FID. The excess sits mostly below one eighth of the Nyquist spatial frequency. Limiting guidance to a middle …  
Control that could embarrass it: Discretising the SDE breaks the martingale property by itself. If the Gaussian-mixture toy with its exact denoiser shows R more than 2% from 1 at 64 steps, the instrument is measuring step size, not the model. The deeper problem: guidance improves images …  
Kill criterion: R at guidance 1 is further than 0.15 from 1 on a well-trained model even at 256 steps, or R is not monotone in guidance scale.  
Cost: Toy: one hour of CPU. Small model: 3000 samples at 64 steps, 4 to 10 hours on the M1 Max. Weights under 500 MB for the XS or S models.

**Skips and repeats in autoregressive TTS as cycle slips of a tracking loop: constant hazard and an exponential law in temperature** (speech synthesis; long shot; wildness 4)  
Claim: An autoregressive TTS model keeps its place in the text with attention heads that track a reading position. That is a phase-tracking loop driven by sampling noise. Tracking-loop theory says loss of lock is a memoryless rare event whose rate falls exponentially with loop signal-to-noise ratio. So within the trained length range, time to first skip or repeat should be exponentially distributed, and the log of the slip …  
Prediction: Within lengths seen in training, the Weibull shape parameter of time-to-first-slip is between 0.85 and 1.15 (drift would give a shape clearly above 1.3). Log slip rate against 1/T or 1/T^2 is linear with R^2 above 0.95 over at least five temperatures. Slip landing points are at least 2x enriched at …

Kill criterion: Weibull shape above 1.3 inside the trained length range, or the temperature plot is not linear (R^2 below 0.85).  
Cost: About 5000 generations of 2 to 3 minutes of speech tokens. At roughly real time for a 0.6B model on the M1 Max that is several days; a T4 on Kaggle or Modal free tier would cut it to one or two. …

Claim: Damage from a packed format can be stated as the amount of noise you would have to add to the input of the full-precision model to get the same performance. Claim: Decisions to stop are low-probability at each step where they should not fire and high-probability where they should. Then: (a) reasoning-token count for a small thinking model at 4-bit rises 10 to 25 percent and its coefficient of variation rises by at least 15 percent; (b) …  
Control that could embarrass it: Matched Gaussian weight noise in the full-precision model, scaled per layer to the rounding error. If it reproduces the length changes, the effect is noise size and the story is complete and simple. If it does not, the Jensen mechanism is wrong and the cause …

**How many of an adapter's bits survive its own packing, and what a kilobyte buys** (text LLM adapters, with a speech-voice side test; wildness 2)  
Claim: A LoRA is reported to hold 2 to 3.4 bits of memorised content per trainable parameter. I claim almost all of that survives storing the adapter itself at 4 bits per parameter and about half survives at 2 bits, so adapters are stored far too generously at 16 bits. I also claim that per bit of file size, a set of per-row scale factors on the base holds more than a LoRA of the same bytes.

**Long shot: delete speech tokens to speed up speech, and read the true information rate off the point where it breaks** (speech synthesis and codecs; wildness 5)

**Same settings, different distribution: sampler order as an unreported variable in every sampled benchmark** (language models, evaluation; wildness 2)  
Claim: Engines apply temperature and nucleus truncation in different orders, so the same nominal settings give different output entropy. I predict this accounts for most of the between-engine gap on sampled benchmarks, and that matching engines on realized entropy instead of nominal settings removes it.  
Prediction: On a 1B to 4B open model at T=0.8, top_p=0.95, mean per-token entropy on high-entropy steps (model entropy above 2 nats) differs by at least 20% between the two orders; HumanEval pass@10 differs by at least 2 points; and after re-tuning one engine's temperature to match the other's mean realized …  
Control that could embarrass it: Real next-token distributions are mostly peaked. If fewer than 5% of generated tokens fall on steps where the two orders keep different sets, the benchmark effect will be inside seed noise. Run 5 seeds per arm and report the seed spread first.  
Kill criterion: Pass@10 and self-consistency gaps between orders are both smaller than the 5-seed standard deviation.  
Cost: About 10 hours of Mac time for the measurements, 2 days of code reading for the census. One 2 to 3 GB model download. No cloud.

**Did image generators inherit 4:2:0? Colour bandwidth as a fossil of JPEG training data** (image generation, forensics, VAEs; wildness 3)  
Claim: JPEG usually stores colour at half the resolution of brightness. A generator trained mostly on web JPEGs may never have seen sharp colour detail, so its outputs should have the colour bandwidth of a JPEG even when saved losslessly. If so, part of every latent is spent encoding colour detail that is not there.

**Sub-pixel shift as a fingerprint: reading an edit model's training resize convention from its output drift** (image editing, preprocessing; wildness 4)  
Claim: Image-edit models are known to return outputs slightly shifted or zoomed relative to the input. I predict the shift is not random but follows the formula for a pixel-centre convention mismatch between the training resize and the inference resize, so it can be predicted from the scale factor and cancelled by pre-shifting the input.  
Prediction: For identity edits at 8 input resolutions, the measured shift fits offset = a + b(s − 1)/(2s) with residual under 0.1 pixel at 6 or more of the 8, and applying the opposite shift to the input removes at least 80% of the drift.  
Control that could embarrass it: Run the VAE encode-decode round trip alone with the same resize path. If the round trip already shows the whole shift, the model is innocent and the bug is in the pipeline code, which is a smaller finding. Also check that shift does not vary with seed; if it …  
Kill criterion: Shift varies across seeds by more than it varies across scale factors, or the formula explains less than half the variance.  
Cost: About 320 edits at 4 to 8 steps: 10 to 15 hours on the Mac with existing weights. No new downloads if Rob already has the edit model.

**What does Whisper see when nothing is said? Normalisation scope and input level as hidden causes of invented text** (speech recognition, audio front ends; wildness 3)  
Claim: Whisper's input clamp is set relative to the loudest moment in whatever array it is given, and stacks differ on whether that array is the whole file, a 30-second chunk or a streaming buffer. I predict this changes how silence and zero padding are represented by up to a quarter of the input range, and that hallucination on silent stretches depends on it.  
Prediction: On long recordings where segment levels vary by 30 dB or more, changing the clamp scope from whole-file to per-window changes the count of hallucinated words in non-speech regions by at least 1.5x in one direction, while WER on the speech regions changes by under 3% relative. Separately, a gain …  
Control that could embarrass it: Whisper was trained on web audio at many levels and may be invariant to all of this. Check first that the features really differ by the predicted amount on real files; if real recordings almost never have a floor above the clamp, scope cannot matter. Also use …  
Kill criterion: Hallucinated-word counts differ by less than 20% across scope arms and across gain arms.  
Cost: About 10 hours of audio through 2 models in about 15 arms: 15 to 20 hours on the Mac. Downloads: 3 GB of models, under 10 GB of audio.

**Conditioning or sampler? A two-by-two swap to explain why the same image model behaves differently in different stacks** (image generation, text encoders, chat templates; wildness 2)  
Claim: Image models that use a chat LLM as text encoder pass their prompt through a template, a token drop index, a hidden-layer choice and a padding rule, and ports differ on these. I predict that these conditioning differences, not sampler differences, explain most of the quality gap users see between stacks, and that text rendering is where it shows.  
Prediction: Between two stacks at the same seed, steps and nominal sampler, OCR exact-match on a 50-prompt text-rendering set differs by at least 10 points; and in a swap test, moving only the conditioning tensors from stack A into stack B closes at least half of that gap.  
Control that could embarrass it: If the conditioning tensors from all ports match to within fp16 rounding, the idea is dead before any image is made, and the sampler explanation in the thread stands. That check is the first thing to run.  
Kill criterion: Conditioning cosine similarity above 0.999 on all tokens across ports, or the swap closes less than 20% of the gap.  
Cost: Tensor comparison: half a day. Two-by-two: 400 images at 20 to 50 steps, roughly 30 to 50 hours on the Mac with a quantized model, or about 6 hours on a free cloud GPU session. No new downloads if …  
Nearest work listed: [Li et al., Text Template Tokens Are Implicit Semantic Registers in Diffusion Transformers, arXiv 2607.19139](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19139), 2026-07-21 (read); [QwenLM/Qwen-Image issue 54, official paper prompt cannot be replicated locally with ComfyUI](https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen-Image/issues/54), 2025-08-09 (read); Padding-token analyses for T5 and CLIP text encoders in text-to-image models (not read directly)

**The invisible byte: Unicode form as an unhandled input variable across open tokenizers** (language models, tokenizers, audit; wildness 2)  
Claim: The same visible text can arrive as composed or decomposed Unicode, and macOS file names, PDF copy-paste and some keyboards produce the decomposed form. I predict most open LLM tokenizers do not normalise it, that decomposed input costs far more tokens in languages with stacked diacritics, and that accuracy drops with it.  
Prediction: Among the tokenizer.json files of the 500 most-downloaded text-generation models, more than 60% apply no Unicode normaliser. For those, decomposed Vietnamese text needs at least 1.5 times the tokens of composed text, and a 1B to 4B model loses at least 5 points on a Vietnamese question-answering …  
Control that could embarrass it: Serving stacks may normalise before the tokenizer, in which case the tokenizer audit overstates the real exposure. Test the actual path: llama.cpp server, Ollama and MLX-LM given decomposed bytes over HTTP. Also confirm the benchmark source text is composed …  
Kill criterion: Fewer than 30% of tokenizers lack a normaliser, or the accuracy drop on decomposed Vietnamese is under 2 points for all three models.  
Cost: Audit: one day, under 5 GB of small JSON files. Accuracy runs: 10 to 15 hours on the Mac with three 2 to 3 GB models.

**How many kilohertz does a speech model really produce? Output bandwidth as a fossil of training-audio history** (speech synthesis, neural codecs, audit; wildness 4)  
Claim: A speech model's stated sample rate says what container it writes, not what it learned. I predict many open models that write 24, 44.1 or 48 kHz audio produce almost nothing above the cutoff of the lossy codecs or low-rate corpora in their training data, so a large share of their codec tokens or vocoder work describes an empty band.  
Prediction: Of 30 open TTS and speech-token models that output 24 kHz or higher, at least 10 have a 99%-energy bandwidth below 0.7 of their Nyquist frequency on fricative-rich sentences, and the cutoffs cluster at 8, 11 and 16 kHz rather than spreading evenly.  
Control that could embarrass it: Speech itself has little energy above 10 kHz, so compare against studio recordings at 48 kHz of the same sentence types and use sibilants, where real speech reaches 16 kHz and beyond. Also exclude any demo sample that was ever stored lossy, since the hosting …  
Kill criterion: Fewer than 5 of 30 models fall below 0.7 of Nyquist, or cutoffs show no clustering at codec-typical frequencies.  
Cost: Stage one: one day of CPU and small downloads. Stage two: 5 hours on the Mac, about 10 GB of models.  
Nearest work listed: [HiFiTTS-2: A Large-Scale High Bandwidth Speech Dataset, arXiv 2506.04152](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04152), 2025-06-04 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One Hugging Face papers query on TTS effective bandwidth and upsampled training data. No arXiv search succeeded. TTS practitioners know this anecdotally; I did not find or rule out a systematic census.

### Lens: data-as-the-subject

**The oracle ceiling for data selection: on a corpus with known entropy, selection is rarity rebalancing and nothing else** (language models (training data selection); wildness 3)  
Claim: Build a synthetic corpus whose exact next-token distribution is computable, so every token's reducible loss is known exactly. An oracle that trains only on the tokens with the highest true reducible loss gives an upper bound on what any selection method can buy. I predict the bound is small when grammar rules are equally frequent and large when rule frequencies are Zipfian, and that a model-free rarity reweighting …  
Prediction: Counted in backward-pass tokens, oracle selection reaches the uniform baseline's final excess loss with at most 1.3x fewer tokens on a flat-frequency source, and 3x to 5x fewer on a source with Zipf exponent 1 over rules. Practical selectors (reference-model perplexity, online high-loss) capture …  
Control that could embarrass it: Random selection at the same keep rate with the learning rate retuned. If that matches the oracle, the gain was a step-size effect. Second control: evaluate on a test set drawn from the unselected distribution, so rebalancing cannot win by changing the test.  
Kill criterion: Oracle gain on the flat source above 2x, or static rarity reweighting capturing under 30% of oracle gain on the Zipf source. Either means selection is doing something other than rebalancing.  
Cost: About 30 runs of 1 to 3 hours on the M1 Max; corpus generation and exact entropies under 2 CPU hours; no downloads beyond a 1 GB FineWeb shard.

**Where does a diffusion model need many images? A data-requirement curve over noise level, and a corpus of thumbnails plus a few sharp images** (image generation (diffusion and flow training); wildness 3)  
Claim: The number of distinct training images a diffusion model needs is not one number. It depends on noise level, and I predict it peaks at intermediate noise and is small at both ends. If so, a model trained on a large set of low-resolution images for the middle and high noise bands and a small set of sharp images for the low-noise band matches one trained on the large set at full resolution.  
Prediction: On 64 px data with 50,000 images: restricting the low-noise third of the schedule to 1,000 images changes FID by under 10%. Restricting the high-noise third to 1,000 changes it by under 25%. Restricting the middle third to 1,000 at least doubles FID and gives over 20% near-copies of training …  
Control that could embarrass it: Restrict all bands to 1,000 images but keep the step count: if the band-restricted arms look like this arm, the band structure means nothing. Also swap which band gets the small set at random boundaries, to check the result is not an artefact of where the …  
Kill criterion: N*(sigma) from the two-model test is monotone in noise level in either direction with no interior peak, or the thumbnail arm is more than 40% worse in FID than full data.  
Cost: About 20 runs of 3 to 6 GPU hours on free Kaggle; datasets under 10 GB.

**Swap pairs: a caption corpus teaches binding only through examples a bag-of-words reading gets wrong** (image generation (text-to-image training data); wildness 3)  
Claim: A text-to-image model has no reason to learn which adjective goes with which noun unless the training set contains pairs of images whose captions use the same words with the pairing swapped. I predict that the count of such swap pairs, not the dataset size, sets binding accuracy, and that natural caption corpora contain almost none.  
Prediction: In a two-object synthetic scene corpus of 100,000 images: with zero swap pairs, accuracy on held-out swapped pairings is below 50%, because the model draws the pairing it saw. With 200 swap pairs added (0.4% of the data), held-out binding accuracy exceeds 85%. Growing the zero-swap corpus tenfold …  
Control that could embarrass it: Add 200 extra ordinary images of the same attribute combinations without their swap partners. If binding rises as much, the gain was exposure, not contrast. Also repeat with a frozen pretrained text encoder: if binding is already perfect at zero swap pairs, …  
Kill criterion: Zero-swap accuracy on held-out pairings above 75%, or the 200-pair arm below 65%.  
Cost: Caption census: 1 CPU hour, 1 GB download. Training: about 15 runs of 2 to 4 hours.

**Most of a taste adapter is not taste: keeps against a random draw from the same archive** (image generation (personal fine-tuning, self-generated data); wildness 3)  
Claim: When an adapter is trained on the images a person kept from a model's own output, most of the change it makes is the model being trained on itself with that person's prompts. The same change appears when training on a random sample of everything generated. The part that is taste is the difference between keeps and discards, and it is small because the keep decisions carry few bits.  
Prediction: Let D_K, D_A and D_D be the shifts in mean image features (DINOv2, held-out prompts, fixed seeds) caused by adapters trained on keeps, on a prompt-matched random sample of all generations, and on discards. I predict cosine(D_K, D_A) above 0.8 and the norm of D_K minus D_A below 40% of the norm of …  
Control that could embarrass it: Shuffle the keep labels within each prompt and retrain. If the shuffled-keep adapter raises the blind keep rate as much as the real one, there is no taste signal in the labels at all, only self-training.  
Kill criterion: cosine(D_K, D_A) below 0.5, or the keeps adapter beats the random-sample adapter on blind keep rate by more than three times the shuffled-label control's gain.  
Cost: Four LoRA trainings of 1 to 3 hours each on the M1 Max; about 2 hours of Rob's blind rating; no downloads beyond models he has.
