**Long shot: a pass or fail is worth H(r) bits, so positive-only self-training should peak near a 37% pass rate and high-pass pipelines discard most of their information** (language models (self-generated training data with a …; wildness 5)  
Claim: When a model generates attempts and a verifier accepts a fraction r, the accepted set carries at most r*log2(1/r) bits per attempt and the rejected set carries (1-r)*log2(1/(1-r)). Training on accepted samples only is therefore most informative per attempt at r = 1/e, about 37%. At a 90% pass rate the failures hold 71% of the available information.  
Prediction: In a toy loop where task difficulty is tuned to hold the pass rate fixed, held-out accuracy gain per generated attempt for positive-only training peaks at a pass rate between 0.25 and 0.45. When failures are also used (attempts prefixed with a pass or fail token), the peak moves to 0.45 to 0.6, and …  
Control that could embarrass it: Accept at random with the same rate. Any gain there is self-distillation, and must be subtracted. Also hold difficulty fixed and vary the pass rate by changing sampling temperature, to check the peak belongs to the pass rate and not to the difficulty mix.  
Kill criterion: Positive-only peak outside 0.15 to 0.6, or the curve is flat within seed noise, or using failures gives under 1.3x at a 90% pass rate.  
Cost: About 40 short runs, 40 to 60 hours of M1 Max time in total; no downloads.

**Long shot: text written in the order of computation. Definitions before uses raises a corpus's learnability exponent** (language models (code and structured text; corpus rewriting); wildness 4)  
Claim: A left-to-right model learns faster from a document when everything a token depends on appears before it. For source code this can be arranged exactly: reorder each module so callees come before callers. I predict the reordered corpus has a faster fall of conditional entropy with context, a better learning-curve exponent, and that a small model trained on it does better even when tested on code in the original order.  
Prediction: On a Python corpus of 100M to 300M tokens with a 20M to 50M parameter model: bottom-up ordering lowers loss on function-name tokens at call sites by over 15% and total bits per byte by 3 to 6% at matched training tokens. gamma estimated on CPU with a variable-order compressor is higher for the …  
Control that could embarrass it: A random permutation of top-level definitions. If random order does as well as callees-first, the gain is from order diversity, not dependency order. Callers-first should be worst; if it is not, the mechanism is wrong.  
Kill criterion: Call-site name loss differs by under 5% between callees-first and callers-first, or the measured learning-curve exponents do not rank in the order the CPU-estimated gamma/(2 beta) predicts.  
Cost: Corpus build and statistics: half a day of CPU. Nine training runs of 2 to 6 hours on the M1 Max. One code dataset download of a few GB.  
Nearest work listed: [Cagnetta, Raventos, Ganguli, Wyart, Deriving Neural Scaling Laws from the statistics of natural language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07488), 2026-02-07 (v3 2026-07-03) (read); [Lee et al., Teaching Arithmetic to Small Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.03381), 2023-07 (not read directly); [Learning to Learn a Language (Prior-Fitted Language Model)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05879), 2026-10-05 (read)

**The context a model can use should grow as tokens seen to the power 1/(2 beta): a check on public Pythia checkpoints with no training** (language models (training data schedule, sequence length); wildness 2)  
Claim: Theory says a model trained on P tokens can only use context out to a length that grows as P to the power 1/(2 beta), where beta is the decay exponent of token correlations in the corpus. So sequences longer than that are wasted early in training, and the right length schedule is fixed by a statistic of the data. I predict public training checkpoints show this growth law, and that it is concave, unlike the cubic …  
Prediction: Define the used context at a checkpoint as the shortest window whose held-out loss is within 0.01 nats of the full-window loss. Across Pythia checkpoints from 1,000 to 143,000 steps, used context grows as tokens seen to the power 0.55 plus or minus 0.1 until it nears 2,048, for at least the three …  
Control that could embarrass it: Repeat the Pythia measurement on text with sentences shuffled within documents. Used context should stop growing at sentence length. If it still grows, the measure is picking up something other than use of long-range structure. Also check the smallest Pythia …  
Kill criterion: Fitted exponent below 0.3 or above 0.8 on the larger models, or exponents that differ by more than 0.2 between model sizes in the range where both are data-limited.  
Cost: About 36 checkpoint downloads (roughly 60 GB in total, can be done a few at a time), 10 to 15 hours of M1 Max evaluation, four small training runs of 3 hours.

**For a voice adapter, minutes chosen for prosodic variety are worth several times random minutes, and phone coverage is worth nothing** (speech synthesis (adapter training data); wildness 2)  
Claim: Old speech corpora were scripted for phone and diphone coverage because concatenative systems needed every unit. A modern pretrained speech model already knows every phone. What a voice adapter lacks is how this speaker handles questions, lists, emphasis and long sentences. So selecting training clips for spread in pitch, energy and timing patterns should beat random selection by a wide margin, and selecting for …  
Prediction: From one speaker with 10 or more hours of varied speech, 5 minutes chosen by farthest-point selection in a prosody feature space (pitch contour shape, energy, speaking rate, sentence type) matches 20 minutes chosen at random on pitch-contour distance to that speaker's held-out questions, lists and …  
Control that could embarrass it: Random selection stratified only by clip length. Prosodic selection will pick longer and odder clips; if length-matched random does as well, the gain was length. Also include a flat reader (LJSpeech): there the prosodic rule should give no gain, and if it …

### Lens: dynamical-systems

**A two-number law for how long two machines produce the same tokens, and why temperature zero buys little** (language models (inference, evaluation); wildness 2)  
Claim: The token index at which two runs of the same model first differ follows a constant-hazard law set by two numbers: the size of the logit noise between the two runs and the density of near-ties between the top two logits. The horizon grows in proportion to precision (one more bit doubles it), not with its logarithm as in a chaotic flow. With a shared Gumbel noise stream, sampled decoding at T near 1 has a flip rate …  
Prediction: Predicted median first-divergence index is within a factor of 2 of the measured one on at least three model and precision pairs. Horizon ratio between bf16 and fp16 is between 6 and 10 (precision ratio 8). Seeded Gumbel sampling at T=1 diverges at a rate between 0.5x and 3x the greedy rate.  
Control that could embarrass it: Shuffle which gap goes with which noise value. If the prediction is just as good, the law is trivial. If the measured hazard is not constant in token index (for example it drops as greedy text falls into loops), the constant-hazard form is wrong. Exact ties …  
Kill criterion: Predicted median divergence index is off by more than 2x on two of three pairs, or the bf16 to fp16 horizon ratio is outside 4 to 16.  
Cost: 6 to 10 hours of Mac time, under 10 GB of public checkpoints, no training.

**Coupled Jacobi decoding for speech tokens and for sampled text: speedup is 1/(1-c), and it rises with temperature** (speech synthesis, language models (inference); wildness 2)  
Claim: When an autoregressive model is decoded with a fixed Gumbel noise stream, generation becomes a deterministic fixed-point problem that can be solved in parallel. The number of tokens accepted per parallel pass is 1/(1-c), where c is the chance that a token survives a wrong token just before it. c rises with sampling temperature, so high-entropy models gain most. Autoregressive speech-token models should gain at least …  
Prediction: Orpheus-class or Llasa-class speech-token models at their default temperature: at least 3x fewer forward passes, lossless. A 1B to 3B text model at T=1: at least 1.5x fewer passes, against under 1.1x for greedy Jacobi. Passes saved increase monotonically with T from 0.3 to 1.3 in both. A single …  
Control that could embarrass it: Independent noise per iteration (the original speculative Jacobi scheme) should show the gain vanish. If coupling gives no more than independent sampling on speech, the contraction story is wrong. Also check that output statistics (WER, speaker similarity) …

**Accelerated failure testing: rare errors of a generative model follow an Arrhenius law in sampling temperature** (language models, speech synthesis (evaluation); wildness 3)  
Claim: The rate of noise-caused failures (a wrong digit, broken JSON, a skipped or repeated word in speech) falls with temperature as a sum of exp(-gap/T) terms. So the log of the failure rate is a convex, nearly straight function of 1/T. Failure rates too rare to measure directly at a low temperature can be bounded from below by measuring at high temperature and extrapolating.  
Prediction: After subtracting the greedy failure floor, log failure rate against 1/T is linear with R squared at least 0.97 for T between 0.5 and 1.3. A line fitted on T at or above 0.9 predicts the rate at T=0.5 within a factor of 3, and errs low in every case. With top-p 0.9 the law breaks: the rate falls …  
Control that could embarrass it: Compare with the direct prediction from logits: compute the summed off-path probability along greedy trajectories at each T. If that simple one-pass calculation predicts the failure rate as well as the sampled experiment does, the sampling is unnecessary and …  
Kill criterion: R squared under 0.9, or extrapolation error above 10x at T=0.5 on two of three tasks.  
Cost: About 12 hours of Mac time for text, 12 more for speech. Under 6 GB of checkpoints.

**The critical temperature of a language model sits below 1 and moves toward 1 with model size** (language models (evaluation, sampling); wildness 4)  
Claim: Sampled text has a phase transition in temperature between repetitive and incoherent output, with natural-language-like power-law correlations at the transition. I predict the transition temperature is measurably below 1 for small models and rises toward 1 as models improve. The distance from 1 measures how badly a model holds long-range structure when it runs on its own output, which perplexity does not show.  
Prediction: With a temperature grid of 0.02, the critical temperature of Pythia 70M is at least 0.05 below that of Pythia 2.8B, and the ordering is monotone across five sizes. The critical temperature also falls by at least 0.02 when the same model is perturbed with weight noise that raises perplexity by 5%.  
Control that could embarrass it: Run the same estimator on real text chunks and on text from a Markov model fitted to the data. Real text should sit at the transition and the Markov model should show none. If the estimator's value shifts by 0.05 just from doubling sequence length, a size …  
Kill criterion: Critical temperatures across all sizes agree within 0.02, or the ordering is not monotone.  
Cost: About 30 hours of generation in total, most of it for the two largest models. 15 GB of checkpoints.

(arXiv 2502.14924)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.14924), 2025-02-19 (not read directly)

**One long run or many short ones? The tail of time-to-solution decides test-time compute for recursive reasoning models** (recursive reasoning models (HRM, TRM); wildness 3)  
Claim: In tiny recursive reasoners, the number of recursion steps until the first correct answer, taken over noise seeds for one hard puzzle, is either memoryless or heavy-tailed. If memoryless, depth and parallel width are interchangeable. If heavy-tailed, restart schedules beat both. I predict heavy tails at low noise and a memoryless law at the noise level that works best, and that deterministic convergence time across …  
Prediction: On Sudoku-Extreme puzzles that the deterministic model fails, the survival curve of time-to-first-correct at noise 0.02 has a power-law tail with index below 1. At the best noise level it is exponential (straight on a semi-log plot, R squared above 0.95). A Luby restart schedule reaches the …  
Control that could embarrass it: Shuffle the puzzle labels on rho: if puzzle difficulty measured by a classical solver's backtrack count predicts convergence time just as well, rho adds nothing. If the halting head already picks the right stopping time, restarts have nothing to gain.  
Kill criterion: Run-time distributions are exponential at every noise level and restarts save under 20% of forward passes. That would still be a clean statement that width and depth are interchangeable.  
Cost: Training about 30 to 40 GPU hours on a free tier if needed. Analysis about 10 hours on the Mac. Negligible downloads.

**Shared-noise sampling as a diff tool: two model variants agree in long runs broken by bursts, and paired evaluations get cheaper** (language models, speech and image token models (evaluation); wildness 4)  
Claim: If two model variants sample with the same Gumbel noise, their outputs differ only where their distributions really differ, and after a split they can fall back into agreement. Because a token's influence on later predictions decays as a power law with exponent below 1, I predict the streams never merge permanently: matched runs have power-law lengths. Keying the noise to recent content instead of position should …  
Prediction: For a model and its 4-bit copy at T=1, shared Gumbel noise keyed on the previous 4 tokens gives at least twice the longest-common-subsequence overlap over 512 tokens of position-keyed noise, and both beat independent seeds by a wide margin. Matched-run lengths follow a power law over at least one …  
Control that could embarrass it: Pair a model with itself after forcing one token swap. If that pair does not re-merge either, nothing about model difference is being measured. Content-keyed noise repeats itself on repeated phrases, so check that single-model output quality and diversity are …  
Kill criterion: Content-keyed overlap is under 1.2x position-keyed, or paired variance reduction is under 1.3x.  
Cost: About 10 hours of Mac time, checkpoints already on hand or under 5 GB.

**Is the boundary between outcomes in noise space fractal under strong guidance? The uncertainty exponent of the noise-to-image map** (image generation (diffusion and flow sampling); wildness 5)  
Claim: For a deterministic sampler, shrink the distance between two starting noises by 10x and count how often the two images still land in different outcome classes. A smooth map gives a 10x drop. I predict that under strong classifier-free guidance the drop is much smaller over a range of scales, as it is for systems with fractal basin boundaries. If so, numerical precision buys reproducibility of the image far more …  
Prediction: On a class-conditional ImageNet 256 model with guidance scale 4 or more, alpha is below 0.7 across at least two decades of eps between 1e-4 and 1e-1 (relative to the noise norm). At guidance scale 1, alpha is between 0.9 and 1.1.  
Control that could embarrass it: Replace the sampler with a fixed random linear map followed by the same decoder and labeller. That map is smooth, so alpha must be 1; if the pipeline gives less, the labeller is producing the effect. Also repeat with 200 steps to check the result is not a …  
Kill criterion: Alpha at or above 0.9 at every guidance scale.  
Cost: About 20,000 images, 20 to 30 hours on the Mac. 3 GB of checkpoints.

### Lens: folklore

**Fixed error versus fresh noise: which one makes a model loop** (text (LLM generation); wildness 2)  
Claim: A weight error that is identical at every step raises the chance that a long generation falls into a loop much more than fresh noise of the same size does. Rounded weights are one case of a fixed error, so low-bit models should loop more often than a temperature-shift picture predicts. The subject is the loop, and rounding is only one way to make a fixed error.  
Prediction: On a 0.5B to 1.5B public reasoning model, at temperature 0.6 and 4,000 generated tokens, a fixed Gaussian weight perturbation tuned to mean KLD 0.05 per token raises loop rate by at least 2x over the clean model, while fresh per-step noise tuned to the same KLD changes it by less than 1.2x. Real …  
Control that could embarrass it: The fresh-noise arm matched on KLD. If fresh noise raises loops as much as fixed noise, the time-correlation story is wrong and it is just damage. Also a temperature-matched arm: if raising temperature by the entropy shift of the rounded model removes the …  
Kill criterion: Fixed-error loop rate is within 1.3x of fresh-noise loop rate with non-overlapping 95% intervals not achieved across 5 seeds, or 4-bit models loop no more than clean ones at 4,000 tokens.  
Cost: About 15 to 25 hours of Mac time for 600 prompts x 4 conditions x 5 seeds at 4k tokens on 1.5B; 3 GB of downloads; fits in 32 GB easily.  
Nearest work listed: [Pipis et al., Wait, Wait, Wait... Why Do Reasoning Models Loop?, arXiv 2512.12895](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.12895), 2025-12 (read); [Don't Repeat Yourself: Stopping Verbatim Loops at Sampling Time, arXiv 2608.22761](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22761), 2026-08-24 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv HTML search for loop/repetition plus quantization terms (found the DRY paper; other queries rate-limited or empty); one WebSearch on reasoning-model looping and temperature; GitHub discussion search in llama.cpp and mlx-lm. Semantic …

**A sanity bound for draft acceptance, and whether a draft head must share its target's errors** (text (speculative decoding); wildness 3)  
Claim: Acceptance against a rounded target can fall by at most the rate at which the rounded target changes its own top token. Reports that break this bound point to a broken path or to a drafter that reads the target's internal state. I think separate draft models obey the bound, and heads that read target hidden states are sensitive to the statistics of those states rather than to precision.  
Prediction: Separate draft (Qwen2.5-0.5B for Qwen2.5-7B): teacher-forced top-1 agreement changes by under 2 points from f16 to Q8 target and under 8 points at Q4, always inside the bound. Head-style drafter: the roughly 15-point gap between head formats replicates beyond a seed spread of 2 points, and …  
Control that could embarrass it: The stochastic re-rounding arm. If acceptance collapses when rounding is re-drawn in the same format, then errors really do need to line up one for one and my reading is wrong. Also a full-precision head on a full-precision body as the ceiling.  
Kill criterion: If the head-format gap is under 3 points across 3 prompt sets, the folklore is noise and the idea is closed. If a healthy Q8 target breaks the bound under greedy acceptance, my bound or my understanding of acceptance is wrong.  
Cost: 6 to 10 hours on the Mac; about 12 GB of downloads (7B in a few formats, 0.5B draft, one head).  
Nearest work listed: [QuantSpec, arXiv 2502.10424](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.10424), 2025-02-05 (not read directly); [llama.cpp discussion 27290](https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/discussions/27290), 2026-08-18 (read); [Ruiz y Mesa, TU Dresden master's thesis](https://cfaed.tu-dresden.de/files/Images/people/chair-cc/theses/2510_Ruiz_y_Mesa_MA.pdf), 2025-10 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv HTML search for speculative decoding with quantized draft (found QuantSpec); GitHub discussion search in llama.cpp and exllamav3 (issue 403, title only). No citation-graph search was possible (rate limits).

**Do all those samplers differ in more than two ways** (image (diffusion sampling); wildness 2)  
Claim: At 20 steps or more, the outputs of the many sampler and scheduler pairs for one seed differ along about two axes: how far the result is from the fully converged answer, and how much fresh noise was injected. Most of the visible difference people attribute to a sampler is smaller than the difference between two seeds.  
Prediction: Across 30 sampler-scheduler pairs x 50 prompts x 8 seeds, seed explains at least 85% of the variance in any quality metric and sampler x scheduler under 5%. For same-seed outputs, a two-dimensional embedding explains at least 80% of the variance in pairwise perceptual distance.  
Control that could embarrass it: Repeat at 8 steps, where solvers truly differ; if the two-axis fit holds there equally well, it is an artifact of the distance measure. Also shuffle sampler labels to confirm the 5% is above chance.  
Kill criterion: Sampler x scheduler explains over 15% of metric variance at 20 steps, or a third axis carries over 15% of distance variance and does not match any known quantity.  
Cost: 12,000 images on SD 1.5 at about 4 s each is roughly 14 hours on the Mac; SDXL subset 10 hours more; 12 GB of downloads.  
Nearest work listed: Karras et al. 2022, Elucidating the design space of diffusion models (not read directly); [ComfyUI discussion 17504](https://github.com/Comfy-Org/ComfyUI/discussions/17504), 2026-11-03 (read)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv HTML search for sampler comparison terms returned zero results (likely the query, not absence). GitHub discussion search in ComfyUI. Round-one titles checked: nearby ideas cover noise floors and converged references but not the count …

**Predicting which layers are worth repeating, and what a repeat is worth in thinking tokens** (text (LLM reasoning); wildness 4)  
Claim: Repeating a block of middle layers helps only when that block acts like one step of a repeated refinement. A cheap test on the unmodified model can say in advance which blocks those are. The gain from a repeat can be priced as a number of extra chain-of-thought tokens.  
Prediction: Across all contiguous spans of 1 to 4 layers in a 0.5B to 1.5B model, a statistic computed without any task labels (change in logit-lens agreement with the final output when the span is applied twice) has Spearman correlation at least 0.5 with measured accuracy change on 500-item tasks. The best …  
Control that could embarrass it: Deleting the same span and repeating a random span of equal length. If random repeats help as often as predicted ones, or if the gain vanishes at 500 items, the folklore was small-sample noise.  
Kill criterion: No span gains more than 2 standard errors (about 4 points at n=500) on any task, or correlation under 0.2.  
Cost: 80 spans x 1,500 items x 2 models at short outputs: about 10 hours on the Mac; 4 GB of downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [Eyal, Dershowitz, Bar. Layer Duplication in LLMs. Findings of EMNLP 2025](https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-emnlp.967/), 2025-11 (not read directly); [Show HN: llm-circuit-finder](https://hn.nuxt.dev/item/47431671), 2026-03 (read)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch on layer duplication; WebFetch of the ACL Anthology page and the HN thread; one long arXiv HTML query returned zero results.

**Repetition penalty as a speaking-rate knob in codec-token speech models** (speech (TTS); wildness 4)  
Claim: In speech models that write audio as codec tokens, a held sound is a run of similar coarse tokens. A repetition penalty makes such runs less likely, so speech gets faster. If so, a penalty applied only to the coarse codebook is a free speed control.  
Prediction: Syllables per second rises monotonically and by at least 8% as the penalty goes from 1.0 to 1.3 at fixed temperature. A penalty on the coarsest codebook positions alone gives at least 80% of that change with at most half the rise in word error rate.  
Control that could embarrass it: Raise temperature alone to match the entropy change the penalty causes; if speed rises the same, it is randomness and not repetition. Also penalise a random set of token ids of equal size.  
Kill criterion: Speed change under 3%, or not monotonic, or word error rate more than doubles before any speed change appears.  
Cost: About 6 hours on the Mac; 4 GB download; aligner and ASR already small.  
Nearest work listed: [Orpheus-TTS README (canopyai)](https://github.com/canopyai/Orpheus-TTS) (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch on Orpheus repetition penalty and speed; arXiv HTML search on TTS sampling and speaking rate returned zero results; gh issue search in canopyai/Orpheus-TTS (titles only).

**Is the standard negative prompt a placebo** (image (diffusion guidance); wildness 2)  
Claim: Most of the benefit people credit to long boilerplate negative prompts comes from having any non-empty negative at all, not from the words in it. A random sentence of the same length should do nearly as well.  
Prediction: In blind pairs at the same seed, boilerplate negatives beat length-matched random-sentence negatives in no more than 55% of pairs on a preference metric and on a 200-pair check by eye, while either beats an empty negative in at least 60%.  
Control that could embarrass it: The targeted-negative arm must visibly remove its object; if it does not, the setup is wrong. If boilerplate wins 65% or more against random text, the folklore is right and the idea is dead.  
Kill criterion: Boilerplate beats random-sentence negatives in at least 65% of pairs on both the metric and the human check.  
Cost: 1,600 images per model, about 3 hours for SD 1.5 and 6 for SDXL on the Mac; one hour of Rob's time for rating.  
Nearest work listed: [Understanding the Impact of Negative Prompts, arXiv 2406.02965](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.02965), 2024-06-05 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv HTML search for negative prompt (found 2406.02965); gh issue search in Comfy-Org/ComfyUI. No forum search (Reddit, Civitai) was done.

### Lens: future-work

**A token is decided when the signal pays for its surprisal: a commit-time law for continuous diffusion language models** (language (continuous diffusion / flow); wildness 3)  
Claim: In a continuous diffusion language model, each token's identity becomes decided at the noise level where the signal-to-noise term r^2/(2 sigma^2) equals that token's surprisal given the tokens already decided. If so, the model's cross-entropy as a function of noise level can be predicted from the histogram of token surprisals in the corpus, with no fitting to the diffusion model itself.  
Prediction: Per-token commit noise level (where the model's posterior on the true token first passes 0.5) has Spearman correlation at least 0.6 with the token's surprisal under a masked or autoregressive LM of similar size; and the cross-entropy-versus-noise curve predicted from the surprisal histogram alone …  
Control that could embarrass it: Predict commit level from token frequency alone (unigram surprisal) and from embedding norm alone. If either does as well as contextual surprisal, the law adds nothing beyond what schedule papers already assume. Also compare against the nearest-neighbour …  
Kill criterion: Spearman below 0.3 between commit level and contextual surprisal, or the unigram control within 0.05 of it.  
Cost: About 6 to 10 hours of machine time, one checkpoint download of 2 to 5 GB, a few GB of text. No training.  
Nearest work listed: [RePlaid, Continuous Diffusion Scales Competitively (arXiv 2605.18530)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18530), 2026-05 (read); [Language Modeling with Hyperspherical Flows (arXiv 2605.11125)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11125), 2026-05 (read); [On the Design Space of Continuous Diffusion Language Models (arXiv 2610.13834)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13834), 2026-10-09 (read); [Token Time Continuous Diffusion for Language Modeling (arXiv 2607.14106)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14106), 2026-07 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv search pages (abstract search: continuous diffusion language commit, entropy schedule); one web search on commit time, surprisal and speciation in continuous diffusion LMs. Found per-token time and entropy-rate schedule papers, no …

**One cause for Muon's side effects: a shared mean direction in the hidden states** (language (optimizers); wildness 3)  
Claim: Two separate late-2026 findings about Muon, that it fine-tunes worse than AdamW below a pretraining-data threshold and that it overloads the first mixture-of-experts router, come from the same thing: Muon-trained backbones put more of every hidden state into one shared direction. Removing that mean during pretraining should restore AdamW-level output margins and fine-tuning behaviour in small models.  
Prediction: At 30M to 60M parameters, the share of final-layer hidden-state energy in the mean vector is at least 2 times higher under Muon than AdamW at matched loss. Adding a parameter-free centering step before the unembedding during Muon pretraining closes at least 50% of the margin-to-spread gap and at …  
Control that could embarrass it: Apply the same centering to AdamW models: if it helps AdamW as much, the effect is not about Muon. Remove a random direction of equal norm instead of the mean: if that helps equally, the mean is not special.  
Kill criterion: Mean-direction energy differs by less than 1.2 times between optimizers at matched loss, or centering closes less than 20% of the fine-tuning gap.  
Cost: Roughly 40 to 60 free-tier GPU hours spread over three weeks of quotas; data under 10 GB; no model downloads unless OLMo-60M is used.  
Nearest work listed: [Pretraining Data Scale Reverses the Muon-AdamW Fine-Tuning Gap (arXiv 2611.03633)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03633), 2026-11-04 (read); [Muon Makes the First MoE Layer Harder to Balance (arXiv 2610.37914)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37914), 2026-10-27 (read); [Tune the Baseline First (arXiv 2611.02529)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02529), 2026-11 (read); [The Loss Does Not See the Basis, but Adam Does (arXiv 2608.05136)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05136), 2026-08 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv search pages for Muon titles and abstracts from the last six months; margin-to-spread searched by name with no hit beyond the source paper. No web search spent on this one.

**An error bar from one training run** (language (training methodology), likely general; wildness 2)  
Claim: The seed-to-seed spread of validation loss can be estimated from a single run by looking at how much validation loss wobbles around its trend while the learning rate is held constant. If the estimate is within a factor of 1.5 of the true spread across many configurations, a single expensive run can carry an honest error bar.  
Prediction: Across at least 8 configurations (two model sizes between 10M and 124M, two optimizers, two batch sizes), the single-run estimate is within a factor of 1.5 of the measured 5-seed standard deviation in at least 7 of 8, and data-order-only variation accounts for at least 80% of total seed variance.  
Control that could embarrass it: A bootstrap over validation examples from one checkpoint, which is what most papers already report. If that matches seed spread as well as the trajectory estimate, the new estimator is unnecessary. Also check after learning-rate decay: if the ratio between …  
Kill criterion: Estimate off by more than a factor of 2 in 3 or more of 8 configurations, or initialisation contributes more than 50% of variance.  
Cost: About 60 runs of 30 to 60 minutes: 40 to 60 free-tier GPU hours. Data under 5 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [On the Variance of Neural Network Training with respect to Test Sets and Distributions, Keller Jordan (arXiv …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.01910), 2023 (not read directly); [Quantifying Variance in Evaluation Benchmarks (arXiv 2406.10229)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.10229), 2024-06 (not read directly); [Fine-Tuning Pretrained Language Models: Weight Initializations, Data Orders, and Early Stopping (arXiv …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.06305), 2020-02 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv search pages (seed variance single run, run-to-run variance pretraining); one web search. The web search summary itself asserted a single run cannot give seed variance and found no paper that tests a trajectory-based estimator.

**Long shot: camera drift in self-rollout video distillation is a broken symmetry, fixed per checkpoint** (video (autoregressive distilled diffusion); wildness 4)  
Claim: The slow one-way camera slide that appears after long self-rollout distillation is not copied from the data or the teacher. It is a direction the student falls into during training, so it is the same for every prompt within one checkpoint and differs between independently trained checkpoints.  
Prediction: Within one public checkpoint, the sign of mean horizontal optical flow over seconds 15 to 30 agrees in at least 80% of 24 prompt-seed pairs (chance is 50%). The bidirectional teacher on the same prompts agrees in at most 60%. Among at least 3 independently trained student checkpoints, not all share …  
Control that could embarrass it: If every checkpoint and the teacher drift the same way, the cause is a data prior (for example a common pan direction in training video), and the symmetry story is wrong. If mirroring the noise flips the direction, the drift is content-driven, not a weight …  
Kill criterion: Within-checkpoint sign agreement under 65%, or all students and the teacher share one direction.  
Cost: About 15 to 25 free-tier GPU hours for three checkpoints (my speed estimate for a T4 is a guess); 3 to 6 GB per checkpoint.  
Nearest work listed: [Causal-rCM (arXiv 2606.25473)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25473), 2026-06-24 (read); [Self-Aligned Forcing (arXiv 2609.38114)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38114), 2026-09-29 (read); [Self Gradient Forcing (arXiv 2607.20368)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20368), 2026-07 (read); BAgger (arXiv 2512.12080) and Wave Forcing (arXiv 2611.00859) (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv search pages (autoregressive video drift, camera drift, self forcing) for 2026. No web search spent. Found drift-reduction methods, no measurement of drift direction across checkpoints.

**Long shot: recursion in tiny recursive models is a training signal, not inference depth** (reasoning models / language; wildness 4)  
Claim: The gain from running a small network many times on its own state comes from training: the extra passes show the network its own partly-solved states and ask it to improve them. A plain network trained on a replay buffer of its own earlier states, with one pass per step, should recover most of the gain, and a recursively trained network should lose little when recursion is cut at test time.  
Prediction: On Sudoku-Extreme, a single-pass 2-layer network trained with replayed own-states reaches at least 80% of TRM's accuracy gain over the non-recursive baseline. A standard TRM evaluated with one third of its training recursions loses under 3 accuracy points.  
Control that could embarrass it: A replay buffer filled with randomly corrupted solutions instead of the model's own states. If that does as well, the benefit is denoising practice, not self-improvement. And a non-recursive model given the same total gradient steps as the replay arm.  
Kill criterion: Replay arm recovers under 40% of the gain, or cutting test-time recursion to one third costs more than 10 points.  
Cost: Four arms at 10 to 30 GPU hours each on a T4-class card (the paper's own training time is from memory and unverified): 2 to 4 weeks of free quota. Data under 1 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [Less is More: Recursive Reasoning with Tiny Networks (arXiv 2510.04871)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.04871), 2025-10 (read); [Attractor Models (arXiv 2605.12466)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12466), 2026-05-12 (read); [Tiny Recursive Models on ARC-AGI-1 (arXiv 2512.11847)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.11847), 2026-01-08 (read); [ARC Prize analysis of HRM, and arXiv 2511.08653 (depth curriculum for TRM)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08653) (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv search pages (tiny recursive model, equilibrium internalization, recursion ablation); one web search on TRM ablations, matched-compute baselines and a self-distillation explanation. The search found deep-supervision ablations and no …

**Whisper's own 2022 open question: how much of its robustness lives in the encoder** (speech recognition; wildness 1)  
Claim: Most of Whisper's robustness to unfamiliar audio is in the encoder. A small CTC head trained on clean read speech on top of the frozen encoder should keep most of the robustness, even though it has no language-model decoder.  
Prediction: A frozen whisper-small encoder with a linear or 2-layer CTC head trained on LibriSpeech 100h keeps at least 70% of Whisper's effective-robustness advantage, measured as the average WER gap on 3 out-of-domain sets to a LibriSpeech-trained wav2vec2 model at matched LibriSpeech test-clean WER.  
Control that could embarrass it: The same CTC head on frozen wav2vec2 or HuBERT features. If it is as robust as the Whisper-encoder head, the robustness is not specific to Whisper's encoder. Also add a 4-gram language model to the CTC output: if that closes the remaining gap, the decoder's …  
Kill criterion: Less than 40% of the advantage retained, which would say the decoder carries most of it.  
Cost: About 6 to 10 hours on the Mac Studio; downloads about 6 GB LibriSpeech 100h, 1 GB of models, 5 to 10 GB of evaluation audio.

(arXiv 2604.21276)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21276), 2026-04 (not read directly)

**Doubled words in few-step text diffusion are predictable from one forward pass** (language (discrete diffusion); wildness 2)  
Claim: The rate of doubled words at low step counts in a masked text diffusion model can be computed in advance: it is the chance that two neighbouring positions revealed in the same step both draw the same token from their separate marginals. The same uncertainty about position should also produce dropped words, so doublings and omissions should rise and fall together.  
Prediction: Across step counts 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, the predicted adjacent-duplicate rate (sum over same-step neighbour pairs of the overlap of their marginals) is within 25% of the observed rate. Per sequence, duplicate count and omission count correlate at 0.5 or higher on a conditional task with a known …  
Control that could embarrass it: An autoregressive model of similar size at the same temperature gives the natural repeat rate of the text; subtract it. Re-run with an unmasking order that never reveals neighbours in the same step: if doublings do not fall to the autoregressive baseline, the …  
Kill criterion: Predicted and observed rates differ by more than a factor of 2 at two or more step counts, or duplicate-omission correlation under 0.2.  
Cost: 4 to 8 hours on the Mac Studio; one model download under 1 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [DiffusionGemma Technical Report (arXiv 2608.00146)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00146), 2026-07-31 (read); [Parallel Decoding Distillation (arXiv 2607.26004)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26004), 2026-07 (read); Non-Autoregressive Neural Machine Translation, Gu et al. 2018 (not read directly)

### Lens: human-in-loop

**Time-to-tell: how many minutes of listening before a TTS voice stops passing, and whether repeated sentence melodies explain it** (speech synthesis (long-form); wildness 3)  
Claim: A listener's ability to tell a modern TTS reading from a human reading is near chance on single sentences but rises steeply with exposure to the same system, because the system reuses a small set of sentence-level pitch and timing patterns that the listener learns. The speed of that learning differs between systems and is predicted by how predictable each sentence's pitch contour is from the previous few sentences. …  
Prediction: For at least 3 of 4 open TTS systems, blinded human-versus-TTS 2AFC accuracy on 8-second excerpts rises from under 65% with no prior exposure to over 85% after 6 minutes of exposure to that system, while accuracy for a held-out human reader pair rises by under 5 points. Across systems, the minutes …  
Control that could embarrass it: Run the same exposure schedule with a second human reader as the 'system'. If accuracy at telling that reader from another human rises just as fast, the effect is voice familiarity, not learned machine patterns. Also flatten F0 variety artificially in a human …  
Kill criterion: Accuracy rises by less than 10 points between 0 and 6 minutes for all systems, or the human-reader control rises as much as the TTS arm.  
Cost: About 3 hours of synthesis, 8 to 10 hours of listening over two weeks, no GPU beyond MPS, model downloads 1 to 6 GB each, LibriVox audio under 1 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [SwanBench-Speech, Comprehensive Benchmarking of Long-Form Speech Generation in Diverse Scenarios, …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28618), 2026-05-27 (read); [Measuring Prosody Diversity in Zero-Shot TTS (DS-WED, ProsodyEval), arXiv:2509.19928](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19928), 2025-09 (not read directly); [Rethinking MUSHRA, arXiv:2411.12719](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.12719), 2024-11-19 (read)

**The preferred sampling temperature of a TTS model rises with passage length** (speech synthesis; wildness 2)  
Claim: The sampling temperature (or guidance strength) a listener prefers is not a constant of the model. For single sentences the preferred setting is low, because variation reads as error. For multi-minute passages the preferred setting is higher, because lack of variation reads as monotony. Model defaults are tuned on single sentences with WER and speaker similarity, so they are wrong for long-form use.  
Prediction: In at least 2 of 3 autoregressive or flow TTS models, the temperature at which a blinded listener prefers the output in 2AFC against the default is at least 0.15 higher for 3-minute passages than for single sentences, and at the long-form-preferred setting sentence-level WER is worse by at least …  
Control that could embarrass it: Present the 3-minute passages as shuffled single sentences drawn from them. If the higher setting is still preferred, the effect is not about length or context but about those texts. Also check that the preferred setting does not simply track loudness or …  
Kill criterion: Preferred setting differs by less than 0.05 between sentence and passage conditions in all three models, or test-retest on a second day flips the direction.  
Cost: 4 to 6 hours of synthesis, 6 hours of listening, downloads 2 to 6 GB per model.  
Nearest work listed: [Investigating Stochastic Methods for Prosody Modeling in Speech Synthesis, arXiv:2507.00227](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.00227), 2025-06-30 (read); [Towards Responsible Evaluation for Text-to-Speech, arXiv:2510.06927](https://arxiv.org/html/2510.06927v1), 2025-10-08 (read)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API query (TTS and temperature and sampling and prosody/expressive) returned two papers, neither on length dependence. Not searched: vendor documentation or forum threads where users may already recommend higher temperature for …

**Slow voice drift is inaudible in continuous listening and obvious in first-versus-last comparison, so drift metrics need a rate, not a distance** (speech synthesis, voice identity; wildness 3)  
Claim: Whether a listener notices that a voice has changed depends on how fast it changed, not on how far. A total change that is easy to hear when the first and last 10 seconds are played back to back goes unnoticed when it is spread over minutes. Current drift metrics are averages of embedding cosine over windows, which have no notion of rate and no known perceptual threshold.  
Prediction: A linear drift between two voices whose endpoints a listener discriminates at 90% or better in direct A/B is detected in under 35% of continuous-listening trials when spread over 3 minutes, and in over 80% when spread over 15 seconds. The 75% detection threshold, expressed as embedding cosine …  
Control that could embarrass it: Real recordings of one reader across a long session have natural drift (fatigue, mic distance). Measure the embedding drift rate there. If natural drift rates already exceed the rates Rob fails to hear in TTS, the claimed threshold is not about machines at …  
Kill criterion: Continuous-listening detection is within 15 points of first-versus-last detection at every duration, or thresholds in rate units vary as much as thresholds in total-distance units.  
Cost: 3 hours synthesis, 8 hours listening, one TTS model and two speaker encoders (under 4 GB total).  
Nearest work listed: [A Novel Automatic Framework for Speaker Drift Detection in Synthesized Speech, arXiv:2604.06327](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06327), 2026-04-07 (read); [SwanBench-Speech, arXiv:2605.28618](https://arxiv.org/html/2605.28618), 2026-05-27 (read); [MagpieTTS-LF, arXiv:2606.18485](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18485), 2026-06-16 (not read directly)

**Batch sameness in image models is sameness of layout: a blur test against embedding diversity scores** (image generation; wildness 2)  
Claim: When people say a model's images for one prompt all look the same, what they are seeing is the same coarse layout: subject position, scale, camera angle, horizon. Diversity scores built on semantic embeddings mostly measure attribute and texture variety and so miss it. A score computed on heavily blurred images should track human judgements of batch variety better than Vendi on DINOv2 or CLIP.  
Prediction: On pairs of 8-image grids for the same prompt, a layout-variance score (variance across the grid of 16x16 downsampled luminance, plus subject-box position and scale) picks the grid the viewer calls more varied in at least 80% of pairs matched on Vendi score, while Vendi on DINOv2 picks it in at …  
Control that could embarrass it: Pairs matched on both layout variance and Vendi but differing in a named attribute count, built as in the DeepMind study. If the viewer still reliably picks one, there is a third factor. Also a real-photo baseline: grids of photographs for the same captions …  
Kill criterion: Layout variance picks the human choice in under 65% of Vendi-matched pairs, or the blur-to-16x16 ranking correlates under 0.5 with the unblurred ranking.  
Cost: 0 to 10 GPU-hours on free tiers for generation, 5 hours of viewing, embedding models under 2 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [Benchmarking Diversity in Image Generation via Attribute-Conditional Human Evaluation, arXiv:2511.10547](https://arxiv.org/html/2511.10547), 2025-11-13 (read); [FoMo: Forking Moment in Generative Trajectory as a Perceptual Distance, arXiv:2609.25716](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25716), 2026-09 (not read directly)

**Can a viewer tell which way time runs in a generated video? Forward-versus-backward accuracy as a test of whether anything happens** (video generation; wildness 4)  
Claim: People tell forward from reversed playback of real video with high accuracy when the clip contains irreversible events. Generated video is full of motion that looks the same in both directions: drift, sway, slow pans. So a viewer's forward-or-backward accuracy on generated clips should be well below that on real clips for the same captions, and that gap measures something no clip-level quality metric does: whether …  
Prediction: Blinded forward-or-backward accuracy for one trained viewer is 88% or higher on real clips and 70% or lower on clips from open text-to-video models for the same captions (100 clips per arm separates these at z above 3). At least 40% of generated clips are judged with under 60% consistency across …  
Control that could embarrass it: Match motion magnitude: pick real clips whose optical-flow energy matches the generated clips. If real-clip accuracy falls to the generated level once motion is matched, the result is only 'generated video moves less', which is already known. Also include …  
Kill criterion: Generated-clip accuracy is within 8 points of real-clip accuracy after motion matching, or within 8 points before matching.  
Cost: 0 to 15 free-tier GPU-hours if clips must be generated, 3 hours of viewing over two sessions, dataset subset under 5 GB.

**Words-to-detection: the excerpt length at which a trained reader spots machine prose, as a per-model threshold** (language models, prose; wildness 2)  
Claim: Detection of machine prose by a practised reader follows a psychometric curve in excerpt length, and the length at 75% correct is a stable property of a model and prompt style. That threshold is a human-anchored measure of how machine-like a model's writing is, and it should move much less under 'write like a human' prompting than automatic detector scores do.  
Prediction: For default-prompted outputs of four current models, one trained reader reaches 75% correct in a blinded yes/no task at under 80 words; d-prime grows with the square root of word count to within 20% between 25 and 400 words; and under a style-evasion prompt the 75% threshold rises by less than a …  
Control that could embarrass it: Topic and era leakage: the reader may be detecting the pre-2020 date or source, not the author. Control by having models rewrite human excerpts lightly and humans (from published human-edited sets) rewrite machine ones, and by including post-2023 human text …  
Kill criterion: Day-to-day test-retest thresholds differ by more than a factor of 1.5, or the curve is flat (under 60% correct even at 400 words) for all four models.  
Cost: 2 hours of generation, 12 hours of reading spread over weeks, no downloads beyond a small detector model.

**Is the tell in the words or in the shape? Detecting machine prose from punctuation and sentence lengths alone** (language models, prose; wildness 4)  
Claim: A practised reader can tell machine prose from human prose with all the words removed, seeing only the sequence of sentence lengths, punctuation marks and paragraph breaks. If so, the feel of machine writing is carried largely by rhythm and paragraph shape, and vocabulary lists like 'delve' are a symptom. Removing the vocabulary cue should cost the reader little; replacing the shape should cost a lot.  
Prediction: On 300-word excerpts shown as a skeleton (each word replaced by a dash of matching length class, punctuation and breaks kept), one trained reader reaches d-prime of 1.0 or more (about 76% correct in 2AFC), and a logistic model on 12 hand-built skeleton features reaches AUC 0.85 or more. In a …  
Control that could embarrass it: Genre confound: human and machine excerpts may differ in skeleton because of genre mix, not authorship. Balance by genre and also test human-versus-human across genres; if skeleton-only accuracy is as high there, the skeleton cue is genre. Also check the …  
Kill criterion: Skeleton-only d-prime under 0.4 on both days, or the vocabulary knockout hurts detection as much as or more than the shape knockout.  
Cost: 3 hours of scripted stimulus building, 8 hours of reading, one small local model already on the machine.

**One trained listener against the crowd: how many minutes of listening separate two near-parity TTS systems** (evaluation method (speech, extendable to image); wildness 1)  
Claim: Most disagreement between raters in MOS tests is disagreement about how to use the scale, not about what they hear. So a single trained listener doing blinded paired comparisons with repeats can separate near-parity systems in far fewer listening minutes than a crowd MOS test, and the within-rater orderings hidden in existing per-rater MOS data will already show this before any new listening is done.  
Prediction: Re-analysis first: in a public dataset with per-rater scores, for system pairs whose MOS differ by under 0.2, the within-rater sign of the difference agrees with the eventual large-sample ordering in at least 70% of rater-pair observations, although the standard MOS test at 20 ratings per system …  
Control that could embarrass it: The single listener may be sensitive but idiosyncratic. Check that his ordering on pairs the crowd does separate (MOS gap above 0.5) agrees with the crowd in at least 90% of cases; if not, he is measuring personal taste. Recruit two other volunteers for a …  
Kill criterion: Within-rater orderings in the public data are no more consistent than between-rater orderings, or the single listener's day-1 and day-2 preferences on the same pairs agree under 75% of the time.  
Cost: Part 1: half a day, dataset under 10 GB. Part 2: 8 to 10 hours of listening.  
Nearest work listed: [Is there a relationship between Mean Opinion Score (MOS) and Just Noticeable Difference (JND)?, …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17010), 2026-02-19 (read); [Towards Responsible Evaluation for Text-to-Speech, arXiv:2510.06927](https://arxiv.org/html/2510.06927v1), 2025-10-08 (read); [TTSDS2, arXiv:2506.19441](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.19441), 2025-06-24 (read); [Multi-Dimensional Prosody Judgment For Live Streaming Speech Synthesis, arXiv:2609.20124](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20124), 2026-09-17 (read)

**Regret slope as a meter: does an in-context learner pay the singular Bayes price or the ridge price?** (language models / in-context learning (small transformers …; wildness 3)  
Claim: A transformer trained to do in-context regression on low-rank linear maps has a cumulative excess loss that grows as c times log n, and c identifies which algorithm it runs. For 8-in, 8-out maps with model rank 4 and true rank 2, theory gives c = 32 for ridge on the full matrix, 24 for a regular rank-4 estimator, 19.5 for the singular Bayes predictor, and 14 for a learner that also infers the rank. I predict a …  
Prediction: For M=N=8, H=4, true rank 2, unit-variance Gaussian noise: n times the per-example excess loss, averaged over n in 200 to 800, is 19.5 plus or minus 2 nats for a 6-layer transformer trained on a Gaussian-factor prior, and 14 plus or minus 2 when trained on a prior that mixes ranks 0 to 4. A ridge …  
Control that could embarrass it: The MCMC Bayes reference must itself reproduce 19.5 at the context lengths used. If it gives 23 at n = 800 because the asymptotics have not set in, the table is useless at feasible n and the experiment needs smaller dimensions. Also train the same transformer …  
Kill criterion: The transformer's slope on rank-2 tasks is within 2 of the ridge value 32, or the slope has not flattened by n = 800 so no coefficient can be read.  
Cost: About 10 to 20 GPU-hours on the M1 Max for training and evaluation, under 8 GB memory, no downloads for stage one. Stage two needs one 0.6 to 1.7B checkpoint (1 to 4 GB).

**The squared-error-optimal guidance weight: two exact regression identities every denoiser should satisfy, and where they fail** (image generation (diffusion and flow models); extends to …; wildness 3)  
Claim: A denoiser trained with squared error should be a conditional mean. Then the best linear mix of its conditional and unconditional outputs for predicting the true target puts weight exactly 1 on the conditional output, and the best scalar gain on the output is exactly 1. I predict that public text-to-image models fail the first identity in the middle noise levels, with an optimal weight above 1, and that the excess …  
Prediction: On about 300 held-out captioned images, w* is between 1.05 and 1.5 for at least a third of the noise range in SD 1.5, SDXL and one flow model with a true CFG path, and w* with dense VLM captions exceeds w* with original alt-text by at least 0.1. The noise range holding 80% of the prompt's mutual …  
Control that could embarrass it: Shuffle the captions across images: w* must drop to about 0. Train a tiny class-conditional diffusion model on a Gaussian mixture to convergence: w* must be 1.00 plus or minus 0.02. If the toy does not return 1, the estimator is biased. Also use images that …  
Kill criterion: w* within 1 plus or minus 0.03 at every noise level for all three models and both caption types. That would be a clean negative: the benefit of guidance has no squared-error component.  
Cost: About 30,000 forward passes per model; 3 to 8 hours per model on the M1 Max; 2 to 7 GB checkpoints; 300 images.

**How much of a language model's missing knowledge is noise? Variance share from the Pythagorean decomposition** (language model training (small models from scratch); wildness 2)  
Claim: Classical theory says the excess loss of a model trained on n samples is estimation variance and falls as 1/n. There is an exact identity that measures the variance part directly. I predict that in one-pass language model training, variance is under a quarter of what more data buys, and that the same models trained for many epochs on the same shards flip to mostly variance.  
Prediction: For a roughly 30M-parameter GPT trained one pass on shards of 25M and 100M tokens, variance divided by [L(n) - L(8n)] is below 0.25. Trained to the best early-stopped loss over many epochs on the same shards, the ratio is above 0.6. The top 10% of token positions hold more than half the variance.  
Control that could embarrass it: Same shard, 8 different seeds: this isolates seed variance from data variance. If seed variance alone equals shard variance, the decomposition says nothing about data. Also run a model class where theory must hold (a bigram or small n-gram model with …  
Kill criterion: Variance share above 0.6 in the one-pass regime at both n. Then language model pretraining is estimation-limited after all, the classical 1/n picture applies better than people think, and the interesting follow-up is why learning curves are not 1/n.  
Cost: 18 small training runs of 0.5 to 3 hours each on the M1 Max for the 25M-token arm; the 100M arm is about 4 times that and fits free cloud tiers. No model downloads; one public corpus of a few GB.  
Nearest work listed: [Yang et al., Rethinking Bias-Variance Trade-off for Generalization of Neural Networks (arXiv 2002.11328)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.11328), 2020-02 (not read directly); [Nakkiran et al., The Deep Bootstrap Framework (arXiv 2010.08127)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2010.08127), 2020-10 (not read directly); [Has LLM Reached the Scaling Ceiling Yet? (arXiv 2412.16443)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.16443), 2024-12 (read); Heskes, Bias/variance decompositions for likelihood-based estimators (Neural Computation 1998) (not read directly)

**Can a carried summary beat a sliding window? Context memory predicted from the model's own loss-by-position curve** (language model inference (long context); wildness 4)  
Claim: The bits of a context that matter for ordinary continuation equal the area between the model's loss-by-position curve and its floor. If that area grows as N to a power below 1, a summary whose size grows with the same power can replace the context for continuation. I predict the power is clearly below 1 and that optimised summary vectors follow it.

The number of optimised memory vectors needed to keep continuation loss within 0.05 nats per token of the full context grows with an exponent within 0.15 of that beta. Going from 2k …  
Control that could embarrass it: A sliding window of the last w tokens at equal memory: if the window does as well as the summary at every budget, summaries buy nothing and the suffix-only bound in 2605.25085 is the whole story. Also a wrong-document summary (must give no gain), and an …  
Kill criterion: beta above 0.9, as the 1/d persistence law in 2607.25184 could imply, or required vectors growing linearly in N while beta is below 0.8.  
Cost: Step one: 2 hours. Step two: about 40 x 4 documents x 5 sizes x a few hundred optimisation steps on short sequences, roughly 2 to 4 days on the M1 Max. One 1 to 4 GB checkpoint; under 16 GB memory if …

**Is one stored bit per learned bit achievable? Testing the adapter-size floor that Excess Description Length implies** (language model fine-tuning; applies to image and speech …; wildness 2)  
Claim: Excess Description Length measures, from the online loss curve, how many bits a fine-tune absorbed. The EDL authors report that LoRA degrades at 0.01 to 1 absorbed bit per parameter, which means LoRA stores roughly 16 to 1600 bits per bit learned. I predict that this is waste in the parameterisation and that a random-subspace adapter with coded coordinates gets within 4 times of one stored bit per learned bit.  
Prediction: On a 500-word cipher task, EDL from one-pass training is within a factor 2 of 3767 bits, and the smallest random-subspace adapter that keeps 95% of the held-out gain stores fewer than 15,000 bits (under 2 kB). For two natural tasks, minimal stored bits divided by EDL is between 1 and 4, against …  
Control that could embarrass it: The same subspace method on a task with random labels: EDL should be near zero and the adapter should not help at any size. If the cipher's EDL comes out at 30,000 bits instead of about 4,000, the online learner is so far from Bayes-efficient that EDL is not …  
Kill criterion: Minimal stored bits exceed 30 times EDL on all three tasks even after sweeping subspace seeds and coordinate precision.  
Cost: About 3 tasks x 8 subspace sizes x 3 seeds short fine-tunes, 1 to 2 days on the M1 Max; one 0.4 to 1.2 GB checkpoint.

**Grading optimisers against the Bayes bound: an absolute efficiency score on tasks where the bound is known** (training (optimisers), small models from scratch; wildness 4)  
Claim: On tasks where the learning coefficient lambda is known in closed form, the best possible excess loss after n samples is lambda/n. So an optimiser can be given an absolute score, lambda divided by (n times its excess loss), instead of being compared only with other optimisers. I predict common one-pass recipes without weight averaging score below 0.5 late in training, and that averaging closes most of the gap.  
Prediction: For the 8x8, rank-4 student, rank-2 teacher task (lambda = 19.5): Adam and SGD with momentum, one pass, no averaging, give n times excess loss above 40 at n = 1e5 (efficiency below 0.5). With a tuned exponential moving average the value is between 19.5 and 26. Muon-style orthogonalised updates rank …  
Control that could embarrass it: A full-rank teacher (regular case, lambda = d/2): averaged SGD must score near 1.0 there, since that is a theorem. If it does not, the harness is wrong. Also an MCMC posterior-mean predictor must score 1.0 on the singular task.  
Kill criterion: All tuned optimisers land within 15% of each other and within 15% of the bound, with or without averaging. Then the score has no resolving power on these tasks.  
Cost: A few hundred small runs, each seconds to minutes; under a day of CPU time. No downloads.

**A train-test gap without a test set: functional variance from a short Langevin chain, per example** (fine-tuning (language, also speech and image adapters); wildness 4)  
Claim: Watanabe's WAIC theorem says generalisation loss equals training loss plus the average, over training examples, of how much each example's log-likelihood varies across the posterior. I predict that a short noisy-gradient chain around a fine-tuned model gives a usable estimate of the train-test gap, and that the examples with the highest variance are the ones the model has memorised.  
Prediction: Across 12 fine-tunes of a 360M model (4 dataset sizes from 500 to 8000 examples, 3 epoch counts), the estimated gap correlates with the true gap at Spearman 0.8 or higher and is within a factor 1.5 in at least 8 of 12. Removing the 5% highest-variance examples and retraining shrinks the gap at …  
Control that could embarrass it: Use the plain variance of per-example loss across the last 60 ordinary training checkpoints instead of a Langevin chain. If that does as well, the theory adds nothing over dataset-cartography heuristics. Also compare with the trivial predictor 'gap is …  
Kill criterion: Spearman below 0.5, or the trivial predictor matches it.  
Cost: 12 fine-tunes plus chains, about 1 to 2 days on the M1 Max; one 0.7 GB checkpoint.  
Nearest work listed: [Okuno and Yano, A Generalization Gap Estimation for Overparameterized Models via the Langevin Functional …](https://doi.org/10.1080/10618600.2023.2197488), 2023-04 (not read directly); Watanabe, Asymptotic equivalence of Bayes cross validation and WAIC in singular learning theory (JMLR 2010) (not read directly); Lau et al., The Local Learning Coefficient (2023) (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: OpenAlex search 'WAIC deep neural network stochastic gradient Langevin generalization gap estimate without validation' (found Okuno and Yano). Hugging Face papers search on the same terms (nothing relevant). No full texts read.

### Lens: learning-rules-beyond-pretraining

**How long does a rare exploit stay hidden? Onset time against base rate has exponent 1 under SGD and about 1/2 under Adam, with a knee at one hit per group** (language models, post-training (RL with verifiable or …; wildness 2)  
Claim: When RL rewards a behaviour the base model produces with small probability p0 (a planted keyword, a grader exploit), the number of steps until the behaviour takes over follows a power law in p0 whose exponent is set by the optimizer. Plain SGD gives steps proportional to 1/p0. Adam gives roughly 1/sqrt(p0 times group size times batch), because it divides a rare, spiky gradient by its own root mean square. Above …  
Prediction: On a 0.5B to 1.5B public model with a planted keyword reward and p0 swept over 0.01% to 10% (at least 8 values, 3 seeds): (a) with AdamW, log(steps to 50%) against log(p0) has slope between -0.35 and -0.65 for p0 below 1/G; (b) with SGD at a learning rate matched at p0=10%, the slope is between …  
Control that could embarrass it: If the SGD and Adam arms give the same slope, the optimizer story is wrong and the one-parameter model does not describe a shared-weight network. If onset time depends on which keyword is used more than on its base rate (semantic fit with the task), base rate …  
Kill criterion: Slopes under Adam and SGD differ by less than 0.2, or R^2 of log-steps against log-p0 is below 0.6 across keywords at fixed optimizer.  
Cost: About 100 short RL runs: 30 to 50 Kaggle GPU hours across two or three weekly quotas, or about 3 days on the Mac for the 0.5B model. Toy version: 2 hours on the Mac. Downloads: one 1 GB model.

Claim: When ES fine-tunes billions of weights with a population of 30, the expected update equals the true gradient step, and the rest is a random walk orthogonal to it that is larger by a factor of about sqrt(d/N). ES works only because the model tolerates isotropic weight noise, so there is a predictable number of ES steps after which the accumulated walk crosses that tolerance and held-out behaviour collapses. …

Kill criterion: Predicted and observed collapse steps differ by more than a factor of 4 across three settings of (sigma, learning rate, N), or subspace ES recovers under 40% of the gain.

**Reward-trained adapters should merge where demonstration-trained adapters do not** (language models, model merging and composition; wildness 3)  
Claim: Two adapters trained by on-policy RL on different tasks can be added together and each keeps most of its gain. Two adapters trained by supervised fine-tuning to the same task scores cannot. The reason is that an RL update is small in KL and low-dimensional, so it writes into few residual directions that later layers of the other adapter read, while an SFT update writes surface-form changes into shared output …

Across all 28+ pairs, a weights-only score (sum over …  
Control that could embarrass it: Norm-match the adapters before merging. If RL adapters merge better only because they are smaller in norm, the claim reduces to 'small updates interfere less', which is known. The self-sample SFT arm is the other check: if it merges as well as RL, the cause …  
Kill criterion: After norm matching, the RL-pair and SFT-pair retention differ by under 10 points, or the cross-layer score does not beat same-layer cosine.

**Adam's epsilon as the forgetting knob: most of what fine-tuning destroys is moved by coordinates whose gradient is noise** (fine-tuning and continual learning (language first; applies …; wildness 2)  
Claim: Adam gives every coordinate a step of roughly the learning rate, including coordinates where the new task's gradient is small or is pure noise. Those coordinates make up most of the network, so most of the distance moved, and most of the damage to old knowledge, is not caused by the new task at all. Claim: A network that has memorised a task but not yet generalised is slow to learn anything new, and the same network after grokking learns new tasks about as fast as a fresh one. **Is it the reward or the on-policy data? A 13-parameter test of why RL needs so few weights** (language models, post-training; wildness 2)  
Claim: RL reaches its reasoning gains with a handful of parameters because it trains on the model's own text, not because it uses reward. Supervised training on the model's own correct samples should work through the same tiny adapter. Supervised training on someone else's solutions should not, because copying another writer's wording is a high-dimensional change.

**Long shot: learning from a critic with no gradient and no weight search, by adding the mean activation difference between praised and criticised rollouts** (language models, non-backprop learning rules; wildness 5)  
Claim: A model can improve on a task using only forward passes and a critic's good-or-bad verdicts. After each batch of rollouts, take the mean hidden state at the last prompt token for rollouts the critic approved, subtract the mean for those it rejected, and add a small multiple of that difference to the layer's bias. Repeating this for a few dozen rounds should recover a meaningful share of what gradient-based RL gets.

Kill criterion: Gain under 10% of the GRPO gain, or no separation from the shuffled-verdict arm at 3 seeds.  
Cost: 30 rounds times 512 rollouts times 5 arms times 3 seeds on a 0.5B model: about 4 to 6 days of Mac time, or two Kaggle sessions. Downloads: one model.

**Clock or lottery: the waiting-time law for sudden learning, over ten thousand seeds with the seed cut into its parts** (training dynamics, small transformers (language and …; wildness 2)  
Claim: When a small transformer sits on a loss plateau and then learns suddenly, the step at which it jumps is mostly fixed by the initial weights and is not a random event during training. Across seeds the waiting time is narrow and close to log-normal, with a hazard rate that rises over time, and not exponential. Data order and rounding together explain a minority of the spread.
