**Does a token cost the same energy whatever it says? Content-dependent joules, and renumbering hidden units to save power** (energy, inference hardware, language and image models; wildness 5)  
Claim: At fixed model, fixed sequence length and fixed kernel path, the energy an Apple GPU spends per token depends on the content being processed, by a measurable amount. Renumbering the hidden units of each MLP, which does not change the function at all, changes the power drawn. Both effects come from how many bits toggle in the arithmetic units between one operand and the next.  
Prediction: On the M1 Max, for a compute-bound workload (prompt processing at 2,048 tokens, fp16, one fixed model of 1 to 3B parameters): GPU rail power differs by at least 1.5% between natural prose and a prompt of one repeated token at identical throughput. Permuting MLP hidden units so that rows are sorted …  
Control that could embarrass it: Throughput must be equal to four digits between conditions, since a 1% speed difference from cache or memory layout looks like a 1% power difference. Record tokens per second per block and analyse energy per token, not watts. Second control: two different …  
Kill criterion: If the synthetic sorted-versus-random matrix product differs by under 2% in GPU power on the M1 Max, stop: the Gregersen effect does not transfer to this hardware. If it transfers but real prose versus repeated-token differs by under 0.5% with a confidence …  
Cost: 3 to 5 days of unattended measurement, 8 GB memory, one small model download at experiment time, and a logging wall meter (about 25 dollars) if Rob does not already have one.  
Nearest work listed: [Gregersen, Patel, Choukse, Input-Dependent Power Usage in GPUs, arXiv 2409.18324](https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2409.18324), 2024-09 (read); Shumailov et al., Sponge Examples: Energy-Latency Attacks on Neural Networks (2021) (not read directly); [Position: LLM Inference Should Be Evaluated as Energy-to-Token Production, arXiv 2605.11733](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11733), 2026-05-12 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One web search on input-dependent GPU power and LLM token content; it found the Gregersen paper and no measurement on real token streams. Hugging Face papers search: 'energy consumption LLM inference depends on input content sponge …

**The flight recorder run: one small model trained a thousand times past convergence, with bit-exact replay of anything that happens** (training dynamics, small transformers; wildness 5)  
Claim: A small transformer trained on a fixed mixed dataset with weight decay, far past the point where its loss looks flat, will show further discrete drops in held-out loss long after everyone stops watching. Because the run is bit-exact, any such event can be replayed from the nearest checkpoint with full instrumentation after it has been found. Grokking is the first rung of a ladder, not a single event.  
Prediction: Across 16 seeds of a 2-layer transformer trained for 1e8 steps on a mixture of five modular and compositional tasks plus a slice of natural text, at least half the seeds show one or more drops of 5% or more in held-out loss on some task after step 1e7, each completing within 2% of the elapsed step …  
Control that could embarrass it: Late drops may be numerical artefacts: Adam's second-moment estimate underflowing or the slingshot instability, which produces spikes that look like events. Run 4 of the 16 seeds in fp64 on CPU for the first 1e7 steps and require that the event statistics …  
Kill criterion: No seed shows a qualifying drop between 1e7 and 1e8 steps on any task. Then the honest report is that small transformers on this mixture are finished after the first transition, which is a useful null for people who argue that longer training hides more.  
Cost: About 30 days of background GPU at roughly 40 steps per second per batch of 16 seeds, under 4 GB memory, 16 to 20 GB of disk, no downloads beyond a small text slice. It can share the machine with …  
Nearest work listed: Power et al., Grokking: Generalization Beyond Overfitting on Small Algorithmic Datasets (2022) (not read directly); [Feature Lottery? A Bifurcation Theory of Concept Emergence, arXiv 2605.24057](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.24057), 2026-05 (not read directly); [Hu et al., Latent State Models of Training Dynamics, arXiv 2308.09543](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.09543), 2023-08 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face papers search on grokking timing and abrupt learning (three queries) and one web search on waiting-time distributions; none targeted 'very long training' directly. I did not check whether someone has published a 1e8-step …

**Same model, same settings, different distribution: sampler semantics across five stacks against the damage of 4-bit weights** (language model inference, evaluation; wildness 1)  
Claim: When two inference stacks are given the same model and the same named settings (for example temperature 0.7 and top-p 0.9), the distributions they sample from differ, because the stacks apply the steps in different orders and add different defaults. That difference is larger than the difference between 16-bit and 4-bit weights inside one stack. People compare quantised models with care and compare stacks with none.  
Prediction: On real logits from a 1 to 3B model over 10,000 token positions, with temperature 0.7 and top-p 0.9 passed explicitly and everything else left at each stack's default: the mean per-token total variation distance between at least two of five stacks (transformers, llama.cpp, MLX-LM, Ollama, vLLM) is …  
Control that could embarrass it: Set every sampler parameter explicitly in every stack, including order where the stack allows it. If the distance then falls to the arithmetic floor (near 1e-3), the effect is entirely defaults and ordering, which is the claim. If a large distance remains, …  
Kill criterion: Mean total variation between every pair of stacks under the named-settings condition is below 0.02 on real logits, or below the fp16 to Q4_K_M distance.  
Cost: Two days, mostly reading sampler code. Compute under 2 hours on the Mac Studio, 8 GB memory. Download at experiment time: one small model in safetensors and GGUF (about 3 GB).  
Nearest work listed: [Yuan et al., Understanding and Mitigating Numerical Sources of Nondeterminism in LLM Inference, arXiv …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09501), 2025-06-11 (read); [Song et al., The Good, The Bad, and The Greedy: Evaluation of LLMs Should Not Ignore Non-Determinism, arXiv …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.10457), 2024-07-15 (not read directly); [Dating the Model: Hidden Dates in System Prompts Affect LLM Evaluation, arXiv 2609.36931](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36931), 2026-09-29 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face papers search on numerical nondeterminism and reproducibility of LLM inference (one query, eight results read as snippets). No search aimed at sampler order specifically; GitHub issues in llama.cpp and Ollama were not searched …

### Lens: multimodal-understanding-and-binding

**Are the fitted modality weights just calibration temperatures, and does the rule hold for speech?** (image+text and audio+text understanding; wildness 3)  
Claim: A recent paper shows a VLM's answer under image-text conflict is a weighted sum of what each modality says alone, but the weights are fitted with no account of where they come from. I claim each weight is close to the temperature that would calibrate that modality's solo log-odds, so the model is correcting its own over- or under-confidence per modality. I also claim the same two-weight rule fits audio-text conflict …  
Prediction: Across at least 3 models and 4 degradation types, the fitted weight for a modality and the inverse calibration temperature of its solo log-odds agree within 0.2 absolute, with Pearson r of at least 0.8 across model-by-modality cells. For audio-text in one omni model the additive rule reaches R …  
Control that could embarrass it: Shuffle which modality's temperature is paired with which weight; if the agreement is as good, the match is an artefact of all values being near 1. Also check whether weights track temperature when the degradation changes calibration but not accuracy …  
Kill criterion: Correlation between weight and inverse temperature below 0.4, or audio R squared below 0.5 with residuals that show a clear interaction term.  
Cost: About 12 hours of Mac Studio time for three models; 15 to 20 GB of weights; no training. Stimulus building about one day.  
Nearest work listed: [Barua, Mahowald, Rudman, VLMs Integrate Conflicting Evidence Like Biased Bayesian Observers, arXiv 2610.19407](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19407), 2026-10-14 (read); [Audio-LLMs don't listen (text dominance), arXiv 2602.11488](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11488), 2026-03-23 (not read directly); [BayesBench, arXiv 2512.02719](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.02719), 2025-12-02 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face papers search and arXiv API for cue combination, modality conflict, text dominance audio, Bayesian observer VLM. No web search. Did not check citations of 2610.19407, which is four weeks old.

**Recognition gates the prior: counting fails on altered images because identity and count arrive through the same picture** (image+text understanding; wildness 2)  
Claim: VLMs miscount a five-legged dog because the image itself tells the model 'dog', and that recognition calls up 'four'. If the identity cue is hidden but the things to count stay visible, counting should recover. This is a case where the additive two-witness rule should fail, because one witness is triggering the other's prior.  
Prediction: On altered-animal and altered-logo items, masking the identity region (head, body texture, brand text) while leaving the counted parts visible raises exact-count accuracy from about 17% to above 60% on at least two small VLMs. The additive rule from 2610.19407 under-predicts the prior's pull on …  
Control that could embarrass it: Masking might help only because it removes clutter. Control: mask an equal-area region that carries no identity cue. If accuracy rises just as much, the claim is wrong.  
Kill criterion: Identity masking raises accuracy by less than 15 points, or the equal-area control gives at least 70% of the same gain.  
Cost: 6 to 8 hours including mask preparation (masks can be drawn semi-automatically with a segmentation model already on disk or by hand for 300 items); about 12 GB of weights.  
Nearest work listed: [Vision Language Models are Biased, arXiv 2505.23941](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23941), 2025-05-29 (not read directly); [Counting to Four..., arXiv 2604.10039](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10039), 2026-04-11 (not read directly); [Ill-Posed by Design, arXiv 2606.24335](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24335), 2026-06-23 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face papers search for counterfactual counting, VLM bias, counting circuits; arXiv API. The full text of 2505.23941 was not opened, so it may already include an identity-masking condition. That needs checking before any run.

**The language model reads the picture as a left-to-right string: binding swaps depend on row versus column** (image+text understanding; wildness 4)  
Claim: Long shot. When a VLM mixes up which colour belongs to which object, the error rate should differ for two objects side by side versus one above the other, because in the token string side-by-side objects are a few tokens apart and stacked objects are a whole row apart. Transposing the image should move the errors with it. Models with two-dimensional position encoding should not show the difference.  
Prediction: In models with 1D positions for image tokens (LLaVA-OneVision, Gemma 3, SmolVLM), swap errors for same-row pairs versus same-column pairs at equal pixel distance differ by a factor of at least 1.5. In Qwen2-VL-family models with 2D positions the ratio lies between 0.8 and 1.25. A linear probe on …  
Control that could embarrass it: Encoder positional embeddings or training data could cause a row-column difference with no role for the language model. The probe on projector outputs tests this. Also rotate by 180 degrees: order reverses but row/column stays, which separates distance from …  
Kill criterion: Ratio below 1.2 in all 1D-position models, or the probe on projector outputs shows the same asymmetry as the full model.  
Cost: About 8 hours; 20 GB of weights across four models; images generated with numpy in minutes.  
Nearest work listed: [Visual symbolic mechanisms / binding via spatial indices, arXiv 2506.15871](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15871), 2025-12-12 (not read directly); [Attention debiasing for image tokens, arXiv 2508.17807](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.17807), 2025-08-25 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API queries on 2026-11-09 for horizontal/vertical binding with raster or token order, and for illusory conjunction in VLMs: no relevant results. Hugging Face search for the same was rate-limited. Spatial-relation benchmarks (What's …

**Caption budget predicts pruning tolerance** (image+text understanding; wildness 4)  
Claim: Long shot. For each benchmark there is a shortest caption, written without seeing the question, that recovers most of the score. I claim this length predicts how many visual tokens can be thrown away at random on that benchmark. If so, visual-token pruning results mostly describe how little of the image the benchmark asks for.  
Prediction: Across at least 10 benchmarks, Spearman correlation of at least 0.8 between K* (caption tokens for 95% of full accuracy gain over the no-image baseline) and N* (random visual tokens kept for the same 95%). N* falls between 1 and 3 times K*. Embarrassing part: on at least 6 of 10 common benchmarks a …  
Control that could embarrass it: The captioner might leak answers by guessing typical questions. Control: captions from a different model family, and captions truncated at random rather than regenerated shorter. If the correlation vanishes with either, the claim is weak.  
Kill criterion: Spearman below 0.5 across benchmarks, or K* and N* differ by more than a factor of 10 on most benchmarks.  
Cost: 3,000 items times about 15 conditions, roughly 45,000 short generations: 20 to 30 hours on the Mac Studio, or split onto a free Kaggle GPU. One model, about 6 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [Random visual tokens and the information horizon, arXiv 2512.07580](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.07580), 2025-12-08 (not read directly); [F^3A: how many visual tokens, arXiv 2605.16359](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16359), 2026-05-09 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API query for caption plus token pruning returned nothing relevant; Hugging Face search rate-limited. Caption-only baselines for VQA are old and common (recalled, not checked); what I did not find is the caption length tied to …

**Fine comparison happens inside one encoder window or not at all: the tile-seam test** (image and video understanding; wildness 3)  
Claim: In models that cut a large image into tiles and encode each tile alone, two things can only be compared finely if they fall in the same tile. A same-or-different judgement should get worse when the two items straddle a tile seam, even at the same pixel distance. The same logic says video speed and direction judgements should improve when frames are pasted into one image.  
Prediction: In tiling models (LLaVA-OneVision, InternVL-family, SmolVLM) same/different accuracy on subtle pairs drops by at least 10 points when the pair straddles a seam versus sits inside one tile at equal separation. Native-resolution single-encoder models (Qwen2-VL family) show under 3 points. …  
Control that could embarrass it: The global thumbnail that tiling models also feed could carry the comparison and erase the effect. Also a seam might hurt only because items near tile edges are encoded worse; control with single items near a seam and a non-comparison question.  
Kill criterion: Seam drop under 4 points in all tiling models, or the single-item edge control explains the whole drop.  
Cost: 10 hours; about 20 GB of weights; needs careful reading of each model's tiling code (1 to 2 hours each).  
Nearest work listed: [Stateful Visual Encoders, arXiv 2606.04433](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04433), 2026-06-03 (not read directly); [MotionBlind, arXiv 2609.09528](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09528), 2026-09-08 (not read directly); [IG-VLM, arXiv 2403.18406](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.18406), 2024-03 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API query on 2026-11-09 for vision-language plus tile/crop boundary terms: three results, none relevant. Subtle-difference benchmarks 2603.07888 and 2609.06245 seen by abstract only. GitHub issues of tiling models not searched; a …

**Reading errors are locked to the patch grid** (image+text understanding (OCR); wildness 2)  
Claim: When a VLM reads small text, each merged visual token holds a few characters. I claim character-order errors happen mostly inside one token, and the pattern of which positions go wrong repeats when the text is shifted by a whole token and changes when shifted by half a token. This would mean small-text failure is a sampling-phase effect of the interface, separate from resolution.  
Prediction: For random strings rendered at about 4 characters per merged token, more than 70% of adjacent-character transpositions fall inside one token, against a base rate of about 75% of adjacent pairs being inside... so the sharper test is the shift: per-position error profiles correlate at least 0.6 after …  
Control that could embarrass it: Model-side resizing can move the grid so the nominal offset is not the real one; verify the actual patch alignment from the preprocessing code and by feeding a grid test image. Also check that a 28-pixel shift does not simply repeat because the string is …  
Kill criterion: Error-profile correlation after a half-token shift is within 0.15 of the full-token shift correlation.  
Cost: 6 hours; about 10 GB of weights; rendering with PIL in minutes.  
Nearest work listed: [Reading, Not Thinking, arXiv 2603.09095](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09095), 2026-03-10 (not read directly); [VLMs are blind, arXiv 2407.06581](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.06581), 2024-07 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API query on 2026-11-09 for VLM OCR with patch boundary/grid/alignment terms: no results. OCR-specific literature (document models, text-recognition transformers) not searched and may hold an equivalent finding for ViT recognisers.

**The clock is text: relabelled timestamps beat reversed frames** (video understanding; wildness 3)  
Claim: In video models that write a timestamp as text before each frame, the model takes order from the written timestamps more than from the frames. Relabelling timestamps in reverse while leaving frames in true order should flip before/after answers more often than reversing the frames while keeping true labels.  
Prediction: In a text-timestamp model, reversed labels with true frames flip at least 60% of before/after answers the model first got right; reversed frames with true labels flip at most 30%. In a model that encodes time by position ID only, reversing the time IDs alone flips under 20%.  
Control that could embarrass it: Shuffled (not reversed) labels: if nonsense labels flip answers as often as reversed ones, the effect is confusion, not use of the clock. Also remove labels entirely to get the frame-only baseline.  
Kill criterion: Label reversal flips fewer answers than frame reversal in the text-timestamp model.  
Cost: 8 hours; 10 to 15 GB of weights; clip preparation half a day.  
Nearest work listed: [Before It Fades, arXiv 2610.01595](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01595), 2026-10-01 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API query on 2026-11-09 for video, timestamp, shuffle/reverse, Video-LLM: no results. Hugging Face search rate-limited. Temporal-grounding papers that ablate timestamp formats were not searched and may contain a similar condition.

**You can only say what the training text talked about** (image+text understanding; wildness 3)  
Claim: For many visual properties, a simple probe on the vision encoder can read the answer while the full VLM cannot say it. I claim the size of that gap, per property, falls with how often the instruction-tuning text mentioned that property. The projector and language model learn to voice only what the captions and answers voiced.  
Prediction: Across at least 12 properties (colour, count, left/right, above/below, orientation, size order, texture, material, text presence, occlusion, lighting direction, blur), Spearman correlation between log mention rate in the 665k instruction set and the probe-minus-VLM accuracy gap is at most -0.7.  
Control that could embarrass it: Properties that are hard for the encoder have small gaps for a trivial reason (both fail). Restrict to properties where the probe is above 90%. Also the gap may track how many visual tokens the property needs; include token-count-matched pairs of properties …  
Kill criterion: Correlation weaker than -0.3 among properties with probe accuracy above 90%.  
Cost: 10 hours; LLaVA-1.5-7B about 14 GB or 4-bit 5 GB; training text download about 1 GB.  
Nearest work listed: Fu et al. 2025, Hidden in plain sight (recalled) (not read directly); [VoxParadox, arXiv 2605.27772](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27772), 2026-05-26 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API query on 2026-11-09 for linear probe, vision encoder, overlooked/unused in VLMs: no results from that exact query. The Fu et al. paper and its follow-ups were not opened; one of them may already relate the gap to training text …

### Lens: neuro-bio

**Mutation-selection balance for features: the rarest thing a model can keep is set by weight decay times sqrt(1-beta2), not by learning rate** (language models (training); should apply to any model …; wildness 2)  
Claim: Under AdamW, a feature with its own coordinates (a token embedding row, a rare class) is either fit almost completely or not learned at all, with a sharp cutoff frequency proportional to lambda times sqrt(1-beta2) and independent of learning rate. Under plain SGD, and for features that share coordinates with common ones, there is no cliff: residual loss falls off as lambda over frequency, and each kept feature costs …  
Prediction: In a small GPT with planted trigger-to-target facts at frequencies from 1e-6 to 1e-2: (1) half-loss frequency scales as lambda^p with p = 1.0 +/- 0.25 when the cutoff is below 1-beta2, rising toward 2 above it; (2) it scales as (1-beta2)^0.5 +/- 0.15; (3) it moves by less than 1.5x when learning …  
Control that could embarrass it: Exempt the embeddings from decay and keep everything else. If the cliff stays in the same place, the mechanism is not the balance on dedicated coordinates. Second control: Pinto et al.'s static theory predicts lambda squared and no beta2 dependence; if the …  
Kill criterion: Slope in lambda outside 0.6-2.2, or beta2 shift under 0.2 decades between 0.95 and 0.999, or learning rate moving the cutoff more than 2.5x.  
Cost: About 60 short runs. 25-40 GPU-hours on free T4s, or three to four days on the Mac. No downloads beyond TinyStories (about 2 GB) and later Pythia-70M/160M for the public check.

**The noise horizon: does per-layer noise in a transformer have a memory of about a dozen layers, set by residual norm growth?** (language models (inference); checkable on diffusion …; wildness 3)  
Claim: Noise added at every layer of a pre-norm transformer does not accumulate with depth. The residual stream grows by a few percent per layer and the final normalisation divides that out, so a perturbation of fixed relative size injected k layers before the end is attenuated by the growth factor to the power k. Total output error then equals the error from the last roughly 1/(2 ln growth) layers, whatever the depth.  
Prediction: For Gaussian noise of equal relative size added to the residual stream after every block: (1) output KL from noising all layers is within 25% of the KL from noising only the last K layers, with K = 1/(2 ln growth), between 8 and 20 for models of 24 to 48 layers; (2) across at least 8 public models …  
Control that could embarrass it: The common finding in mixed-precision work that the first layers are the most sensitive. If early-layer noise of equal relative size does more damage than late-layer noise, block Jacobians amplify faster than norm growth damps, m is above 1 early on, and the …  
Kill criterion: All-layer noise does more than 2x the damage of last-K-layer noise in most models, or fitted m exceeds 1 over more than a third of the depth.  
Cost: Two to three days on the Mac. Downloads: about 15 GB of small public checkpoints. No training.  
Nearest work listed: Heimersheim and Turner, Residual stream norms grow exponentially over the forward pass (Alignment Forum, 2023) (not read directly); [LayerRoPE: Dynamic Depth-wise Magnitude and Angular Superposition, arXiv 2610.09179](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09179), 2026-10-06 (not read directly); [The Curse of Depth in Large Language Models, arXiv 2502.05795](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05795), 2025-02-09 (not read directly); [Tracing distinguishability through transformer processing with stochastic LayerNorm, arXiv 2608.30720](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30720), 2026-08 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face papers search: 'residual stream norm grows exponentially forward pass', 'noise injection every layer transformer error accumulation depth saturation', 'stable regions residual stream perturbation sensitivity'. One WebSearch on …

**Robustness is not one trait: crab-style multi-perturbation test on fifty public Pythia seeds** (language models (evaluation); extendable to small diffusion …; wildness 2)  
Claim: Models trained with the same recipe and different seeds have the same loss but different tolerances, and a seed's tolerance to one kind of global perturbation does not predict its tolerance to another. Flatness is not a single axis. Picking the seed is then a free robustness lever, but only per perturbation.  
Prediction: Across the 10 seeds at each of 5 PolyPythia sizes, for five perturbation classes (weight rounding to 3 bits, Gaussian activation noise, attention-logit gain of 1.3, random removal of 10% of heads, character-level input noise): (1) the coefficient of variation across seeds of loss increase is at …  
Control that could embarrass it: If one sharpness number predicts all five tolerances with correlation above 0.6, robustness is one trait after all and the biology does not transfer. Second control: re-evaluate on a second held-out set; if seed rankings do not reproduce (test-retest Spearman …  
Kill criterion: First principal component explains more than 70% of variance, or median cross-perturbation Spearman above 0.6.  
Cost: One to two days on the Mac for evaluation. Downloads: about 20 GB for 50 small checkpoints. From-scratch arm: about 10 hours.  
Nearest work listed: [PolyPythias: Stability and Outliers across Fifty Language Model Pre-Training Runs, arXiv 2503.09543](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09543), 2025-03-12 (not read directly); D'Amour et al., Underspecification Presents Challenges for Credibility in Modern Machine Learning (2020) (not read directly); Ratliff, Franci, Marder et al., Neuronal oscillator robustness to multiple global perturbations, Biophysical … (not read directly); Round one of this programme: 'The Muon quantization paradox' (read)

**The spacing effect as a probe: a single-timescale learner must prefer massed repetition, so any interior optimum reveals multi-timescale memory in the weights** (language models (training, data ordering); wildness 3)  
Claim: If a fact is shown twice and tested R steps after the second showing, a learner whose memory decays at one rate retains most when the two showings are adjacent. Humans do the opposite, with a best gap of roughly 10-20% of R that shrinks as R grows. Transformers trained with AdamW will show the human pattern, and the size of the effect measures how many timescales their weights carry.  
Prediction: For synthetic facts injected twice into a small GPT's training stream: at R of 1,000 and 10,000 steps, retention (log-probability of the fact at test) peaks at a gap between 5% and 40% of R, exceeds massed presentation by at least 20% of the massed value, and the best gap fraction at R=10,000 is …  
Control that could embarrass it: Adam's momentum buffers make adjacent repeats behave differently for a trivial reason. Reset or freeze the optimizer state for the fact's gradient, or use gaps no shorter than 1/(1-beta1) steps as the massed baseline. If the interior peak vanishes once …  
Kill criterion: Retention is monotone in gap (massed best, or longest gap best) at both R values after the momentum control.  
Cost: About 40 runs, 30 hours on the Mac or one weekend of free T4 time. No downloads beyond TinyStories.  
Nearest work listed: [Chang et al., How Do Large Language Models Acquire Factual Knowledge During Pretraining?, arXiv 2406.11813](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.11813), 2024-06-17 (not read directly); [Do Your Best and Get Enough Rest for Continual Learning, arXiv 2503.18371](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.18371), 2025-03-24 (not read directly); [Can a Language Model Learn Facts Continually in Its Weights?, arXiv 2607.11020](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11020), 2026-07-14 (not read directly); Amiri et al., Repeat before Forgetting: Spaced Repetition for Efficient and Effective Training of Neural … (not read directly)

**Do learned 2D rotary frequencies arrange themselves into hexagonal grid modules?** (image and video models (vision transformers, diffusion …; wildness 4)  
Claim: When a vision transformer is allowed to learn the direction and magnitude of its 2D rotary frequencies, frequencies of similar magnitude within a head will settle about 60 degrees apart, and magnitudes will cluster into bands separated by a ratio near 1.4 to 1.65. This is the arrangement grid cells use. If it holds, axial x/y rotary embeddings, which every large image and video model ships with, are the wrong fixed …  
Prediction: In trained RoPE-Mixed style models, the histogram of angle differences between frequency vectors in the same head whose magnitudes are within 20% of each other has a peak at 60 +/- 10 degrees with density at least 1.5x a uniform baseline, and it sharpens during training from a random start. A fixed …  
Control that could embarrass it: GridPE (2024) reports that randomly oriented wave vectors consistently outperform fixed-direction variants. If random directions tie or beat the hexagonal layout and learned angles are uniform, the grid-cell story is decoration and the only real finding is …  
Kill criterion: Learned angle-difference histogram is within noise of uniform at the end of training in both public checkpoints and from-scratch runs.  
Cost: Step one: two hours, downloads under 1 GB. Step two: 15 runs of ViT-S on ImageNet-100, roughly 60-90 T4 hours, about two weeks of free quota.

**Is a speech or image tokenizer a good genetic code? Scoring codecs by how harmless the language model's own mistakes are** (speech synthesis and autoregressive image generation; wildness 3)  
Claim: What makes a codec good for a token language model is not how well it reconstructs, and not only how predictable its tokens are, but whether the tokens the model confuses with each other decode to nearly the same sound or image. The ratio of decoded distortion under model-typical substitutions to distortion under random substitutions will predict downstream generation quality better than reconstruction metrics do.  
Prediction: Define the error-minimisation ratio as mean decoded distortion when 5% of tokens are replaced by the model's second choice, divided by the same under random replacement. Across at least 6 codecs with small token models trained under one recipe, this ratio spans at least a 3x range, correlates with …  
Control that could embarrass it: Token perplexity alone might predict downstream quality just as well, in which case error tolerance adds nothing to what LLM-Codec already optimises. Fit a regression with perplexity first and report only the added variance explained by the ratio. If it is …  
Kill criterion: Spearman under 0.4 between the ratio and downstream quality, or under 10% added variance beyond perplexity.  
Cost: About a week. 8 small training runs of 3-6 hours each on a T4, plus encoding. Downloads: codec weights (under 5 GB total) and LibriTTS-100 (about 7 GB).  
Nearest work listed: [LLM-Codec: Neural Audio Codec Meets Language Model Objectives, arXiv 2604.17852](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17852), 2026-04-20 (not read directly); [Language-Codec: Reducing the Gaps Between Discrete Codec Representation and Speech Language Models, arXiv …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.12208), 2024-02 (not read directly); [Hierarchical Decoding for Discrete Speech Synthesis with Multi-Resolution Spoof Detection, arXiv 2603.05373](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05373), 2026-04-11 (not read directly); Freeland and Hurst, The genetic code is one in a million, Journal of Molecular Evolution 1998 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face papers search: 'neural audio codec token substitution robustness language model errors'. Did not search speech conferences (Interspeech, ICASSP) directly, and codec work moves fast, so a substitution-tolerance metric may exist …

**One gain knob, two modes: does a global attention gain move a language model between integrating weak evidence and selecting one item?** (language models (inference); wildness 4)  
Claim: Multiplying every attention logit in a pretrained model by one scalar will show a double dissociation. Tasks that need many weak cues pooled will do best at gain below 1. Tasks that need one item picked out from distractors will do best at gain above 1. The default of 1 is a compromise that is best for neither.  
Prediction: On a pretrained 1-3B model, with gain swept from 0.7 to 1.5: the best gain for many-shot in-context classification and in-context linear regression is at or below 0.9; the best gain for needle retrieval among distractors and for copying is at or above 1.1; the two optima differ by at least 0.15; …  
Control that could embarrass it: Scale the final output logits by the same factor instead of the attention logits. If that reproduces the task differences, the effect is output calibration and has nothing to do with attention competition. Second: if every task peaks within 0.03 of gain 1, …  
Kill criterion: Best gains for the integrate and select families differ by less than 0.05, or no task improves by more than 1 point at any gain.  
Cost: Two days on the Mac. Downloads: about 10 GB of public checkpoints.  
Nearest work listed: Velickovic et al., softmax is not enough (for sharp out-of-distribution performance), 2024 (not read directly); Nakanishi, Scalable-Softmax Is Superior for Attention, 2025 (not read directly); Eldar, Cohen and Niv, The effects of neural gain on attention and learning, Nature Neuroscience 2013 (not read directly); Round one of this programme: 'Quantization as an entropy shift' (read)  
Author's note on the search: Hugging Face papers search: 'attention temperature scaling inference task difficulty language model gain'. Results were about sampling temperature, not attention gain. I did not search for inference-time attention temperature sweeps on …

**Haldane's cost of keeping: does excess loss from weight decay equal lambda times the number of features kept, regardless of what they are?** (language models and small vision models (training theory); wildness 3)  
Claim: In the regime where features share coordinates, the loss a model gives up to weight decay is the same for every feature it maintains, rare or common, and adds up to about lambda times the summed weight those features need. Decay that spares large weights, as sleep-time downscaling does in cortex, would remove this cost for established features while still clearing small ones.  
Prediction: In a from-scratch sparse-feature model with K planted features and SGD with L2 decay: excess loss over the no-decay run is linear in lambda (R squared above 0.95 over a 30x range) and linear in K, and changes by less than 20% when the feature frequency distribution is changed from uniform to Zipf …  
Control that could embarrass it: In normalised networks weight decay mostly sets the effective learning rate and has little to do with shrinking features. If the GPT arm shows no dependence of excess loss on K-like quantities, and sparing changes results only through the effective learning …  
Kill criterion: Excess loss changes by more than 2x between uniform and Zipf frequencies at fixed K in the toy, or the sparing rule gives no gain in the GPT after learning rate is retuned for both arms.  
Cost: Toy: an afternoon. GPT arm: about 15 runs, 10 hours on the Mac. No downloads beyond TinyStories.  
Nearest work listed: [AdamHD: Decoupled Huber Decay Regularization, arXiv 2511.14721](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.14721), 2025-11-18 (not read directly); [Pinto, Galanti, Balestriero, The Fair Language Model Paradox, arXiv 2410.11985](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.11985), 2024-10-15 (read); de Vivo et al., Ultrastructural evidence for synaptic scaling across the wake/sleep cycle, Science 2017 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: Covered by the searches for the first idea, plus the AdamHD abstract. I did not search for 'magnitude-dependent weight decay' or 'selective weight decay' specifically; variants almost certainly exist and should be collected before running.

### Lens: no-boss

**Every subset of layers: the complete skip lattice of 12 to 24 block models, and an exact test of the additivity that pruning methods assume** (language first; then speech (Whisper base) and vision …; wildness 2)  
Claim: For public models with 12 to 24 blocks, the loss with every possible subset of blocks skipped can be computed exactly on a Mac. I expect the exact lattice to show that block interactions are strong but short-range in depth, so the true best set of blocks to remove is usually not contiguous and is not what greedy or single-block scores find. Layer-importance papers sample this space and assume the opposite or never …  
Prediction: On at least 3 of 4 models (GPT-2 small, Llama-3.2-1B, Whisper base encoder+decoder, ViT-S): (a) when 25% of blocks are removed, the exact best subset is non-contiguous and its loss increase is at least 20% smaller than both the best contiguous block and the greedy choice; (b) a purely additive …  
Control that could embarrass it: Repeat on a second, unrelated evaluation text and check the best subsets agree. Repeat with mean-ablation instead of skipping. If greedy finds the exact optimum at every size, or the additive model explains over 90% of variance, the lattice says the sampled …  
Kill criterion: Greedy removal is within 5% of the exact optimum loss at every subset size on all models, and the additive model explains more than 90% of variance.  
Cost: About 1 day for 12 to 18 block models, about 1 week of background Mac time if the 24-block lattice is included. Downloads under 6 GB. Memory under 8 GB. Output tables under 1 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [David Noel Ng, 'LLM Neuroanatomy' (RYS) blog post](https://dnhkng.github.io/posts/rys/), 2026-03-10 (read); [Ding et al., Pruning as a Cooperative Game, arXiv:2602.07804 (ICLR 2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07804), 2026-02-08 (read); [Efficient Shapley Value-based Non-Uniform Pruning of LLMs, arXiv:2505.01731](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01731), 2025-05 (not read directly); Sun et al., Transformer Layers as Painters, arXiv:2407.09298, 2024-07 (not read directly)

**The canary census: which open models can recite benchmark canary strings, and does it mean they read the test data or only the discussion of it** (language; evaluation; wildness 2)  
Claim: A benchmark canary is a random GUID placed in test files so that trainers can filter them out. I expect that a large and rising share of open-weight models assign the BIG-bench GUID far more probability than chance, and that canaries which appear only inside data files are memorised much less. That split would separate 'the model saw people talking about the canary' from 'the model was trained on the protected …  
Prediction: Across at least 60 open base and chat models of 4B parameters or fewer released 2022 to 2026, the share giving the BIG-bench GUID a mean loss under 1 bit per hex character (chance is 4 bits) rises from under 10% for pre-2023 releases to over 50% for 2025-26 releases. For at least two canaries that …  
Control that could embarrass it: Fifty fresh random GUIDs after the same preamble must score 4 bits per character; if they do not, the preamble itself is leaking structure. A second control gives the true GUID after a neutral preamble. If file-only canaries are memorised as often as the …  
Kill criterion: Fewer than 5% of models beat 3 bits per character on any canary, or the control GUIDs deviate from 4 bits by more than 0.2.  
Cost: About 10 to 20 hours of Kaggle or Mac time, mostly download. Roughly 200 GB of transient downloads, deleted per model. A few hours to catalogue canaries by hand.  
Nearest work listed: [Jozdien, 'BIG-Bench Canary Contamination in GPT-4', LessWrong](https://www.greaterwrong.com/posts/kSmHMoaLKGcGgyWzs/big-bench-canary-contamination-in-gpt-4), 2024-10-22 (read); [Efrat et al., LMentry, arXiv:2211.02069](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.02069), 2022-11 (not read directly); Roberts et al., Data Contamination Through the Lens of Time, arXiv:2310.10628, 2023-10-16 (not read directly)

**Ten thousand takes of one sentence: the hazard rate of catastrophic failure in speech synthesis** (speech synthesis; wildness 2)  
Claim: Mean word error rate hides the rate at which a TTS model produces a broken utterance: silence, a loop, an early stop, babble. I expect autoregressive models to fail like a decay process, with a constant chance per second of falling into a bad state, so survival falls exponentially with utterance length. I expect flow-matching models to fail on particular inputs every time or never.  
Prediction: For at least 3 autoregressive models, log survival probability is linear in audio length with R^2 above 0.9. The fitted hazard per second differs by more than 30 times between models whose mean WER differs by under 0.5 points. For flow-matching models, more than 80% of failures come from under 5% …  
Control that could embarrass it: The recogniser may be what fails, not the TTS. Hand listening to all flagged clips settles this, and real recorded speech of the same sentences run through the same detector gives the detector's own false-alarm rate. If hazards rank the models the same way …  
Kill criterion: Hazard per second ranks the models identically to mean WER and differs by under 3 times between them, or log survival against length has R^2 under 0.5 for the autoregressive models.  
Cost: About 500,000 utterances. One to two weeks of background Mac time plus Kaggle weekly quota for the autoregressive models. Under 15 GB of model downloads. Ten to fifteen hours of listening.  
Nearest work listed: [Asaria, Salomone, Gandhi, Reliable Neural-Codec TTS by ASR Self-Verification and Distillation, …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18323), 2026-06-16 (read)

**The rare-mechanism census: train one small language model a thousand times and count the minority solutions** (language; interpretability; training; wildness 4)  
Claim: Interpretability results describe one trained model, or five to fifty seeds. I expect that for some well-defined behaviours a small share of seeds learn a different internal solution with the same accuracy. A study with ten seeds would usually miss it.  
Prediction: Among 5 behaviours fixed in advance (for example induction, previous-token attention, bracket matching, repeated-name completion, a simple agreement task), at least 1 shows a minority solution class in 1% to 10% of 1,000 seeds, separated from the majority in a pre-chosen mechanism score and within …  
Control that could embarrass it: Heads can be permuted within a layer, so a 'different solution' may be the same one in another slot; scores must be permutation-invariant. Re-score every run on a second evaluation text to make sure the clusters are not measurement noise. If the clusters …  
Kill criterion: No behaviour shows a cluster holding at least 1% of seeds that survives permutation-invariant scoring and longer training.  
Cost: About 85 hours of Mac GPU time for 1,000 runs, 25 more for the warmup arms. About 15 GB of stored weights. No downloads beyond the corpus.  
Nearest work listed: [van der Wal et al., PolyPythias, arXiv:2503.09543](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09543), 2025-03-12 (read); [Méloux et al., Is Mechanistic Interpretability Identifiable?, arXiv:2502.20914](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20914), 2025-02-28 (read); Zhong et al., The Clock and the Pizza, arXiv:2306.17844, 2023-06 (not read directly)

**How many people can a fast image model draw? A capture-recapture count of identities in step-distilled models** (image generation; wildness 3)  
Claim: Users of few-step image models complain that every seed gives the same face. I expect the number of distinct identities a distilled model can draw for a generic portrait prompt to be a countable few hundred to few thousand, at least ten times fewer than its teacher. The count can be estimated the way ecologists estimate species from repeat sightings.  
Prediction: For 'portrait photo of a woman' and 'of a man' at 10,000 seeds, the Chao1 estimate of distinct identities (face-embedding clusters at a verification threshold) is under 2,000 for at least 2 of 3 distilled models and at least 10 times higher for the matched teacher at its default steps and guidance. …  
Control that could embarrass it: Run the same pipeline on 10,000 real portraits of distinct people; the estimator must return a number far above 10,000. Run it on a set with known repeat identities; it must return roughly the true count. If the teacher at its normal guidance scale is as …  
Kill criterion: Student and teacher identity counts are within a factor of 2, or the estimator fails either calibration set.  
Cost: About 10 hours for students, 40 to 50 hours for teachers at 25 steps. Under 20 GB of model downloads. Calibration photo sets about 15 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [Arora and Zhang, Do GANs actually learn the distribution?, arXiv:1706.08224](https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.08224), 2017-06-26 (read)

**The date scan: does an open model's effort depend on what day it thinks it is, for every day from 2015 to 2030** (language; wildness 5)  
Claim: In December 2023 a developer reported that GPT-4 Turbo wrote about 5% less when told it was December than when told it was May, and a replication with 240 samples found nothing. I propose to settle the question on open models by reading the end-of-turn probability directly for every calendar date across fifteen years. I expect weekday and holiday effects to be small or absent, and a clear step in behaviour at the …  
Prediction: In at least 2 of 6 open chat models: the end-of-turn hazard, averaged over 50 prompts, shifts by at least 3% within six months either side of the documented cutoff. Weekend against weekday differs by under 1%, and 20 to 31 December against May differs by under 2%, which would count against the …  
Control that could embarrass it: Replace the date with a serial number of the same token length; any 'effect' that survives is tokenisation, not date. Shuffle date labels to get the null distribution of the periodic fit. Write the date in three formats; an effect present in only one format …  
Kill criterion: No periodic or step component exceeds the shuffled-label null at 0.5% in any model.  
Cost: About 300,000 short forward passes per model; 5 to 10 hours per model on the Mac. Under 20 GB of downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [Coverage of Rob Lynch's test and Ian Arawjo's failed replication (Ars Technica and others)](https://arstechnica.com/information-technology/2023/12/is-chatgpt-becoming-lazier-because-its-december-people-run-tests-to-find-out), 2023-12 (not read directly)

**Does every stack show the model the same picture? An exhaustive size-by-size audit of image preprocessing for open vision-language models** (multimodal; evaluation; wildness 1)  
Claim: Before a vision-language model sees an image, each inference stack resizes it and cuts it into tiles using its own port of the reference code. The tile layout depends only on the image's width and height, so every size up to 4096 by 4096 can be checked in every stack without running a model. I expect several popular model and stack pairs to disagree with the reference on a measurable share of sizes, and accuracy on …  
Prediction: Across 15 widely downloaded open VLMs and 3 stacks (transformers as reference, llama.cpp, MLX-VLM), at least 4 model-stack pairs give a different tile grid or token count on at least 1% of the 16.8 million (width, height) pairs. On sizes where they disagree, OCR or document-question exact match is …  
Control that could embarrass it: The accuracy gap may come from weight quantisation in the GGUF or MLX file and not from preprocessing. Feed the reference stack's pixel tensor into the other stack's model; if the gap remains, preprocessing is not the cause. If the disagreeing sizes are ones …  
Kill criterion: No model-stack pair disagrees on more than 0.1% of sizes weighted by real-image frequency, and pixel differences change accuracy by under 0.5 points.  
Cost: Step 1 is a few CPU hours. Steps 2 and 3 about 20 to 30 hours on the Mac. About 30 GB of small-VLM downloads in two formats.  
Nearest work listed: [llama.cpp issue 16776, Resize and tile split discrepancy between HF transformers and LlamaCpp for SmolVLM2](https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/issues/16776), 2025-10-26 (read); Parmar, Zhang, Zhu, On Aliased Resizing and Surprising Subtleties in GAN Evaluation, arXiv:2104.11222, 2021-04 (not read directly)

**A one-listener lab: ten thousand forced-choice trials to find where speech metrics stop tracking what a person hears** (speech synthesis; evaluation; wildness 4)  
Claim: Objective speech metrics were fitted to rate differences between systems, not small changes within one system. I expect that for some kinds of damage a trained listener reliably hears a change that the metrics score as noise, and for other kinds the metrics move while the listener hears nothing. One person doing short daily sessions for six weeks can measure this with classical psychophysics.  
Prediction: With damage applied along 4 axes (weight noise in the acoustic model, dropped codec bits, fewer sampling steps, shorter speaker prompt), at the listener's 75%-correct threshold a standard quality predictor changes by less than its own seed-to-seed standard deviation on at least 2 axes. On at least …  
Control that could embarrass it: One listener may be idiosyncratic. If the three check listeners' thresholds differ from the main listener's by more than 2 times, the result is about one pair of ears. If catch-trial false alarms exceed 10%, the listener was guessing.  
Kill criterion: Listener thresholds and metric thresholds (defined as a 1 standard deviation move) agree within a factor of 1.5 on all four axes.  
Cost: About 15 Mac hours to generate stimuli. About 14 hours of listening spread over six weeks. No downloads beyond two small TTS models and the metric models.

### Lens: numerical-analysis

**Adam's second moment cannot decay in bfloat16: the default optimizer silently becomes a one-way ratchet** (training (any modality); most relevant to community …; wildness 2)  
Claim: With round-to-nearest and p significand bits, multiplying a value by beta leaves it unchanged whenever 1-beta is below about 2^-(p+1). For bfloat16 (p=8) that covers beta2=0.999, so Adam's second-moment state can rise but never fall when it is stored in bf16. The optimizer then behaves like a max-tracking method whose step size only shrinks, and the same law freezes any weight EMA with decay 0.9999 held in fp16 or …  
Prediction: In a real PyTorch AdamW run with bf16 state and beta2=0.999, under 1% of coordinates ever see their second moment decrease over 1000 steps, against over 99% with fp32 state. Fed the same gradient sequence, the bf16-state run ends with a median per-coordinate step at least 2x smaller than the …  
Control that could embarrass it: PyTorch may compute the scalar multiply in higher precision, or its fused/foreach path may round differently, so that the state decays normally. The replay in step 1 settles this in minutes. A second embarrassment: the state is monotone but the loss curves of …  
Kill criterion: More than 20% of coordinates show decreasing second moment in real bf16 state at beta2=0.999, or the fp32-master-plus-bf16-state twin matches the all-fp32 twin within seed spread.  
Cost: 1 hour for the replay, 3-6 hours for twins on Mac MPS or CPU. No downloads beyond a small text dataset.  
Nearest work listed: [Full-Weight VLA Fine-Tuning Without an fp32 Master Copy Silently Erases Most Updates, arXiv 2610.31859](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31859), 2026-10-22 (read); [M+Adam: Low-Precision Training via Additive-Multiplicative Optimization, arXiv 2607.10611](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10611), 2026-07-12 (read); [FlashOptim: Optimizers for Memory-Efficient Training, arXiv 2602.23349](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23349), 2026-02-26 (not read directly); [Revisiting BFloat16 Training, arXiv 2010.06192](https://arxiv.org/abs/2010.06192), 2020-10-13 (not read directly)

**The residual stream is a recursive sum: bf16 inference error is set by the adds, not the matmuls** (language models (inference), also deep DiTs; wildness 2)  
Claim: Higham's bound for recursive summation says rounding error scales with the running total, not with the term being added. In a deep transformer the residual stream is the running total and each block output is a small term. So in an all-bf16 model the two adds per block should inject more error than everything inside the block, and keeping only the residual tensor in fp32 should recover most of the fp32 behaviour.  
Prediction: For a model of 32 or more layers loaded entirely in bf16, moving only the residual tensor and its adds to fp32 removes at least 60% of the mean KL between fp32 and bf16 next-token distributions. The removed share grows with depth, reaching 75% or more at 60 layers. Moving an equal number of other …  
Control that could embarrass it: The matched-count arm. If fp32 on random other tensors helps as much, the residual is not special. Also the Sept 2026 paper below reports that widening FP32 scope made bf16-versus-fp16 agreement worse; my target is fidelity to fp32, which is a different …  
Kill criterion: fp32 residual removes under 30% of the KL, or the matched-count arm comes within 10 points of it.  
Cost: 3-5 hours, about 8 GB of checkpoints, mostly CPU.  
Nearest work listed: [Greedy Decoding Is Not Precision-Invariant, arXiv 2609.26621](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26621), 2026-09-22 (read); [Understanding and Mitigating Numerical Sources of Nondeterminism in LLM Inference, arXiv 2506.09501](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09501), 2025-06-11 (read); [Demystifying Numerical Instability in LLM Inference (HEAL), arXiv 2606.21023](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21023), 2026-06-19 (read)  
Author's note on the search: OpenAlex: 'numerical nondeterminism LLM inference bfloat16 reproducibility', 'logit margin token flip quantization greedy decoding divergence'. Full-text grep of 2609.26621 failed (no HTML). Not checked: whether any inference stack already …

**Backward error for samplers: invert the approximate output through the full model and test the noise for Gaussianity** (image and video generation (evaluation); wildness 3)  
Claim: The full model's ODE flow is a bijection from noise to latents. So any approximate sampler (few-step, cached, quantized, distilled) produces exactly what the full model would produce from some other noise. The approximate sampler draws from the right distribution if and only if those equivalent noises are standard Gaussian, and that is very cheap to test in 16,000 or more dimensions.  
Prediction: For a 4-step distilled student inverted through its teacher, at least one of 8 radial frequency bands of the equivalent noise deviates from unit power by 5% or more, with a deficit at high frequencies. For 4-bit weights at the full step count, all bands stay within 1%. A 2x cache lands in between …  
Control that could embarrass it: The calibration run. If inverting the reference sampler's own outputs already gives non-Gaussian noise at 200 steps, the instrument's floor is too high, which is likely under strong guidance. A second control: a different seed of the full model must score as …  
Kill criterion: Calibration floor above 2% in any band without guidance, or all approximations (including the 4-step student) within 1% of Gaussian.  
Cost: About 100 x 200 forward passes per approximation; 4-8 hours on the M1 Max for a 2B-class model at 512px, one checkpoint pair of 5-10 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [Spectral Collapse in Diffusion Inversion, arXiv 2602.13303](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13303), 2026-02-09 (read); BELM: Bidirectional Explicit Linear Multi-step Sampler for Exact Inversion, NeurIPS 2024 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: OpenAlex: 'diffusion inversion noise Gaussianity test sampler evaluation'. Not checked: likelihood-based evaluation of distilled samplers, or watermark and detection papers that test inverted-noise statistics.

**Guided sampling against Euler's stability test: a threshold in steps remaining, not in sigma** (image generation (sampling); wildness 4)  
Claim: For a flow model the Euler amplification factor along a Jacobian direction is 1 + (mu-1)/m, where mu is the eigenvalue of the denoiser Jacobian and m is the number of steps remaining. An ideal unguided denoiser has mu at or above zero, so Euler never overshoots. Guidance combines two Jacobians with a negative weight and can push mu below 1-2m, which makes Euler unstable with sign-alternating error in the last m …  
Prediction: For a non-distilled model at guidance 5 or more and 10 or fewer steps, more than 20% of prompts have a direction with mu below 1-2m in at least one of the last three steps (below -1 at the last step, -3 at the second to last). Along those directions the per-step displacement changes sign between …  
Control that could embarrass it: The unguided spectrum. If the learned unguided denoiser already has mu below -1, the effect is not about guidance. And if switching guidance off for only the last m* steps changes nothing measurable, the instability is harmless even if present. Note the …  
Kill criterion: Most negative mu at the final step is above -1 for over 90% of prompts at guidance 7. Then report the measured spectrum as a negative result.  
Cost: About 9,000 forward passes: 15 minutes for SiT-XL, 2-3 hours for a 2B model on the M1 Max. 3-10 GB download.

**Iterative refinement for sampling: a cheap sibling propagates, the full model only corrects, and Jacobian mismatch sets the rate** (image and video generation (inference); wildness 3)  
Claim: Iterative refinement solves a system with a cheap inexact solver and uses the accurate operator only to compute residuals. Applied to a sampling trajectory, the cheap model (4-bit copy, pruned or smaller sibling) runs the sequential steps and the full model is evaluated only at the cheap trajectory's states, which can be done in one batch or on another machine. The fixed point is the full model's exact trajectory, …

With G a step-cached or half-depth variant the factor is under 2x per sweep. The measured factor is predicted within 30% by the …  
Control that could embarrass it: A plain second cheap run with a fresh correction term set to zero, and a baseline where the same number of full-model evaluations is simply spent on a shorter full-model sampling run. If the short full run is closer to the reference than cheap-plus-one-sweep …  
Kill criterion: One sweep with the 4-bit sibling improves distance by under 2x, or Jacobian mismatch fails to rank three different cheap models correctly.

**Multilevel Monte Carlo gradients across resolution: unbiased high-resolution training at a fraction of the cost, but only for high-noise timesteps** (image and video generation (training); wildness 4)  
Claim: Multilevel Monte Carlo estimates an expensive expectation as a cheap coarse estimate plus a rarely sampled coupled correction. For diffusion training the coarse level is the same image at quarter resolution with matched noise and matched signal-to-noise ratio. The correction is cheap only if coarse and fine per-sample gradients are highly correlated, which I expect at high-noise timesteps and not at low-noise ones.  
Prediction: On a resolution-flexible public DiT, the correlation between coupled quarter-resolution and full-resolution per-sample gradients (measured through the variance of their difference on a fixed random projection of 10,000 coordinates) is 0.9 or higher for the noisiest 40% of timesteps and under 0.5 …  
Control that could embarrass it: Uncoupled pairs (same image, independent noise) and unmatched signal-to-noise pairs. If correlation is equally high without coupling, the effect is just that gradients share a mean. If it is low even with coupling at high noise, the idea is dead. Also, with …  
Kill criterion: Coupled correlation under 0.8 at quarter resolution in every timestep band.  
Cost: Measurement: 4-6 hours on the M1 Max, 3-10 GB checkpoint. From-scratch test: 1-2 days of free-tier GPU.  
Nearest work listed: Multi-Level Monte Carlo training of neural operators, CMAME 2026, 2026-02-10 (not read directly); Multilevel and Sequential Monte Carlo for Training-Free Diffusion Guidance, arXiv 2601.21104, 2026-01-28 (not read directly); Bayesian computation with generative diffusion models by Multilevel Monte Carlo, Phil. **Sequence extrapolation over depth: treat layer outputs as a converging sequence and extrapolate past the exit point** (language models (inference, interpretability); wildness 5)  
Claim: Vector extrapolation methods (Aitken, reduced rank extrapolation) recover the limit of a linearly converging sequence from a few iterates. If the late layers of a transformer refine a prediction in a roughly geometric way, then extrapolating from two or three consecutive layer states should predict the final-layer output better than the last available state alone. Extrapolating past the final layer might even …  
Prediction: Exiting at 75% depth, x_l + alpha*(x_l - x_{l-k}) with one global alpha fitted on held-out text lowers KL to the final-layer distribution by at least 15% relative to a tuned-lens exit from x_l alone, with alpha above zero in at least two of three models. Past the final layer, some alpha in (0, 0.3] …  
Control that could embarrass it: The pair-affine upper bound. If one-parameter extrapolation captures under a third of what an unconstrained map of two layers gains, the 'converging sequence' picture adds nothing. A second control: extrapolating with a shuffled earlier layer should not help.  
Kill criterion: Fitted alpha at or below zero in two of three models, or KL reduction under 5%.  
Cost: 3-5 hours on the M1 Max, about 6 GB of checkpoints.

### Lens: old-tricks

**Noise margin: does a transformer restore its signals like a logic gate, and does one margin number predict tolerance to every inference shortcut?** (language models first; the same test on a diffusion …; wildness 3)  
Claim: A trained transformer has a per-layer noise margin: a perturbation size below which noise injected at every layer stays bounded at the output, and above which output damage grows with depth. That one number, measured with plain Gaussian noise, predicts how much activation rounding, stale cached features and token merging the model tolerates, because all of them are noise below or above the margin.  
Prediction: On Qwen3-1.7B and Llama-3.2-3B: with isotropic Gaussian noise added after every block, there is a relative amplitude e* between 1% and 10% of residual norm such that for e below e*/2 the output KL when all L blocks are noisy is less than 2 times the KL when only the last L/4 blocks are noisy …  
Control that could embarrass it: A random-weight network of the same architecture with matched activation norms. Layer norm alone gives some contraction. If the untrained network shows the same knee and the same depth independence, the effect is architecture, not learned restoration, and the …  
Kill criterion: No knee: output KL scales as a smooth power of e with the same exponent at every depth setting, or KL grows in proportion to the number of noisy blocks at all amplitudes. Or the knee exists but e* fails to rank real shortcuts (Spearman below 0.4).  
Cost: About 12 GPU-hours on the Mac for six models; downloads about 25 GB of public weights; 32 GB memory is enough at 8-bit for 8B.

**Greedy repetition loops as fixed-point limit cycles: remove the quantizer from the feedback path and the loop should die** (language models; wildness 4)  
Claim: Repetition loops under greedy decoding are the limit cycles of a recursive system with a quantizer in the loop. The argmax is the quantizer. If the model is fed the probability-weighted average embedding instead of the argmax token's embedding, cycles with period above one should disappear and the state should settle to a fixed point.  
Prediction: Take 200 prompts on which a 1B to 3B model enters a loop of period at least 2 tokens under greedy decoding within 400 tokens. Restart from 20 tokens before loop onset with soft feedback (expected embedding). Fewer than 10% show any periodic argmax sequence of period 2 or more over the next 400 …  
Control that could embarrass it: Soft feedback on prompts that do not loop. If soft feedback collapses those to a constant state just as fast, the result is 'soft inputs break the model', not 'the quantizer causes loops'. Also top-2 mixing only (average of the two best token embeddings): if …  
Kill criterion: More than 50% of looping prompts keep a period of 2 or more under soft feedback, or soft feedback degrades non-looping text equally (no difference in outcome between looping and non-looping prompts).  
Cost: 6 to 10 hours on the Mac; about 8 GB of weights; no training.  
Nearest work listed: [Xu et al., Learning to Break the Loop (NeurIPS 2022)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2206.02369), 2022-06 (not read directly); [Wang et al., Solving LLM Repetition Problem in Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04419), 2025-12 (not read directly); [The Hyperfitting Phenomenon](https://huggingface.co/papers/2412.04318), 2024-12-05 (read)

**Is word skipping in autoregressive speech models a memoryless slip process? Hazard curves for TTS failures** (speech synthesis (and Whisper-class recognition as a second …; wildness 4)  
Claim: Skips and repeats in autoregressive TTS arrive like cycle slips in a tracking loop: at a constant rate per frame that does not depend on how long the utterance has run, and that rate rises exponentially as sampling temperature rises. The usual account (the model drifts once it passes its training lengths) predicts a rising rate instead. The two accounts call for different fixes.  
Prediction: For at least two of three open autoregressive TTS models, on utterances from 5 to 60 seconds that are inside the training length range, the per-frame hazard of the first alignment error is constant within a factor of 1.5 across the utterance (Weibull shape parameter between 0.8 and 1.25), and log …  
Control that could embarrass it: Shuffle test: concatenate independently generated short clips to the same total length. That construction is memoryless by design and gives the reference curve. If real long utterances fail much more than the concatenation at the same length, drift dominates …  
Kill criterion: Weibull shape above 1.5 inside the training length range for all models (hazard rising with position), or no clean temperature law.  
Cost: Roughly 30 hours of generation on the Mac (about 36,000 clips, most short); 10 GB of weights; alignment is cheap.

**How much of the next gradient is predictable? A Kolmogorov-Szego bound on what momentum-like methods can gain** (training (small transformers, any modality); wildness 2)  
Claim: The share of the next minibatch gradient that any linear rule can predict from past gradients is fixed by the spectral flatness of the gradient time series. I expect that share to be near zero in almost all directions, so momentum there is only averaging, and large only in the few sharpest directions, where the gradient flips sign every step and is highly predictable. A standard momentum average is a worse predictor …

In the top 5 Hessian eigendirections it exceeds 30% once training reaches the edge of stability. An optimizer that subtracts the …  
Control that could embarrass it: Shuffle the minibatch order in time while keeping weights on the same trajectory (recompute gradients at stored checkpoints with fresh batches). If predictability in the sharp directions survives when the weights are frozen, it is a data-order artifact, not …

**A parity check for the residual stream: reference-free detection of inference damage from the directions the model never uses** (evaluation and inference, language and diffusion models; wildness 3)  
Claim: The low-variance directions of a layer's residual stream act like parity bits. Their energy on clean runs is small and stable, so extra energy there measures injected error without running a reference model. For errors that are close to isotropic (activation rounding, fp16 accumulation, hardware faults) this reading should predict output damage well. For weight rounding it should fail, because that error is …  
Prediction: On two LLMs of 1B to 4B, with H built from the bottom half of principal directions at a middle layer: across 40 damage settings (activation bits 3 to 8, injected Gaussian noise, random bit flips), excess syndrome energy predicts log output KL with R squared above 0.85. For weight-only rounding at 2 …  
Control that could embarrass it: Replace the learned check space with a random subspace of the same size. If a random subspace predicts damage as well, the low-variance directions are not special and total error norm is doing the work, which needs a reference. Also test on …  
Kill criterion: R squared below 0.5 for isotropic damage classes, or no gap between learned and random subspaces, or clean out-of-distribution text raises the syndrome as much as 4-bit activations do.  
Cost: 6 hours on the Mac; uses weights already downloaded for other ideas.

**Checkerboard denoising: compute half the tokens per step on alternating lattices, as consoles rendered half the pixels per frame** (image generation (extends to video); wildness 2)  
Claim: In a many-step diffusion transformer, computing only the tokens on one colour of a checkerboard at each step, alternating colours, and filling the other half from the previous step should lose less quality than choosing the same number of tokens by importance scores. The fixed lattice guarantees every token is at most one step stale and that the missing information is confined to diagonal high frequencies.

**How many bits does one token position carry? Channel capacity from measured noise tolerance, checked against how much text can be crammed into one vector** (language models; wildness 5)  
Claim: If each residual direction has a signal spread and a largest harmless noise level, the residual stream at one position is a noisy channel whose capacity follows from the classic water-filling sum. I expect this operational capacity to be a few thousand bits, far below the raw storage of the vector, and to match within a factor of two the independent limit on how many tokens can be reconstructed from a single trained …  
Prediction: For models from 0.4B to 4B, capacity at the most capacious layer is between 1,000 and 10,000 bits per position at a KL tolerance of 0.01 nats, grows roughly linearly with width, and predicts the maximum number of random-text tokens recoverable from one optimized input embedding (times the model's …  
Control that could embarrass it: Compute the same sum with tolerance replaced by a constant fraction of each direction's signal spread (so capacity is just dimension times a constant). If that trivial count predicts the cramming limit as well, the measured tolerance adds nothing.  
Kill criterion: Capacity misses the cramming limit by more than a factor of 5 in most models, or the trivial dimension count predicts it equally well.

### Lens: rate-distortion

**The search cutoff: Renyi-1/2 entropy, not perplexity, sets what tree search can extract from a language model** (language models (also ASR decoding, proof search); wildness 4)  
Claim: The expected work for an ordered tree search guided by a language model to find a target sequence grows with the Renyi entropy of order 1/2 of the model's next-token distributions, not the Shannon entropy. Between the two there is a band where the model assigns the target enough probability to be found in principle, but search cost has a Pareto tail with infinite mean. Two models with equal perplexity can therefore …  
Prediction: For recovery of text from a k-bit hash of each token with a stack search, node expansions per token follow a Pareto law with tail exponent rho solving k = E0(rho)/rho computed from the model's own logits. Median work stays under 10 nodes per token down to k near the Shannon rate, while mean work …  
Control that could embarrass it: Temperature-rescale each model so all have the same Shannon rate but different Renyi rates (and the reverse). If search cost follows the Shannon rate under this manipulation, the claim is wrong. Also, if the Renyi and Shannon rates turn out to be within 0.5 …  
Kill criterion: Measured tail exponents differ from the logit-predicted ones by more than 30 percent on real text while matching on the synthetic source, or the Renyi-Shannon gap is under 0.5 bit per token.  
Cost: About 2 days. Under 4 GB of public checkpoints. Logit dumps a few GB. No training.  
Nearest work listed: Arikan, An inequality on guessing and its application to sequential decoding, IEEE Trans. IT 1996, 1996 (not read directly); [Importance Sampling for Event Discovery via Guesswork](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24537), 2026-06-23 (read); [Estimating near-verbatim extraction risk in language models with decoding-constrained beam search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.24917), 2026-03-26 (read)

**A compressor with no model in it: hashed tokens decoded by the reader's language model** (language models, compression, assistive text entry; wildness 4)  
Claim: Text can be compressed by a device that has no language model, by storing a short hash of each token, and recovered exactly by a language model at the other end. Slepian-Wolf says the Shannon limit for this is the model's cross-entropy; the search cutoff says the practical limit is higher. The usable rate should sit near the Renyi-1/2 rate plus a small margin.  
Prediction: With a 0.5B-class model as decoder and beam width 16, exact sentence recovery exceeds 95 percent at a hash rate 1.5 bits per token above the model's measured Renyi-1/2 rate, and falls below 50 percent at 1 bit below it, while the Shannon rate is at least 2 bits lower still. A random hash of k bits …  
Control that could embarrass it: Compare against plain gzip and against LLM arithmetic coding at the same total bits. If the hashed scheme needs more bits than gzip for exact recovery, it has no use. If recovery tracks the Shannon rate closely with beam 16, the cutoff idea is wrong and plain …  
Kill criterion: The 50 percent recovery threshold sits within 0.5 bit of the Shannon rate, or at more than 4 bits above the Renyi rate.  
Cost: 2 to 3 days, no training, under 5 GB of checkpoints.  
Nearest work listed: Cai et al., Context-aware abbreviation expansion using large language models (Google, 2022), 2022 (not read directly); Deletang et al., Language modeling is compression (2023); LLMZip (2023), 2023 (not read directly)

Claim: A student cannot acquire a teacher's hidden trait from fewer tokens than the best statistical test needs to tell the teacher's outputs from the base model's. So trait transfer plotted against tokens times per-token KL divergence (teacher against base, on that data) should share one onset across data domains and trait strengths. **How many positions can one attention head address? A half-nat-per-dimension limit set by the effective dimension of its keys** (language models, long context, KV cache; wildness 3)  
Claim: For one attention head to put most of its weight on a single key among N others, ln N must stay below about half the effective dimension of that head's keys. With 64 nominal dimensions this never binds, but if the keys of retrieval heads occupy 16 to 24 effective dimensions the limit falls between a few thousand and a few hundred thousand tokens. Claim: If a conditional generator samples from the true conditional distribution, the mean squared feature distance between a real sample and a generated sample for the same condition equals the mean squared distance between two generated samples. Cost: 2 days, inference only, under 8 GB of checkpoints.
