### Lens: robs-instruments

**What stays in the recurrent state after a direction is removed from the stream** (text LLM (hybrid attention plus Gated DeltaNet); wildness 4)  
Claim: In Qwen3.5, three of four layers keep a recurrent state that is separate from the residual stream. Removing a concept direction from the stream may leave the concept readable in that state, and later layers may read it back. If so, every stream-only edit on hybrid models is leaky, and the leak grows with the share of recurrent layers.  
Prediction: After projecting the antithesis direction out of blocks 12 to 19, a linear probe on the recurrent state of DeltaNet layers 20 and above still separates antithesis from plain openings at 80% or better held-out accuracy, while the same probe on the edited residual falls to 60% or below. Also …  
Control that could embarrass it: Train the same probe on the state of a model run with shuffled labels, and on the state at attention-only layers' inputs. If shuffled-label accuracy is within 10 points of the real probe, the probe is reading state capacity, not the concept. Also a matched …  
Kill criterion: State probe accuracy after the stream edit is under 65%, or adding state projection leaves the tic rate at 0.06 to 0.10. Then the leftover 8% is not a state leak and H5 is closed.  
Cost: About 2 days: 1 day to add state capture with a parity test, a few hours of forward passes, a few hours of generation and grading. Under 12 GB memory. No downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [Residual Streams Read, Recurrent States Remember: The Global Workspace in Mamba Models (arXiv 2609.32102)](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32102), 2026-09-26 (not read directly); [Tethered Reasoning / HELIX (arXiv 2602.17691)](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17691), 2026-02-06 (not read directly); [Interpreting and Steering State-Space Models via Activation Subspace Bottlenecks (arXiv 2602.22719)](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22719), 2026-02-26 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API: 'steering AND Mamba', 'activation steering AND state space AND recurrent', 'steering AND (Mamba OR linear attention OR DeltaNet)'. Abstracts read only. No search found work on Gated DeltaNet or Qwen3.5 specifically; that absence …

**A cosine table that predicts steering side effects before any text is generated** (text LLM; wildness 2)  
Claim: The cosine between two concept directions, read from saved activations, predicts how much removing one will change the other in generated text. If the relation is steady, a table of cosines made from forward passes alone tells you which edits are safe and which will drag something else along.  
Prediction: Across the off-diagonal pairs of a 20-tic battery, the absolute cosine at block 15 and the measured cross-effect (change in rate of tic B when A is removed, as a share of B's baseline) correlate at Spearman 0.5 or higher. Pairs with absolute cosine under 0.1 show cross-effects inside the …  
Control that could embarrass it: Compute the same table at block 8, where edits were shown to do nothing. If block-8 cosines predict cross-effects as well as block-15 cosines, the cosine is tracking surface word overlap between the packs and not the mechanism.  
Kill criterion: Spearman under 0.25 across pairs, or the register-antithesis pair fails to replicate on new prompts.  
Cost: 3 to 4 days. Matrix: 1 hour. 20 arms at 96 prompts plus grading: roughly 2 days of unattended generation. No downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [STR: Supervised Transcoder Replacement for Reducing Steering Side Effects (arXiv 2609.32519)](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32519), 2026-09-26 (not read directly); [Steering Without Side Effects (KL-then-steer, arXiv 2406.15518)](http://arxiv.org/abs/2406.15518), 2024-06-21 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API: 'steering vectors AND side effects', 'steering vectors AND cosine AND side effects'. Abstracts only. I did not check the SAE literature on feature overlap, which may contain a close result.

**The sentence habit is chosen before the first word, and a word ban only moves it** (text LLM; wildness 3)  
Claim: Sentence-level habits are planned at the last prompt token, before any output word exists. A decoding rule that bans the words leaves the plan in place, so the plan comes out in a sibling form. Word-level habits have no such plan and a ban simply removes them.  
Prediction: A linear probe on the block-15 residual at the last prompt token predicts whether the coming opening will use the antithesis form at AUROC 0.75 or better on held-out prompt families. For five sentence-tier tics, a ban reroutes 20% to 50% of the removed rate into sibling forms; for five word-tier …  
Control that could embarrass it: Fit the probe on prompt text alone (bag of words on the prompt). If the text-only probe matches the activation probe, the model state adds nothing and 'planning' is just prompt wording.  
Kill criterion: Probe AUROC under 0.65 with sampling noise accounted for, or rerouted share does not differ between the two tiers by at least 15 points.  
Cost: 2 to 3 days. Probe: a few hours from saved data. Ten tics by two arms by 96 prompts: about 1.5 days unattended.  
Nearest work listed: qwen-concept-bridge tic-battery-proposal.md and decisions.md (Rob's own records), 2026-10 (read)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API: 'steering AND logit bias AND style', 'activation steering AND decoding AND style AND baseline', 'lookahead AND hidden states AND future tokens AND probe'. All returned nothing useful, which more likely reflects narrow queries …

**The draft head as a free meter for prefabricated text** (text LLM; wildness 4)  
Claim: The multi-token-prediction head guesses several tokens ahead, and the main model accepts or rejects each guess. Stretches where the guesses are accepted are stretches the model had already committed to. Machine-prose habits should sit inside those stretches, so acceptance rate is a per-token score for canned text that costs nothing to log. A model edited to drop a habit should decode measurably slower.  
Prediction: Tokens inside labelled antithesis constructions are accepted at depth 3 at 85% or more, against 61.5% overall. After the projection edit, mean depth-3 acceptance on the same prompts falls by at least 5 points and MTP decode speed falls by 3% to 8%. A per-text acceptance score separates Rob's graded …  
Control that could embarrass it: Match each tic span with a non-tic span of the same length and the same mean token probability under the main model. If acceptance is equal after matching, the head only reports ordinary predictability and adds nothing beyond perplexity.  
Kill criterion: Acceptance inside tic spans is within 5 points of matched spans, or the AUROC is under 0.60.  
Cost: 2 days. The logging hook is the main work; the runs are under 2 hours. One model already on disk. The steering arm on the 9B needs the capture harness ported, or the 4B MTP head if it exists in MLX.  
Nearest work listed: [AdaMTP: An Adaptive Training Paradigm for Multi-Token Prediction (arXiv 2608.00434)](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00434), 2026-08-01 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API: 'multi-token prediction AND uncertainty', 'multi-token prediction AND (uncertainty OR probe OR quantization)', 'speculative decoding AND acceptance rate AND quantization AND detect'. One relevant hit. Detection of machine text …

**What a voice adapter learns when the audio codebooks are fed in the wrong order** (speech (autoregressive TTS); wildness 3)  
Claim: An Orpheus adapter trained with scrambled codebook order still produces speech but stops following the text. That suggests voice character is learned from which tokens appear, and word content from the order they appear in. A planned set of scrambles would show which codebook level ties speech to text.  
Prediction: Speaker-embedding similarity of the legacy adapter to 13 held-out clips is within 0.05 cosine of the corrected adapter, while its word error rate is above 50% against under 10% for the corrected one. In a planned ladder, swapping only the two finest codebook levels raises word error by under 3 …  
Control that could embarrass it: Run the base model with no adapter through the same scoring. If the legacy adapter's speaker similarity is no closer to the target than the base model's default voice, it learned nothing about the voice and the whole reading fails.  
Kill criterion: Legacy adapter speaker similarity is more than 0.15 below the corrected adapter, or the fine-level swap breaks word accuracy as badly as the coarse swap.  
Cost: Half a day for the existing-adapter measurement. 1.5 days for the four training runs at roughly an hour each plus scoring. Memory within what the recipe already uses.  
Nearest work listed: [Stack-and-Delay: a new codebook pattern for music generation (arXiv 2309.08804)](http://arxiv.org/abs/2309.08804), 2023-09-15 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API: 'codebook AND delay pattern AND speech'. One query, one loosely related hit. Thin; the codec-language-model literature should be searched properly before this runs.

**Speech grading by transcription cannot hear breath** (speech (TTS evaluation); wildness 2)  
Claim: The usual score for a speech model is the word error rate from a recognizer, often behind a voice-activity filter. That filter treats breathy or soft speech as silence. So the score rewards models for flattening expressive voices, and picking checkpoints by that score moves a cloned voice away from the person.  
Prediction: Across the three registered checkpoints of each of three adapters plus at least four VoiceStudio engines, the rank by word error rate and the rank by a breathiness measure (cepstral peak prominence, lower means breathier) correlate at Spearman -0.5 or stronger: the better the score, the less …  
Control that could embarrass it: Add white noise to modal (non-breathy) speech to the same harmonics-to-noise level. If the filter drops that too, the effect is plain noise sensitivity and says nothing special about breathy voice.  
Kill criterion: Spearman weaker than -0.2, or Rob's blind likeness ranking agrees with the word-error ranking in more than 70% of pairs.  
Cost: 1.5 days. Generation about 3 hours, scoring 1 hour, 20 minutes of Rob's listening. No new downloads if the VoiceStudio engines are already installed; that was not verified.  
Nearest work listed: [Whisper-Aware LLM: Self-Supervised Uncertainty Learning for Robust Whispered Speech Recognition (arXiv …](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10836), 2026-08-11 (not read directly); [AISHELL6-whisper (arXiv 2509.23833)](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23833), 2025-09-28 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API: 'whispered speech AND voice activity', 'whispered speech AND Whisper AND recognition'. Abstracts only. I did not search the TTS-evaluation literature directly, so a paper on WER bias against expressive speech may exist.

**Calibrated damage shows what a small vision judge is really looking at** (vision (zero-shot VLM judge versus few-shot embedding heads); wildness 2)  
Claim: A 9B zero-shot judge scored the same on full-size and 768 px frames. I think it decides from coarse layout and ignores detail, while a few-shot head on DINOv2 features uses detail. If so they should fail under different damage, their errors should be nearly unrelated, and a simple average should beat both.  
Prediction: The judge's pooled AUROC stays within 0.02 of 0.745 down to 384 px and drops by more than 0.05 only below 256 px. The heads lose more than 0.05 already at the photo320 damage. Error correlation between the two on the 134 labelled cells is 0.3 or lower, and the mean of their calibrated scores …  
Control that could embarrass it: Score a blank grey frame and a frame from the wrong video with the same tag question. If the judge's AUROC on real frames is not clearly above what tag base rates alone give, it was using the question and not the image.  
Kill criterion: Judge AUROC falls by more than 0.05 at 384 px, or the stacked AUROC is under 0.78, or error correlation is above 0.6.  
Cost: 1 day. About 71 targets by 9 versions by 4 s for the judge is under 1 hour; heads are instant. No downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [WinCLIP: Zero-/Few-Shot Anomaly Classification and Segmentation (arXiv 2303.14814)](http://arxiv.org/abs/2303.14814), 2023-03-26 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API: 'zero-shot AND few-shot AND CLIP AND ensemble AND complementary', 'zero-shot AND linear probe AND complementary AND vision-language'. Abstracts only. Combining zero-shot and few-shot scores is well known for CLIP; the …

**How much of taste is the prompt and how much is the draw** (image generation (preference data plus text-encoder …; wildness 4)  
Claim: Rob's archive records every generation with its full prompt and whether he kept it. I think whether an image is kept can be partly predicted from the prompt alone, using a single direction in a text encoder's activations. The part that cannot be predicted from the prompt is the luck of the draw, and the archive can put a number on that split.  
Prediction: A kept-minus-discarded direction in the text encoder's mid layers predicts kept status on held-out prompt families at AUROC 0.60 to 0.70. Among prompts that were generated more than once, differences between draws of the same prompt account for at least half of the variance in kept status.  
Control that could embarrass it: A predictor using only prompt length, date and model version. Taste and habits drift over time, and newer models are kept more often. If that baseline reaches the same AUROC, the direction has found the calendar, not taste.  
Kill criterion: Held-out AUROC under 0.57, or no better than the length-and-date baseline by at least 0.03.  
Cost: Step 1: 1 hour. Step 2: half a day of forward passes on a model already on disk. Step 3: 1 day plus 20 minutes of Rob's rating. No downloads.  
Nearest work listed: none listed  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API: 'human preference AND text-to-image AND personalized AND single user' returned nothing. Public preference predictors trained on crowd ratings exist and were not reviewed here. Treat prior art as unchecked.

### Lens: robs-results

**Does the wide-K penalty cost N times K squared? An M and N sweep that separates a hidden re-copy from a cache effect** (kernels / MPS performance; wildness 2)  
Claim: The slowdown of MPS matmul with a transposed right-hand weight is extra work whose size depends on N and K but not on M. If so, the slowdown ratio minus one should scale like K divided by M. A cache or page-stride explanation predicts a ratio that does not depend on M.  
Prediction: At K=32768, N=6144, fp16: halving M from 4608 to 2304 raises the ratio from about 3.5x to about 6x (excess 2.5 becomes about 5). Doubling M to 9216 lowers it to about 2.3x. Under the cache explanation the ratio stays within 10% of 3.5x at all three M.  
Control that could embarrass it: If the excess time scales with M (the b term carries it), the re-copy story is wrong and the penalty is inside the GEMM inner loop. Also check that small M does not switch PyTorch to a different path; the issue says single-token decode is a different path, so …  
Kill criterion: Ratio at M=2304 and M=9216 both within 10% of the M=4608 ratio at K=32768.  
Cost: About 30 minutes of GPU on the Mac Studio, under 6 GB memory in fp16 and under 8 GB in fp32 at the largest shape. No downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [pytorch/pytorch#200782 (Rob's own issue, filed 2026-10-13)](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues/200782), 2026-10-13 (read); [pytorch/pytorch#198182](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues/198182), 2026-09-22 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: None beyond Rob's own duplicate triage in docs/upstream/pytorch-mps-wide-linear-issue.md (about 15 gh searches, refreshed 2026-10-09). I ran no new search for this idea.

**Recover the hidden summation tree of MPS GEMM and test whether it changes where the speed changes** (kernels / numerics; wildness 3)  
Claim: The 19 structured fp16 results that match no simulated summation order come from a small number of multiway summation trees that a black-box probe can recover. The tree's block width changes at the same shapes where MPS changes speed: the wide-K onset, head dimension 160, and K above 32768.  
Prediction: At most 4 distinct trees explain all 66 structured cases. At least one tree boundary falls between K=4096 and K=8192 in fp16 with a transposed weight, and the tree at head dimension 160 differs from the tree at 128.  
Control that could embarrass it: Run the same probe on CPU fp32 with a known sequential loop and on Accelerate; the probe must recover the known order. If the MPS tree is the same everywhere while speed changes by 3x, the fingerprint idea is wrong.  
Kill criterion: More than 8 distinct trees, or no tree change at any of the three speed boundaries.  
Cost: One to two days to adapt and validate the probe, under one hour of GPU, under 4 GB. Download: the FPRev source, a few hundred KB.  
Nearest work listed: [Xie et al., Revealing Floating-Point Accumulation Orders in Software/Hardware Implementations (FPRev), USENIX …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.00442), 2024-11-01, v3 2025-07-01 (read)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch ('FPRev revealing floating-point accumulation order summation tree numerical libraries GPU matmul') and one WebFetch of the arXiv abstract. arXiv API and Semantic Scholar queries failed (empty result and HTTP 429). I did not …

**One statistic per layer should say where rotation helps and which 4-bit format wins; Wan and Qwen disagree and nobody knows why** (quantization / images and video; wildness 2)  
Claim: Whether Hadamard rotation lowers a layer's error, and whether W4A8 beats Q4_K on that layer, is decided by how far the layer's weights are from Gaussian inside the grouping unit. A crest factor (row or group maximum over rms) measured before rotation should predict both outcomes. The Wan and Qwen split then becomes a statement about how the two families distribute weight energy.  
Prediction: Row crest factor before rotation, compared with the Gaussian expectation for that K, predicts the sign of rotation's effect in at least 90% of the 505 layers. The crest factor inside groups of 16 predicts the W4A8 against Q4_K winner in at least 80% of layers.  
Control that could embarrass it: A lookup on layer type and width alone (attention or MLP, K) may predict the outcomes just as well. If it does, the statistic adds nothing and the split is architecture bookkeeping.  
Kill criterion: Cross-model accuracy of the single statistic below 75% for rotation sign, or no better than the layer-type lookup.  
Cost: About 3 to 4 CPU hours, under 16 GB memory, no downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [Rob's weight-error-by-format note](docs/research/2026-11-09-weight-error-by-format.md), 2026-11-09 (read); QuIP / QuIP# incoherence processing (2023 to 2024) (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: None in this pass. Round one already ran the white-error test idea; I checked its title to avoid overlap. This idea is about predicting which layers benefit, not about the error being white.

**Is the unexplained 5 to 8% kernel drift a GPU frequency governor reacting to idle gaps?** (kernels / benchmarking method; wildness 3)  
Claim: The packed kernel path leaves short idle gaps between Metal passes that the stock path does not. The Apple GPU governor lowers frequency in those gaps. That would explain a slowdown that appears in full generation but not in the tight microbenchmark, and a fixed per-step cost larger than the fit predicted.  
Prediction: During a kernel generation run the GPU spends at least 10% of active time below its top frequency state; during the microbenchmark, under 2%. A background keep-warm load that fills the gaps recovers at least 4 points of the 5 to 8% drift.  
Control that could embarrass it: If frequency residency is the same in the kernel run and the microbenchmark, the governor is not the cause. Then the next suspects are per-step Python and MPSGraph compile caching on macOS 27, and the experiment should say so.  
Kill criterion: Top-state residency within 2 points across the kernel run and the microbenchmark, or keep-warm recovers under 1 point.  
Cost: About 2 hours including tool setup, 16 GB for Krea at 512, no downloads beyond macmon.  
Nearest work listed: none listed  
Author's note on the search: None. I did not search for Apple GPU frequency scaling and benchmark bias. This is unchecked.

**Base weights carry a small LoRA through the quantizer like dither, and there is a strength below which merged adapters silently vanish** (quantization and adapters / images, likely LLMs; wildness 3)  
Claim: When a LoRA far smaller than the quantization step is merged and requantized, it survives because the base weights spread it across many rounding decisions. The survival is statistical, so there is a predictable threshold set by adapter norm, rank, layer width and bit depth. Below it the merged adapter is lost even though the file loads and renders.  
Prediction: On real Krea or H3 weights with a real identity LoRA at 6 bits, the regression coefficient lies in 0.95 to 1.05. Identity similarity of merged renders stays within 10% of the side path at strength 1.0 and 6 bits, and loses more than half of the LoRA's identity gain at strength 0.3 and 4 bits. The …  
Control that could embarrass it: Merge a random matrix with the same norm and rank as the LoRA. If side-against-merge PSNR is the same 17.8 dB, that figure is only redrawn rounding noise and says nothing about the adapter. Also, if identity survives at every grid point, the threshold is …  
Kill criterion: Stage 1 coefficient outside 0.8 to 1.2 on real weights, or stage 2 shows no identity loss at 4 bits and strength 0.25.  
Cost: Stage 1: about 1 CPU hour, under 16 GB. Stage 2: 12 grid points x 4 seeds x 2 paths at 512, about 2 GPU hours on Krea Turbo. No downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [arXiv 2609.18063, The Other Half of the Memory Wall (Edge0)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18063), 2026-09-16 (not read directly); QA-LoRA (Xu et al., 2023) (not read directly); [The Kaitchup, 'Don't merge your LoRA adapter into a 4-bit model'](https://kaitchup.substack.com/p/dont-merge-your-lora-adapter-into) (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch ('merging LoRA into quantized base model requantization loses adapter rounding LoRA merge quantized weights 2026') and one WebFetch of arXiv 2609.18063. arXiv API query returned nothing. I found warnings that merging hurts, …

**Long shot: the size of the negative conditioning acts as a hidden noise-level signal, which is why zeroed negatives make grain** (image generation / guidance; wildness 4)  
Claim: The model reads the overall size of its text conditioning as information about how confidently to denoise. A zeroed negative makes the unconditional branch predict a different amount of fine detail than the conditional branch. Guidance then multiplies that difference, and it shows as grain.  
Prediction: With negative = a times the encoded empty prompt, output power above half the Nyquist frequency rises steadily as a goes from 1 to 0, reaching at least 2x at a=0. The excess scales with (CFG - 1) within 20% across CFG 2, 3.5 and 5. A random perturbation of the negative with the same norm change …  
Control that could embarrass it: The random-direction perturbation at equal norm. If it produces as much grain as shrinking the norm, the model is not reading the norm; any departure from the trained null does it, and the boring explanation wins.  
Kill criterion: High-frequency power is not monotone in a, or the random control gives at least 80% of the excess.  
Cost: About 144 renders. At roughly 7.5 s per guided step and 28 steps this is about 8 GPU hours at 512; a quarter of the grid in 2 hours is enough for a first answer. 16 GB. No downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [ComfyUI ConditioningZeroOut documentation](https://comfy.icu/node/ConditioningZeroOut) (not read directly); [Guiding Diffusion Models with Semantically Degraded Conditions (CVPR 2026, arXiv 2603.10780)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10780), 2026-03 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch ('zeroed negative conditioning vs encoded empty prompt classifier-free guidance grain artifacts flow matching DiT unconditional embedding norm'). The search found nothing on grain or on norm as a signal. No paper was opened.

**Long shot: step distillation narrows the range of clip shapes a video model can make, and the floor is set by latent frames, not tokens** (video generation; wildness 4)  
Claim: The small Wan recipes that came out corrupted fail because the few-step path only works for shapes close to what it was distilled on. The base model at many steps would be fine at the same shapes. The failure tracks the number of latent frames more than the number of tokens.  
Prediction: With the step-distill weights off and 30 steps, at least 3 of the 4 recipes are coherent. With distillation on, clips with 5 latent frames fail at both areas and clips with 9 or more pass at 704x416. Lowering the schedule shift from 5 to 2 fixes fewer than half of the failing seeds.  
Control that could embarrass it: If the base model at 30 steps is also corrupted at 17 frames, distillation is not the cause and the floor belongs to the base model or the VAE's first-frame handling. If shift 2 fixes most failures, it is a schedule mismatch and not a shape range.  
Kill criterion: Base at 30 steps coherent in fewer than 2 of the 4 recipes, or shift 2 repairs more than half of failing seeds.  
Cost: Distilled arm: 64 short clips, about 1 GPU hour. Base arm at 30 steps: about 6 to 8 GPU hours at these small sizes. Under 20 GB. No downloads if both weights are already on disk.  
Nearest work listed: [LightX2V step distillation docs](https://lightx2v-en.readthedocs.io/en/latest/_sources/method_tutorials/step_distill.md) (not read directly); [Adaptive Video Distillation (CVPR 2026)](https://cvpr.thecvf.com/virtual/2026/poster/37903), 2026 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch ('step-distilled video diffusion model fails at low resolution or few frames, timestep shift token count, Wan 2.2 lightx2v short clips artifacts'). It found no report on shape range. No paper was opened.

### Lens: speech-and-audio-as-modelling-problems

**Late turn-taking in duplex speech models may be a temperature artefact: the decision to start speaking is a rare event, and sampling below 1 delays it** (conversational / full-duplex speech; wildness 3)  
Claim: Moshi-family models decide at each 80 ms frame whether to emit a first word or a padding token. Sampling the text stream at temperature 0.7 shrinks the small per-frame probability of starting, and shrinks it more when that probability is spread over many possible first words. A model whose underlying timing is human-like would then show the late, reaction-like turn starts that were reported in October 2026 and read …  
Prediction: Read the model's own per-frame start probability at T=1 after each user turn end: the implied median gap is under 250 ms. Sampling only the pad-versus-speak choice at T=1 (word choice still at 0.7) cuts the measured median gap by at least 40% (for example 480 ms to under 290 ms) and raises the …  
Control that could embarrass it: If the T=1 start probabilities read straight from the logits already imply a median above 400 ms, the model itself is late and the sampler is innocent. Second check: the paper reports 18% overlapped transfers, and my toy predicts 2-6% at T=0.7, so the deep …  
Kill criterion: Implied T=1 median gap above 400 ms, or less than a 15% change in measured median gap when only the pad/speak temperature moves from 0.7 to 1.0.  
Cost: One 5-8 GB model download; about 2 hours for the logit-reading pass on the Mac; 1-2 days for the sampler change and timing extraction. No training.  
Nearest work listed: [Coupled but Late: Turn-Taking Between Full-Duplex Speech Models in Unscripted Dialogue, arXiv 2610.08683](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08683), 2026-10-06 (read); [Reference-Guided Turn-Taking Style Transfer for Full-Duplex Spoken Language Models, arXiv 2610.20763](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20763), 2026-10-14 (read); [D2: High-Capability Full-Duplex Spoken Language Models from Half-Duplex Backbones, arXiv 2610.17124](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17124), 2026-10-12 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv API: full-duplex AND turn-taking (10 newest); (Moshi OR full-duplex) AND temperature AND latency (rate-limited, no result). One WebSearch on Moshi temperature and latency: nothing found. Grepped the HTML of 2610.08683 for …

**How far ahead does a speech model plan its melody? A paired-noise measurement of prosodic look-ahead, with a cross-language prediction** (speech synthesis (autoregressive codec-token models); wildness 3)  
Claim: An autoregressive TTS model sees the whole sentence, but how much of the sentence it uses at each moment is unknown. I predict it plans sentence length from the first frame (higher starting pitch and a longer pre-speech breath for longer sentences) but marks English questions only in the last few words, while marking Spanish or French yes/no questions from the first half second, as speakers of those languages do.  
Prediction: With shared sampling noise, changing final '.' to '?' in English leaves the token stream identical to a neutral-edit baseline until the last 3 words in at least 80% of pairs. In Spanish, at least 30% more pairs than baseline diverge within the first 500 ms. Doubling sentence length by appending a …  
Control that could embarrass it: A null pair where the late edit is prosodically neutral (a synonym of the same length at the same position). Near-ties in sampling can flip an early token for no reason, so every effect is measured as excess over this null. If the question-mark pairs diverge …  
Kill criterion: Length effect on first-word pitch under 0.2 semitones and early-divergence excess under 5% in all three languages.  
Cost: No new downloads for Orpheus; about 2 GB for CSM. Roughly 4,000 short generations, 6-10 hours on the Mac. Analysis on CPU.  
Nearest work listed: [Steering intonational tunes in text-to-speech synthesis, arXiv 2610.27403](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27403), 2026-10-19 (read); [Can Prosodic Style Be Inferred from Text Alone?, arXiv 2610.05575](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05575), 2026-10-04 (read); [GEPARD: streaming autoregressive TTS for realtime dialogue, arXiv 2609.04222](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04222), 2026-07-04 (not read directly)

**Flow-matching speech decides word timing in the first fifth of the flow: text guidance is only needed until then** (speech synthesis (flow-matching, in-context models such as …; wildness 2)  
Claim: In a model that learns alignment with no aligner, the placement of words in time is fixed very early in sampling, pitch is fixed in the middle, and fine spectrum last. If so, the text condition and its guidance pass do useful work only before the timing is fixed. Guidance can be dropped after that point with almost no loss, and the early steps could run at a coarser time resolution.  
Prediction: In F5-TTS, word boundaries from a forced alignment of the one-step clean estimate are within 30 ms of their final positions for 90% of words by flow time 0.2 (noise at 0, data at 1). Pitch-contour correlation with the final output exceeds 0.9 by 0.5. Applying text guidance only for t < 0.3 raises …  
Control that could embarrass it: The late-only schedule at matched cost. If late-only guidance does as well as early-only, the commit-time story is wrong and guidance is doing something other than fixing alignment. Also check a second model with a different mechanism (CosyVoice 2's flow …  
Kill criterion: Boundary error still above 60 ms for more than 30% of words at t = 0.4, or early-only guidance worse than full guidance by more than 1 point of word error rate.  
Cost: About 1.5 GB download. 300 prompts x 4 schedules plus trajectory dumps: 4-6 hours on the Mac. The training stage would need a free-tier GPU for a day or more and is optional.

**The guidance scale is a clear-speech dial: guided flow-matching TTS over-articulates the way people do for a listener who cannot hear well** (speech synthesis (flow-matching and diffusion TTS); wildness 3)  
Claim: Guidance pushes each sound away from what the model would produce without the text. Phonetically that widens the vowel space, strengthens consonant bursts and widens pitch range, which is what human clear speech does. So default guidance settings make these models sound like careful reading, and the same dial is a free intelligibility-in-noise control.  
Prediction: In F5-TTS, the area of the corner-vowel triangle in formant space grows by at least 15% from guidance weight 1 to 3, on vowels that are correctly recognised at both settings. Pitch range grows by at least 1 semitone. The signal-to-noise ratio at which a recogniser gets half the words right improves …  
Control that could embarrass it: At low guidance the model also makes outright errors, which would shrink the measured vowel space for a boring reason. Restricting to vowels correct at both settings handles that. The sharper control is splitting guidance into text and speaker parts (the …  
Kill criterion: Vowel triangle area changes by less than 5% between weights 1.5 and 3 on matched correct vowels, or the change is equal under speaker-only guidance.  
Cost: No new downloads if idea 3 has run. About 5,000 short generations, 6-8 hours on the Mac; formant analysis on CPU in under an hour.

**The price of not waiting: how much of a word is recognised only after it ends, and predicting that share from text alone** (streaming speech recognition; wildness 2)  
Claim: The benefit of look-ahead in streaming recognition has two parts with different time scales. A fast part of about 100 ms is acoustic: the end of a sound is shaped by the next one. A slow part of about half a second is lexical: some words, mostly short function words, cannot be identified until the next word arrives. The slow part can be predicted for each word from a text model with no audio, so a streaming system …  
Prediction: Truncate audio exactly at a word's end and decode with Whisper small: 20-35% of words that are eventually correct are wrong or missing. The deficit against look-ahead L fits two exponentials with time constants 80-120 ms and 400-700 ms. Beyond 200 ms the slow part is at least 30% of the remaining …  
Control that could embarrass it: Replace the audio after the word with unrelated speech from the same speaker, or with room tone, of the same length. If recognition of the word recovers just as much with unrelated continuation, the gain comes from not ending the file abruptly, not from …  
Kill criterion: Unrelated continuation recovers more than 70% of the benefit of the true continuation at L = 640 ms, or the text-only score has AUC under 0.6.  
Cost: No new downloads beyond Whisper weights already in use and alignments (a few hundred MB). 5,000 words x 7 delays x 3 models of short clips: about 8-12 hours on the Mac.

**An exchange rate between context and sound: how many decibels a name's improbability costs, and how many a prompt mention buys back** (speech recognition (names and rare words); wildness 2)  
Claim: Whether a recogniser gets a rare name right is a contest between acoustic evidence and its text prior. I predict the contest is linear: each unit of prior disadvantage against the nearest common competitor costs a fixed number of decibels of noise tolerance, and one mention of the name in the prompt buys back a fixed number, independent of the audio. Whisper should weight its prior more heavily than a …

One prompt mention is worth 6 to 10 dB; a second adds less than 2 dB. Fitting correct-versus-competitor on acoustic margin and prior …

**Long shot: generated music prefers tempos whose beat is a whole number of token frames** (music generation (autoregressive codec-token models); wildness 5)  
Claim: A token model keeps a steady beat by attending to what happened one beat ago and repeating it. That copy is exact only when the beat lasts a whole number of frames. So a 50-frames-per-second model should favour tempos of 3000/k beats per minute (136.4, 130.4, 125, 120, 115.4, 111.1), and requested tempos in between should be pulled to the nearest of these.  
Prediction: Among MusicGen-small clips with a stable beat, the share whose beat period lies within 0.1 frame of a whole or half number of 20 ms frames is at least 1.5 times the share for real music of matched genre passed through the same codec and beat tracker. Requesting 100 to 140 BPM in 1 BPM steps gives a …  
Control that could embarrass it: Produced music clusters at whole-number BPMs such as 120 and 125, which are also whole-frame periods. The test therefore rests on non-integer comb values: do clips land on 130.43 and 115.38 more than on 130, 128 and 115? If the excess is only at 120 and 125, …  
Kill criterion: Excess under 1.1x with 1,000+ clips, or all excess confined to tempos that are also integer BPM.

**Long shot: keep the pauses. A small language model trained on transcripts with timing marks learns syntax from fewer words** (language model training, using speech timing as supervision; wildness 4)  
Claim: Pauses, word lengthening and pitch resets mark phrase boundaries in speech. A small language model trained on transcripts that keep these marks as extra tokens should learn syntax from fewer words than the same model trained on bare text. This is the infant 'prosodic bootstrapping' hypothesis put to a direct test.  
Prediction: A 10-million-word model trained with quantised pause and final-lengthening tokens reaches the syntax-test accuracy (BLiMP, scored on plain text with the marks removed or marginalised) of a text-only model trained on 1.5 to 2 times as many words. ### Lens: statistical-physics

**The variance of distractor logits sets the context length where an attention head loses its target** (text (any attention model); wildness 2)  
Claim: A retrieval head puts a logit gap D on the right key and logits with standard deviation s on the n wrong keys. The head's weight on the right key should fall below one half at ln n* = D - s^2/2 when D >= s^2, and at ln n* = D^2/(2 s^2) when D < s^2. Both numbers can be measured at short context and used to predict the failure length at long context.  
Prediction: For heads with D >= s^2 measured at 512 tokens, the measured ln n* matches D - s^2/2 within 0.5 nats for at least 70 percent of heads. The rule that ignores variance (ln n* = D) overshoots by s^2/2, a factor of about 7 in n when s = 2. My simulation with Gaussian logits: (D=6, s=2) predicted 4.0, …  
Control that could embarrass it: Fit the same data with the range-only bound from Velickovic et al. and with ln n* = D. If either predicts n* as well as the variance rule, the physics adds nothing. Also replace the Gaussian assumption with the measured histogram: if the empirical-histogram …  
Kill criterion: Dead if the median absolute error of the two-number rule exceeds 1 nat, or if D grows with ln n at slope near 1 in all tested models, since then there is no failure length to predict. The second outcome is itself worth reporting.  
Cost: About 6 to 10 hours on the Mac Studio. Downloads about 5 GB of small models. Under 16 GB memory.  
Nearest work listed: [Velickovic et al., softmax is not enough for sharp out-of-distribution, arXiv 2410.01104](https://arxiv.org/html/2410.01104), 2024-10 (read); ASEntmax, arXiv 2506.16640, 2025-06 (not read directly); arXiv 2508.17821 and 2606.22325 (attention dispersion at length) (not read directly); Scalable-Softmax (logits scaled by log n) (not read directly)

**One exponent identity linking gradient-influence decay to the correlation decay of sampled text** (text; wildness 4)  
Claim: Three published power laws about language models may be one law. If the decay exponent p of gradient influence with distance plays the role of the coupling exponent in a one-dimensional long-range spin glass, then at the temperature-one transition the squared correlation of sampled text decays with exponent 2 - 2p and the plain correlation with about 1 - p. Models with longer-range influence should then show …  
Prediction: Using p from arXiv 2606.29139: Pythia-160M p = 0.819 gives exponent 0.18 (Nakaishi et al. report about 0.2). Pythia-70M p = 0.882 gives 0.12. Pythia-410M p = 0.715 gives 0.29. Qwen2.5-0.5B p = 0.881 gives 0.12. All measured p values fall in 2/3 < sigma < 1, the non-mean-field range. Secondary …  
Control that could embarrass it: Human text has its own correlation exponent. If all models show the same exponent as the training corpus regardless of p, then the exponent comes from the data and p is irrelevant. Also 2606.29139 finds p = 0.837 on shuffled text, close to the natural-text …  
Kill criterion: Dead if the 410M and 70M correlation exponents differ by less than 0.05 when the rule says they differ by 0.17, or if the ordering is reversed.  
Cost: Zero to check the appendix. Otherwise about 8 hours on the Mac Studio, 2 GB of downloads.

**A fluctuation-response ratio for diffusion denoisers, measured per direction and per noise level** (image (applies to audio and video diffusion); wildness 3)  
Claim: For an exact denoiser, the Jacobian along a direction v equals the posterior variance of v·x0 given the noisy input, divided by the noise variance. Define X as the ratio of the two, with the variance taken from the model's own sampler started at that noisy input. X should be 1 for a self-consistent model. Departures locate, by direction and noise level, where the model's response and its own samples disagree.  
Prediction: These are guesses to be fixed before running. In a well-trained small pixel model, X lies within 0.9 to 1.1 for high-frequency directions at all noise levels. For the five lowest-frequency directions X departs by more than 30 percent in a band of noise levels around where class identity is decided. …  
Control that could embarrass it: Run the whole pipeline on an analytic Gaussian-mixture denoiser where X = 1 exactly. If the estimator does not return 1 within its error bar there, nothing else can be trusted. Second control: double the sampler steps; if the departures shrink toward zero, …  
Kill criterion: Dead if X stays within 0.9 to 1.1 everywhere, including under guidance, once sampler steps are converged. Also dead as a diagnostic if X does not correlate (rank correlation below 0.3) with any sample-quality measure across checkpoints.  
Cost: About 1 to 2 days on the Mac Studio: roughly 50 x 12 x 64 short sampler runs. Under 8 GB memory. Download under 1 GB.  
Nearest work listed: arXiv 2609.36210, Jacobian spectra of diffusion denoisers, 2026-09 (not read directly); Manor and Michaeli, On the posterior distribution in denoising, arXiv 2309.13598, 2023-09 (not read directly); Free Hunch, arXiv 2410.11149; also 2605.00941 and 2608.21972 (not read directly); Ambrogioni, arXiv 2603.20092, 2026-03 (not read directly)

**Alignment paths over uninformative audio should wander as length to the two-thirds power** (audio (speech alignment); wildness 4)  
Claim: A Viterbi or DTW alignment is the best path through a grid of noisy scores. Over a stretch of L frames where the acoustics do not pin the path, the gap between the found boundaries and the true ones should grow as L^(2/3), not L^(1/2). Timing error over a gap then grows faster than a random-walk estimate says, and anchor spacing should be set accordingly.  
Prediction: On synthetic independent scores the exponent is 0.67 plus or minus 0.05 for L from 50 to 5,000 frames. On real CTC emissions with a masked or noise-buried stretch, the exponent is between 0.6 and 0.75 for L beyond about 10 correlation lengths. Score fluctuations of the best path grow with exponent …  
Control that could embarrass it: Shuffle frames inside the masked stretch to remove time correlation. If the exponent is 2/3 only after shuffling and 1/2 or something else on raw emissions, the law does not apply to real audio at practical lengths. Also set all scores in the stretch equal …  
Kill criterion: Dead if the real-emission exponent is below 0.55 across all tested lengths up to 5,000 frames, or if no clean power law appears over at least one decade.  
Cost: Stage one about 1 hour CPU. Stage two about 4 hours on the Mac Studio, 1 GB model download plus LibriSpeech test-clean (350 MB).  
Nearest work listed: Hwa and Lassig, Similarity detection and localization, Phys. Rev. Lett. 1996 (not read directly); Kim and Lamacraft, planted directed polymer, arXiv 2404.07263, 2024-04 (not read directly); Basu, Sidoravicius, Sly, slow bond problem in last-passage percolation (not read directly); arXiv 2406.19363, 2609.33650, 2610.22583 (forced alignment comparisons and robustness) (not read directly)

**How long a text-carried attribute survives in a sliding window: an exponential law with a threshold** (text, with the same form for streaming speech and …; wildness 3)  
Claim: When a model generates with a sliding window, an attribute held only by the window contents (language, speaker, topic, style) should persist for a time that grows exponentially with window width W. The rate per token depends only on the logit bias toward the attribute divided by temperature. Below a bias of about 2 there should be no persistent state at all, however wide the window.  
Prediction: Rate per token of window: 0.089 at a = 3, 0.273 at a = 4, 0.724 at a = 6, and zero for a at or below 2. My simulation at a = 3 gave slopes 0.196, 0.166, 0.145 for W from 8 to 32, falling toward 0.089, so finite-W corrections are large below W of about 100. Doubling temperature at a = 6 should cut …  
Control that could embarrass it: Measure the flip probability as a function of the fraction of dissenting tokens. If it is not close to the logistic form, or depends mostly on the last few tokens, the mean-field model is wrong and the fit of ln tau against W would be a coincidence. Also …  
Kill criterion: Dead if ln tau is not linear in W over W = 64 to 256 at fixed temperature, or if the measured slope differs from the integral by more than a factor of 2 at two or more temperatures.  
Cost: About 1 day on the Mac Studio. Lifetimes get long fast, so high-bias points need the short-W end only. 1 GB download.

Author's note on the search: Hugging Face paper search: 'language drift sliding context window persistence exponential', 'context drift long generation'. Rate-limited queries not completed: 'path dependence in-context learning'. Streaming TTS and autoregressive video …

**The temperature where sampled text falls apart is where one bad token causes one more** (text; wildness 4)  
Claim: Insert one off-distribution token into a context and count the extra off-distribution tokens it causes downstream. Call that average m. The temperature at which sampled text stops being coherent should be the temperature at which m reaches 1. Near that temperature, runs of incoherent tokens should have sizes that follow a power law with exponent 3/2.  
Prediction: For Pythia-160M the temperature where m = 1 lies within 0.1 of the transition temperature near 1 reported by Nakaishi et al. Burst-size exponent at that temperature is 1.5 plus or minus 0.2. The cutoff burst size scales as (1-m)^-2 below it, within a factor of 2 over m from 0.5 to 0.9.  
Control that could embarrass it: Vary the quantile that defines a bad token. If the temperature where m = 1 moves by more than 0.1, the threshold is a product of the definition. Also, the m = 1 point must be compared with the simple single-token estimate; if the single-token error rate alone …  
Kill criterion: Dead if m stays below 0.5 up to the temperature where text is plainly incoherent, or if burst sizes are exponential with no power-law range of at least one decade.  
Cost: About 1 day on the Mac Studio. 1 GB download.

### Lens: tiny-model-science

**Voice and persona drift as an urn process: bounded when the prompt stays in view, relaxing to the average when it leaves, collapsing below temperature one** (speech synthesis and language models (toy sequence model …; wildness 3)  
Claim: A model that infers a hidden speaker or persona from context and then conditions on its own samples behaves like a Polya urn. If it is close to Bayes-optimal and the prompt stays in its window, drift is bounded by the uncertainty left at the end of the prompt. Unbounded drift needs a finite window or a sampling temperature other than one, and each leaves a different, measurable signature.  
Prediction: In a 4-layer transformer trained on sequences drawn from a per-sequence hidden distribution: (a) with the prompt pinned in context, variance of the inferred latent saturates within 20% of the Bayes posterior variance at prompt end (simulation: 0.339 vs 0.333 for an 8-token prompt); (b) with a …  
Control that could embarrass it: Replace self-generated continuation with real continuation from the same latent. If the latent estimate degrades just as fast, the effect is plain forgetting and has nothing to do with self-conditioning.  
Kill criterion: Drop it if a trained transformer's drift at temperature 1 with pinned prompt grows without bound (more than 3x the posterior variance by 4000 tokens) in stationary training, or if entropy at temperature 0.7 does not fall. Either would mean trained models are …  
Cost: About 6 to 10 hours of Mac training across variants, under 8 GB memory, no downloads for the toy. The later TTS check needs one public checkpoint and a speaker-embedding model.

**A dose-response curve for hallucination on silence: how much label misalignment it takes, and why suppression does not transfer across noise types** (speech recognition (tiny encoder-decoder trained from …; wildness 2)  
Claim: An encoder-decoder speech recogniser writes text on silence because a small share of its training clips paired non-speech audio with text. On noise it has seen, the rate should follow the simple Bayes ratio. On noise it has not seen, the text prior wins and the learned suppression does not carry over.  
Prediction: With misaligned share e (noise audio, boilerplate text) and silence-labelled-empty share s in training: on in-distribution noise the rate of non-empty output is within 10 points of e/(e+s). On noise with a different spectrum the rate is at least 2x that Bayes value. With s=0 and e at or above 0.3%, …  
Control that could embarrass it: A CTC model with no autoregressive decoder trained on the same data. If it hallucinates as much, the decoder prior is not the mechanism.  
Kill criterion: Drop it if the rate on unseen noise stays within 1.5x of e/(e+s) across the sweep. Then the model is simply calibrated and there is no generalisation failure to explain.  
Cost: 54 short runs of roughly 10 minutes each, about 9 hours on the Mac, under 4 GB, no downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [How to Reduce Whisper Hallucination, arXiv 2609.32560](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32560), 2026-09-26 (read); [Investigation of Whisper ASR Hallucinations Induced by Non-Speech Audio, arXiv 2501.11378](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.11378), 2025-01-20 (not read directly); [Whisper Hallucination Detection and Mitigation via Hidden Representation Steering and Sparse AutoEncoders, …](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07473), 2026-06-05 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: arXiv HTML search 'Whisper hallucination silence'; Hugging Face papers search 'Whisper hallucination cause training data subtitles' and 'language prior hallucination encoder-decoder cross-attention weak evidence'. No from-scratch …

**Does a small transformer count a repeated claim as new evidence? Sycophancy under pressure with a known Bayes answer** (language models (toy transformer trained from scratch); wildness 3)  
Claim: When a user repeats the same assertion, no new information arrives, yet large models give way. I think the default behaviour of attention is to weight a claim by how many times it appears, so counting repeats as evidence is what a transformer does unless training teaches it to de-duplicate. A toy with an exact Bayes posterior can measure the excess.  
Prediction: Train with 1 to 3 exact repeats that carry no extra information, test with up to 16. At 8 repeats the model's log-odds for the claimed answer exceed the Bayes value by at least 1 nat, and adoption of a claim that the model's own evidence rejects at 80% posterior is at least 1.5x the Bayes rate. …  
Control that could embarrass it: Pad with k copies of an irrelevant token instead of the claim. If the answer shifts as much, the effect is length extrapolation and not evidence counting.  
Kill criterion: Drop it if the excess at 8 repeats is under 0.3 nats in models of two or more layers across three seeds.  
Cost: About 4 hours on the Mac for the grid, under 4 GB, no downloads.

**Long shot: deference is decided by learning order, so two models with the same loss can differ in sycophancy** (language models (toy transformer trained from scratch); wildness 4)  
Claim: Copying a stated answer is a simpler circuit than computing one, so it forms first. If the advisor is present from the start of training, the compute circuit is learned late or weakly and the model leans on the claim. If the model first learns to compute and only then meets the advisor, it reaches the same training loss but defers far less when the two disagree.  
Prediction: Two curricula that end within 1% of each other in training loss: with the advisor present from step 0, deference on held-out conflict cases (advisor wrong, own evidence strong) is at least 2x the rate of the curriculum that adds the advisor after the compute circuit has generalised. Grokking of the …  
Control that could embarrass it: Train both curricula far past convergence with weight decay. If the gap closes, the effect is a transient and not a lasting property, which weakens the story a lot.  
Kill criterion: Drop it if the deference gap on conflict cases is under 1.3x across five seeds at matched loss.  
Cost: About 5 hours on the Mac, under 4 GB, no downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [The Transient Nature of Emergent In-Context Learning in Transformers, arXiv 2311.08360](https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08360), 2023-11-14 (not read directly); [Is Sycophancy a Form of In-Context Updating?, arXiv 2610.28325](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28325), 2026-10-20 (read)  
Author's note on the search: Same searches as the repetition idea, plus Hugging Face papers search 'knowledge conflict in-context versus in-weights toy transformer source reliability', which returned only large-model benchmarks. No search specific to curriculum order …

**The depth tax of redundant input: a constant two or three layers, which matters for small speech models and not for large ones** (speech and multimodal language models (toy transformer …; wildness 3)  
Claim: Speech-input models reason worse than text-input ones, and one proposed reason is that long, redundant frame sequences dilute meaning. I predict the cost in depth is a small constant, about two or three layers to find boundaries, index symbols and pool, and that it does not grow with the stretch factor. If so, depth cannot explain the gap in 32-layer models but can explain a large gap in 6 to 12 layer ones.  
Prediction: For a k-hop composition task, the largest k a model with L layers solves on input stretched r times equals what a model with L-d layers solves on unstretched input, with d=1 for fixed-length stretch and d between 2 and 3 for variable-length noisy stretch, flat within one layer for r from 4 to 16. …  
Control that could embarrass it: Give the stretched model a single learned pooling layer in front (a strided convolution). If that alone removes the whole penalty for variable-length input, the tax is one layer and the effect is too small to matter anywhere.  
Kill criterion: Drop the constant-tax claim if d grows by more than one layer per doubling of r. That outcome would support the dilution story instead and is still worth reporting.  
Cost: About 70 runs of 5 to 15 minutes, roughly 10 hours on the Mac, under 6 GB, no downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [Which Speech Representation Better Matches Text-Native Reasoning?, arXiv 2606.12199](https://arxiv.org/html/2606.12199v1), 2026-06-11 (read); [Closing the Modality Reasoning Gap for Speech Large Language Models, arXiv 2601.05543](https://huggingface.co/papers/2601.05543), 2026-01-09 (read); [Reading, Not Thinking, arXiv 2603.09095](https://huggingface.co/papers/2603.09095), 2026-03-10 (read)

**Which modes a one-step student drops is set by its starting point and the highest noise level it trains on, not by the reverse-KL objective** (image and video generation (low-dimensional toy with an …; wildness 2)  
Claim: Diversity loss in distribution-matching distillation is usually blamed on reverse KL being mode-seeking. With an exact teacher the best possible student still covers every mode, so the loss is a training-dynamics effect: a mode with no student samples near it sends almost no gradient unless training includes noise levels high enough for modes to overlap. Which modes survive should be predictable from the student's …  
Prediction: 16-mode Gaussian mixture with unequal weights, exact analytic teacher score. If the highest training noise level is below the level where neighbouring modes merge (separation about 2 noise standard deviations), the student covers at most 50% of modes in most seeds. If it is above, the student …  
Control that could embarrass it: Initialise the student already covering all modes and train with low noise only. If modes are still lost, the objective itself is pushing mass away and the dynamics story is wrong.  
Kill criterion: Drop it if coverage does not change by at least 30 points when the upper noise limit crosses the merge level, or if initial mass does not beat mode weight as a predictor.  
Cost: About 3 hours on CPU for the full sweep, under 2 GB, no downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [A First-Step Bias Restores Lost Diversity in Diffusion Model Distillation, arXiv 2610.37110](https://huggingface.co/papers/2610.37110), 2026-10-27 (read); [Diversity-Preserved Distribution Matching Distillation, arXiv 2602.03139](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03139), 2026-02-03 (not read directly); [CrossDistill, arXiv 2609.14725](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14725), 2026-09-20 (not read directly)

**Is the English pivot just data imbalance? Decoding the middle layers of models trained on cloned languages** (language models (small transformers trained from scratch); wildness 2)  
Claim: Large multilingual models appear to pass through English-like representations in their middle layers. I predict this needs nothing about English: train a small model on two copies of one synthetic language that differ only in symbols, with unequal data, and the middle layers of the rare copy will decode to the common copy's tokens. At equal data there should be no pivot.  
Prediction: At a 90/10 split, a logit-lens readout of the middle third of layers on rare-copy inputs puts the common copy's corresponding token above the rare copy's own token on more than 70% of positions. At 50/50 this is under 55%. The pivot share rises monotonically with imbalance and appears at the same …  
Control that could embarrass it: Use untied input and output embeddings and a tuned lens. If the pivot share vanishes, it was a readout artefact of the shared unembedding and not a property of the computation.  
Kill criterion: Drop it if the middle-layer pivot share at 90/10 is under 60% or does not differ from 50/50 by at least 15 points.  
Cost: 12 runs of about 1 to 2 hours each on the Mac, 12 to 24 hours total, under 8 GB, no downloads.  
Nearest work listed: [The Role of Language Imbalance in Cross-lingual Generalisation: Insights from Cloned Language Experiments, …](https://huggingface.co/papers/2404.07982), 2024-04-11 (read); [Do Llamas Work in English?, arXiv 2402.10588](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.10588), 2024-02-16 (not read directly)

**Long shot: a from-scratch organism for emergent misalignment, where the spread is set by how many domains share the character variable** (language models (toy transformer trained from scratch); wildness 4)  
Claim: Fine-tuning a large model on one narrow bad behaviour can change its behaviour everywhere. The current account is that the fine-tune strengthens a persona shared across domains. I predict this can be built from nothing: pretrain a small model on many synthetic domains that share a hidden good-or-bad answerer variable, fine-tune on bad answers in one domain, and the spread to other domains will grow with the number …  
Prediction: With 8 or more domains sharing the answerer variable in pretraining, a fine-tune that reaches 95% bad answers in one domain flips more than 50% of answers in held-out domains. With 2 shared domains the spread is under 15%. Stopping the fine-tune at 80% narrow accuracy cuts the spread by at least …  
Control that could embarrass it: Pretrain with the answerer variable independent per domain (no sharing). If the spread is just as large, the cause is generic damage from fine-tuning and not a shared persona.  
Kill criterion: Drop it if spread at K=16 is under 25%, or if it does not rise with K.  
Cost: About 6 hours on the Mac, under 4 GB, no downloads.

### Lens: year-2031

**When is a model's fingerprint fixed? Fork a pretraining run at many points and find the window where tail behaviour is decided** (language models, training; wildness 3)  
Claim: The per-item quirks that differ between pretraining seeds are decided in a short early window, and by data order more than by weight initialisation. Two runs that share the first 5 percent of training and then see different data orders end up almost as alike, item by item, as two copies of the same finished run. If so, the seed lottery can be played cheaply: fork early, pick the fork, train once.  
Prediction: Take per-item losses on 3,000 off-distribution probe texts, subtract the mean over all runs, and correlate between runs. A fork at 5 percent of training reaches at least 0.8 of the ceiling set by two forks at 90 percent. Two runs with the same init and different order from step 0 reach at most 0.4 …  
Control that could embarrass it: Similarity could simply grow in proportion to the fraction of training shared, with no early knee. Plot correlation against shared fraction: a straight line means there is no special window, only shared history. Also check that residual correlation is not …  
Kill criterion: Fork at 5 percent reaches less than 0.5 of the ceiling, or the curve against shared fraction is linear within noise, or the early-data swap effect is inside the fork-to-fork spread.  
Cost: 25 to 30 runs of about 1 hour on the Mac, or 2 Kaggle sessions. Under 5 GB of data. No downloads beyond a public text corpus; PolyPythias checkpoints later, about 20 GB for the small sizes.  
Nearest work listed: [Identically Trained Language Models Break on Different Jailbreaks, arXiv 2610.34074](https://huggingface.co/papers/2610.34074), 2026-10-25 (read); [PolyPythias: Stability and Outliers across Fifty Language Model Pre-Training Runs, arXiv 2503.09543](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09543), 2025-03-12 (read); Frankle et al., Linear Mode Connectivity and the Lottery Ticket Hypothesis (2020); Achille et al., Critical … (not read directly)

**How far can a continuous thought travel? Chain-length horizon of continuous, quantized and token thoughts under matched noise** (language models, latent reasoning, looped models; wildness 4)  
Claim: A reasoning state passed forward as a continuous vector loses accuracy with chain length like a random walk, so its usable length grows only with the square of its noise margin. A state snapped to one of a finite set of codes at each step has a usable length that grows much faster than that, because each step removes the error. Text tokens are good for long reasoning for this reason and not because they are …  
Prediction: On chained-composition tasks trained at lengths up to 8 and tested to 128: the continuous model drops below 50 percent by length 32 while the code-book model stays above 90 percent at 128. With Gaussian noise injected at one quarter of the mean half-distance between codes, continuous half-accuracy …  
Control that could embarrass it: The continuous model may learn attractors and correct itself, matching the code book with no bottleneck. That would kill the method but still be a finding, and the Jacobian measurement would show it. The search task is the second control: if continuous wins …  
Kill criterion: Continuous half-accuracy length is within a factor of 2 of the code-book length under matched noise on the composition tasks, or noise scaling does not follow the square law within a factor of 2.  
Cost: About 40 small training runs of 20 to 40 minutes on the Mac, 2 days in all. No downloads for the first stage; Ouro 1.4B (about 3 GB) for the follow-up.  
Nearest work listed: Hao et al., Training Large Language Models to Reason in a Continuous Latent Space (Coconut), 2024 (not read directly); [What Makes Recurrence Effective in Looped Language Models?, arXiv 2609.36636](https://huggingface.co/papers/2609.36636), 2026-09-29 (read); [Principled Thoughts for Latent Recursive LLM Systems, arXiv 2609.36159](https://huggingface.co/papers/2609.36159), 2026-09-28 (read); [The Case Against Continuous Chain-of-Thought (Neuralese), LessWrong](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ynC26Z2CJXsqj6ZnZ/the-case-against-continuous-chain-of-thought-neuralese) (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: One WebSearch on continuous versus discrete latent reasoning, error accumulation, quantized thoughts (found mixed claims and no chain-length law; nothing on quantized thoughts). I did not search specifically for vector-quantized latent …

**Is the 26-byte reasoning update a prompt? Finding the sentence that does what 13 or 1,023 parameters do** (language models, post-training, evaluation; wildness 2)  
Claim: Most of the benchmark gain from very small weight updates is elicitation: longer answers, the right output format, fewer extraction failures. A fixed instruction or a decoding change recovers most of it, and the items it fixes are mostly items where the base model computed correctly and answered badly. The size of the gain from a tiny update is then a measure of how badly a checkpoint was elicited, and it should …  
Prediction: On a 0.5B to 1.5B instruct model and GSM8K: the best of 200 fixed instruction suffixes plus a raised token limit recovers at least 70 percent of the accuracy gain of a tiny RL update. At least 60 percent of the items the update fixes are format, truncation or answer-extraction failures in the base …  
Control that could embarrass it: The update may fix real arithmetic or planning errors that no prompt fixes. The item-level audit decides this directly. A second control: a single learned steering vector at one layer; if that also fails to match the update, the update is doing more than …  
Kill criterion: Best prompt plus decoding recovers under 30 percent of the gain, and fewer than 30 percent of fixed items are answer-form failures.  
Cost: About 20 GPU hours on free T4 for RL runs and evaluation, 1 day of labelling and analysis. Model downloads 1 to 3 GB each.  
Nearest work listed: [Morris et al., Learning to Reason in 13 Parameters, arXiv 2602.04118](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04118), 2026-02-04 (not read directly); [Exploring Weight-Space Programming, arXiv 2611.09382](https://huggingface.co/papers/2611.09382), 2026-11-06 (read); [Understanding On-Policy Distillation via Sparse Crosscoders, arXiv 2609.35210](https://huggingface.co/papers/2609.35210), 2026-09-28 (read)

**Small-batch gains in repeated-data pretraining: is it the noise? A zero-cost synthetic noise from the two halves of a large batch** (language models, training, optimizers; wildness 2)  
Claim: When the same data is trained on for many epochs, small batches reach lower validation loss than large ones. If the cause is gradient noise, then a large batch with noise of the same shape added back should do as well, and plain round noise should not. The right-shaped noise is free: split the batch in two, and the difference of the two half-gradients has exactly the covariance of minibatch noise.  
Prediction: On a 20M to 50M model trained 30 epochs on 20M tokens, with learning rate and number of updates matched: half-difference noise at batch 512 recovers at least 70 percent of the validation-loss gap between plain batch 512 and the best small batch under SGD with momentum and under Muon. Isotropic …  
Control that could embarrass it: Small batches also take more updates per epoch. If the gain comes from update count and not from noise, synthetic noise at matched updates recovers nothing. The other embarrassment: tuned weight decay at large batch closes the gap by itself, in which case …  
Kill criterion: Half-difference noise recovers under 30 percent of the gap at matched updates under all three optimizers, or does no better than isotropic noise.  
Cost: About 40 runs of 30 to 60 minutes on the Mac, roughly 30 hours. No downloads beyond a small public corpus.  
Nearest work listed: [Scaling Repeated-Data Pretraining: Batch Size, Regularization, and GPU-Time Tradeoffs, arXiv 2610.15965](https://huggingface.co/papers/2610.15965), 2026-10-11 (read); Wu et al., On the Noisy Gradient Descent that Generalizes as SGD, ICML 2020 (not read directly); Geiping et al., Stochastic Training is Not Necessary for Generalization, 2021 (not read directly)  
Author's note on the search: Semantic Scholar and arXiv queries on large-batch injected noise for language models were rate-limited and returned nothing. Prior art is from memory plus the one abstract read. I have not checked whether the repeated-data paper's body …

**One curve for what a model knows and what it makes up: cross-seed agreement against training support, in a language model and a diffusion model** (language models and image generation; wildness 4)  
Claim: Train the same recipe twice with different seeds. On inputs well covered by the data the two models agree; on thinly covered inputs they disagree, and their errors are different errors. This should hold in the same form for a language model answering fact questions and a diffusion model drawing from the same noise, and the switch from agreement to disagreement should be sharp in the number of supporting training …  
Prediction: For planted facts seen at most 2 times in training, two seeds give the same wrong answer under 20 percent of the time. Two-seed disagreement detects wrong answers with AUROC at least 0.85 and beats sampling-consistency from a single model by at least 0.05 AUROC. In the diffusion model, same-noise …  
Control that could embarrass it: Sampling one model several times may detect wrong answers as well as a second seed does, which would make seeds unnecessary. And shared wrong answers (the most common value for a relation) may dominate, so disagreement misses most hallucinations. Both are …  
Kill criterion: Cross-seed disagreement beats single-model sampling consistency by less than 0.05 AUROC, or more than half of the wrong answers on rare facts are identical across seeds, or the diffusion model stays similar across seeds even on held-out conditions.  
Cost: Language part: 3 runs of about 2 hours on the Mac. Diffusion part: 3 runs of about 6 hours on T4. One day of analysis. Small public image set, under 5 GB.

**Do latent spaces that interpolate well make generators that train well? A cross-tokenizer check without training anything** (image generation; wildness 2)  
Claim: A generator's samples land between training codes, so a latent space is good for generation to the extent that points between two real codes still decode to natural images. Across public image tokenizers, a score for how natural decoded interpolations look should predict published generation quality better than reconstruction quality does. This would explain in one line why semantic latents have been beating …  
Prediction: Across at least 8 public tokenizers with published matched-generator results on ImageNet 256, the rank correlation between interpolation naturalness and generation FID is at least 0.7 in magnitude, while the rank correlation between reconstruction FID and generation FID is at most 0.3.  
Control that could embarrass it: The interpolation benchmark paper may already report this correlation, in which case the idea is done. Also, latent dimension alone may predict generation FID as well as interpolation does; partial out dimension.  
Kill criterion: Rank correlation under 0.4 in magnitude, or no better than latent dimension alone.  
Cost: About 6 hours of tokenizer inference on the Mac. Tokenizer weights total about 5 GB. ImageNet validation subset about 1 GB.  
Nearest work listed: [Image Tokenizers Should Be Trained Between Images (InterpTok), arXiv 2610.16759](https://huggingface.co/papers/2610.16759), 2026-10-12 (read)  
Author's note on the search: None beyond reading the InterpTok abstract. The benchmark it cites must be read before any work starts.
